gives an even, positive, normalised measure on the interval
[-1,1]
. The above integral representation then interprets the
{}_{0}F_{1}
function as a
weighed superposition of time-delays
.

There is another solution which is regular everywhere except the origin:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{1}{r^{\nu+\frac{d}{2}-2}}&\left\{{}_{0}F_{1}\left[1-\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r^{2}}{4}\right]-\frac{\pi\cot\nu\pi}{\Gamma(\nu)\Gamma(1+\nu)}\left(\frac{\omega r}{2}\right)^{2\nu}\ {}_{0}F_{1}\left[1+\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r^{2}}{4}\right]\right\}\mathbb{V}_{I}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}\\
&=-\left(\frac{\omega}{2}\right)^{\nu}\frac{\pi\ r^{2-\frac{d}{2}}}{\Gamma(\nu)}Y_{\nu}(\omega r)\mathbb{V}_{I}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}\ .\end{split}

(187)

Here,
Y_{\nu}
is the Neumann function: when
\nu\notin\mathbb{Z}
, this solution is obtained from the regular solution by the replacement
\nu\to-\nu
and adding an appropriate amount of the regular solution. The expression in terms of
{}_{0}F_{1}
should, however, be carefully interpreted whenever
\nu\equiv\ell+\frac{d}{2}-1
is an integer (i.e., whenever the number of space dimensions
d
is even). When
\nu
is a positive integer, the hypergeometric series for
{}_{0}F_{1}[1-\nu,z]
is divergent, and the
\cot\nu\pi
factor is also divergent. However, these two divergences cancel each other in the above expression, so the limit
\nu\to\text{Integer}
exists and converges to the Neumann function. These statements should be contrasted against the case when
d
is odd and
\nu
is a half-integer: in this case, the hypergeometric series for
{}_{0}F_{1}[1-\nu,z]
is convergent, and the factor
\cot\nu\pi
evaluates to zero.

Alternately, one can characterise the solutions according to their behaviours at
r=\infty
, i.e. either as outgoing or ingoing solutions:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{1}{r^{\nu+\frac{d}{2}-2}}&\left\{{}_{0}F_{1}\left[1-\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r^{2}}{4}\right]\pm(1\pm i\cot\nu\pi)\frac{2\pi i}{\Gamma(\nu)^{2}}\frac{1}{2\nu}\left(\frac{\omega r}{2}\right)^{2\nu}\ {}_{0}F_{1}\left[1+\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r^{2}}{4}\right]\right\}\mathbb{V}_{I}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}\\
&=\pm i\left(\frac{\omega}{2}\right)^{\nu}\frac{\pi\ r^{2-\frac{d}{2}}}{\Gamma(\nu)}H^{\pm}_{\nu}(\omega r)\mathbb{V}_{I}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}\end{split}

(188)

Here, the solution with
H^{+}
denotes the outgoing waves, while the
H^{-}
denotes the incoming waves.
Our comments regarding the case where
d
is even and
\nu
is a positive integer still apply. Using the identity

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{(+i)(1+i\cot\nu\pi)}{(-i)(1-i\cot\nu\pi)}=[e^{-i\pi}]^{2\nu}\ ,\end{split}

(189)

it can be seen that the two
solutions here are related by time-reversal, i.e., under
\omega\mapsto e^{i\pi}\omega
, the outgoing wave is mapped to the incoming wave, and under
\omega\mapsto e^{-i\pi}\omega
the incoming wave is mapped to the outgoing wave. For radiation reaction, we are mainly interested in outgoing waves whose radial part is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)&\equiv\frac{1}{r^{\nu+\frac{d}{2}-2}}\\
&\times\left\{{}_{0}F_{1}\left[1-\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r^{2}}{4}\right]+(1+i\cot\nu\pi)\frac{2\pi i}{\Gamma(\nu)^{2}}\frac{1}{2\nu}\left(\frac{\omega r}{2}\right)^{2\nu}\ {}_{0}F_{1}\left[1+\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r^{2}}{4}\right]\right\}\ .\end{split}

(190)

When
\nu
is a half-integer, the function
G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}
can be greatly simplified by the use of
reverse Bessel polynomials
. They are defined via

\begin{split}\theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}(z)&\equiv\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}e^{z}z^{\nu}K_{\nu}(z)=\frac{2^{\nu}\Gamma(\nu)}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{z}\left\{\ {}_{0}F_{1}\left[1-\nu,\frac{z^{2}}{4}\right]+\frac{\Gamma(-\nu)}{\Gamma(\nu)}\left(\frac{z}{2}\right)^{2\nu}\ {}_{0}F_{1}\left[1+\nu,\frac{z^{2}}{4}\right]\right\}\\
&=\sum_{n=0}^{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{z^{\nu-\frac{1}{2}-n}}{2^{n}n!}\frac{\left(\nu-\frac{1}{2}+n\right)!}{\left(\nu-\frac{1}{2}-n\right)!}\\
\end{split}

(191)

Here
K_{\nu}(z)
is the Macdonald function, and the second line shows that
\theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}(z)
is a polynomial of degree
\nu-\frac{1}{2}
with positive integer coefficients, the coefficient of
z^{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}
being normalised to unity. Explicit forms of the first few reverse Bessel polynomials are tabulated below:

Table 4:
\theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}(z)
for various values of
\nu

\nu

\theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}(z)

\frac{1}{2}

1

\frac{3}{2}

1+z

\frac{5}{2}

3+3z+z^{2}+r^{2}z

\frac{7}{2}

15+15z+6z^{2}+z^{3}

\frac{9}{2}

105+105z+45z^{2}+10z^{3}+z^{4}

\frac{11}{2}

945+945z+420z^{2}+105z^{3}+15z^{4}+z^{5}

\frac{13}{2}

10395+10395z+4725z^{2}+1260z^{3}+210z^{4}+21z^{5}+z^{6}

Another useful property of the reverse Bessel polynomials evident from the above table is the value of the constant term in these polynomials

\theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}(0)=(2\nu-2)!!=\frac{(d-2)!!}{\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-1}}\ .

(192)

The radial part of the outgoing waves can then be written in the form

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)=\frac{\theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}(-i\omega r)}{\theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}(0)}\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{r^{\nu+\frac{d}{2}-2}}=\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-1}\frac{\theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}(-i\omega r)}{(d-2)!!}\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{r^{\nu+\frac{d}{2}-2}}\end{split}

(193)

for
\nu\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}
.
The large
r
asymptotics of this outgoing spherical wave are given by taking the largest power in the reverse Bessel polynomial:

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)\to\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-1}\frac{(-i\omega)^{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}}{(d-2)!!}\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{r^{\frac{d-3}{2}}}\ \quad\text{as}\quad r\to\infty\ .\end{split}

(194)

This asymptotic formula also holds for
d
even and
\nu\in\mathbb{Z}
, provided the double factorial for even integers is defined recursively with the convention that

0!!\equiv\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}
. For general
\nu
, we can justify this via the asymptotic expansion of Hankel functions (see
https://dlmf.nist.gov/10.17
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}\pm i\left(\frac{\omega}{2}\right)^{\nu}\frac{\pi\ r^{2-\frac{d}{2}}}{\Gamma(\nu)}H^{\pm}_{\nu}(\omega r)\to\left(\frac{\omega\ e^{\mp i\frac{\pi}{2}}}{2}\right)^{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{\Gamma(\nu)}\frac{e^{\pm i\omega r}}{r^{\frac{d-3}{2}}}\ \quad\text{as}\quad r\to\infty\ .\end{split}

(195)

We will conclude this discussion with some useful identities: the raising and lowering relations for Hankel functions lead to

\begin{split}-\frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\left[\frac{G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)}{\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-1}r^{\ell+1}}\right]&=\frac{G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell+1)}{\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell}r^{\ell+2}}\ ,\quad\frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\left[\frac{G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)}{\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-1}\ r^{3-d-\ell}}\right]=\omega^{2}\frac{G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell-1)}{\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-2}\ r^{4-d-\ell}}\ .\end{split}

(196)

These identities can be used to give the following formulae for the derivative of
G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}
, i.e.,

\begin{split}\partial_{r}G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)&=-\frac{\left(\ell+d-3\right)}{r}G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)\ +\frac{\omega^{2}}{2\ell+d-4}G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell-1)\\
&=-\left(\ell+d-3\right)G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell+1)+\frac{\ell+1}{2\ell+d-2}\frac{\omega^{2}}{2\ell+d-4}G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell-1)\ .\end{split}

(197)

Another useful identity arises from the cartesian version of Eq.(
196
):

\begin{split}-\partial_{i}\left[\frac{G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)}{\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-1}r^{\ell+1}}\right]&=x^{i}\frac{G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell+1)}{\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell}r^{\ell+2}}\ ,\quad\partial_{i}\left[\frac{G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)}{\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-1}\ r^{3-d-\ell}}\right]=\omega^{2}x^{i}\frac{G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell-1)}{\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-2}\ r^{4-d-\ell}}\ .\end{split}

(198)

By repeated application of the first identity, we get the following relation, which expresses the radial part of
\ell^{th}
spherical wave as a derivative of the
\ell=0
wave:

\begin{split}(-1)^{\ell}\partial^{<i_{1}}\partial^{i_{2}}\ldots\partial^{i_{\ell}>}\left\{\frac{G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell=0)}{(d-2)r}\right\}=\frac{G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)}{\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-1}r^{\ell+1}}x^{<i_{1}}\ldots x^{i_{\ell}>}\ .\end{split}

(199)

Here we work with the convention that
\mathcal{N}_{d,-1}=(d-2)
which is the correct analytic extension of
\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell}
to negative
\ell
’s. In the above relation, the STF projection on the indices ensures that the derivatives acting on
x^{i}
’s always give zero at every step.

Green function for magnetic radiation

Now that we understand the homogeneous solutions, we can solve the full inhomogeneous Helmholtz equation in Eq.(
184
) via Green functions. As we did in magnetostatics, we will begin with an ansatz for the vector potential:

\begin{split}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{t}(\vec{r},t)&=\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{r}(\vec{r},t)=0\ ,\quad\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{I}(\vec{r},t)\equiv\sum_{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}e^{-i\omega t}\Phi_{{}_{B}}(r,\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})\mathbb{V}_{I}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ \end{split}

(200)

where
\Phi_{{}_{B}}
denotes the frequency domain magnetic Debye field. Substituting this ansatz into Eq.(
184
), we get a sourced Helmholtz equation for
\Phi_{{}_{B}}
:

\begin{split}-\frac{1}{r^{d-3}}\partial_{r}[r^{d-3}\partial_{r}\Phi_{{}_{B}}]-\omega^{2}\Phi_{{}_{B}}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}(\ell+1)(\ell+d-3)\Phi_{{}_{B}}=r^{2}J_{V}(r,\omega)\ ,\end{split}

(201)

where the source appearing in the RHS is

\begin{split}J_{V}(r,\omega)\equiv\int_{\hat{r}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathbb{V}_{I}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}\ast}(\hat{r})J^{I}(\vec{r},\omega)\ .\end{split}

(202)

We will posit a solution of the form

\begin{split}\Phi_{{}_{B}}(r,\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})&\equiv\int_{\vec{r}_{0}}\mathbb{G}_{B}(r,r_{0};\omega,\ell)\mathbb{V}_{J}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}\ast}(\hat{r}_{0})J^{J}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)\\
&=\int_{0}^{\infty}dr_{0}\ r_{0}^{d-1}\mathbb{G}_{B}(r,r_{0};\omega,\ell)J_{V}(r_{0},\omega)\ ,\end{split}

(203)

which generalises the static expression in Eq.(
166
). The Green function
\mathbb{G}_{B}
obeys

\begin{split}-\partial_{r}[r^{d-3}\partial_{r}\mathbb{G}_{B}]-\omega^{2}r^{d-3}\mathbb{G}_{B}+(\ell+1)(\ell+d-3)r^{d-5}\mathbb{G}_{B}=\delta(r-r_{0})\ ,\end{split}

(204)

and is built by stitching together the homogeneous solutions of the vector Helmholtz equation. Our normalisations here are such that
\mathbb{G}_{B}
generalises the static Green function defined in Eq.(
161
). The conditions on the Green function
\mathbb{G}_{B}
are that it should be continuous, its derivative should have an appropriate discontinuity, and it should match onto an outgoing wave far away from currents. These determine

\begin{split}\mathbb{G}_{B}(r,r_{0};\omega,\ell)&=\frac{1}{2\nu}\frac{r_{<}^{\nu-\frac{d}{2}+2}}{r_{>}^{\nu+\frac{d}{2}-2}}{}_{0}F_{1}\left[1+\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r_{<}^{2}}{4}\right]\\
&\times\left\{{}_{0}F_{1}\left[1-\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r_{>}^{2}}{4}\right]+(1+i\cot\nu\pi)\frac{2\pi i}{\Gamma(\nu)^{2}}\frac{1}{2\nu}\left(\frac{\omega r_{>}}{2}\right)^{2\nu}\ {}_{0}F_{1}\left[1+\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r_{>}^{2}}{4}\right]\right\}\\
&=\frac{1}{2\nu}r_{<}^{\nu-\frac{d}{2}+2}{}_{0}F_{1}\left[1+\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r_{<}^{2}}{4}\right]G_{B}^{\text{Out}}(r_{>},\omega,\ell)\ ,\end{split}

(205)

where we have defined:

\begin{split}r_{>}\equiv\text{Max}(r,r_{0})\ ,\quad r_{<}\equiv\text{Min}(r,r_{0})\ .\end{split}

(206)

The reader can check that the above expression reduces to Eq.(
161
) in the static (i.e.,
\omega\to 0
) limit.
Once we have the vector potential, we can compute the
the corresponding electric/magnetic field components. The VSH expansion of the field strengths takes the form

\begin{split}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{rt}(\vec{r},t)&=0\ ,\\
\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{It}(\vec{r},t)&=\sum_{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}e^{-i\omega t}E_{v}(r,\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})\ \mathbb{V}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}_{I}(\vec{r})\ ,\\
\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{rI}(\vec{r},t)&=\sum_{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}e^{-i\omega t}H_{v}(r,\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})\ \mathbb{V}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}_{I}(\vec{r})\ ,\\
\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{IJ}(\vec{r},t)&=\sum_{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}e^{-i\omega t}H_{vv}(r,\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})\ \left[\mathscr{D}_{I}\mathbb{V}_{J\alpha\ell\vec{m}}(\vec{r})-\mathscr{D}_{J}\mathbb{V}_{I\alpha\ell\vec{m}}(\vec{r})\right]\ ,\end{split}

(207)

where the components are given by

\begin{split}H_{v}&=\partial_{r}\Phi_{{}_{B}}=\int_{\vec{r}_{0}}\partial_{r}\mathbb{G}_{B}(r,r_{0};\omega,\ell)\mathbb{V}_{J}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}\ast}(\hat{r}_{0})J^{J}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)\ ,\\
H_{vv}&=\Phi_{{}_{B}}=\int_{\vec{r}_{0}}\mathbb{G}_{B}(r,r_{0};\omega,\ell)\mathbb{V}_{J}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}\ast}(\hat{r}_{0})J^{J}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)\ ,\\
E_{v}&=i\omega\ \Phi_{{}_{B}}=i\omega\int_{\vec{r}_{0}}\mathbb{G}_{B}(r,r_{0};\omega,\ell)\mathbb{V}_{J}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}\ast}(\hat{r}_{0})J^{J}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)\ .\end{split}

(208)

Note that apart from the magnetic fields, we have an induced electric field when toroidal currents are time-dependent. These time-varying electric and magnetic fields sustain each other as they escape the source and propagate far away as radiation.

Fields outside sources

To get a handle on the structure of multipole radiation, we will now focus on fields outside the sources. We will generalise our definition of static magnetic multipole moment
in Eq.(
167
) as follows:

\begin{split}\mathcal{J}^{B}(\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})&\equiv\frac{1}{2\nu}\int_{\vec{r}_{0}}r_{0}^{\nu-\frac{d}{2}+2}{}_{0}F_{1}\left[1+\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r_{0}^{2}}{4}\right]\mathbb{V}_{I}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}\ast}(\hat{r}_{0})J^{I}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)\ .\end{split}

(209)

It is clear from our general solution that this is the magnetic moment that determines the fields outside currents.
The magnetic Debye field outside the currents is given by

\begin{split}\Phi_{B}^{\text{Out}}&=G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)\ \mathcal{J}^{B}(\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})=\frac{\theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}(-i\omega r)}{\theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}(0)}\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{r^{\ell+d-3}}\mathcal{J}^{B}(\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})\ .\end{split}

(210)

The values of field strength components are given explicitly in terms of
\mathcal{J}^{B}
by

\begin{split}H_{vv}^{\text{Out}}&=G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)\ \mathcal{J}^{B}(\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})=\frac{\theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}(-i\omega r)}{\theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}(0)}\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{r^{\ell+d-3}}\mathcal{J}^{B}(\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m}),\\
H_{v}^{\text{Out}}&=-\frac{\left(\ell+d-3\right)}{r}G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)\ \mathcal{J}^{B}(\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})+\frac{\omega^{2}}{2\ell+d-4}G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell-1)\ \mathcal{J}^{B}(\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})\\
&=-\left(\ell+d-3\right)\frac{\theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}(-i\omega r)}{\theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}(0)}\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{r^{\ell+d-2}}\ \mathcal{J}^{B}(\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})\\
&\qquad+\frac{\omega^{2}}{2\ell+d-4}\frac{\theta_{\nu-\frac{3}{2}}(-i\omega r)}{\theta_{\nu-\frac{3}{2}}(0)}\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{r^{\ell+d-4}}\ \mathcal{J}^{B}(\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m}),\\
E_{v}^{\text{Out}}&=i\omega G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)\ \mathcal{J}^{B}(\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})=i\omega\frac{\theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}(-i\omega r)}{\theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}(0)}\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{r^{\ell+d-3}}\mathcal{J}^{B}(\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})\ .\end{split}

(211)

Here we have used Eq.(
197
) for evaluating
\partial_{r}G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}
. The large
r
asymptotics can be worked out using Eq.(
194
):

\begin{split}H_{vv}^{\text{Rad}}&=\Phi_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Rad}}=\frac{(-i\omega)^{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}}{(2\nu-2)!!}\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{r^{\frac{d-3}{2}}}\ \mathcal{J}^{B}(\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m}),\\
H_{v}^{\text{Rad}}&=\partial_{r}\Phi_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Rad}}=-\frac{(-i\omega)^{\nu+\frac{1}{2}}}{(2\nu-2)!!}\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{r^{\frac{d-3}{2}}}\mathcal{J}^{B}(\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m}),\\
E_{v}^{\text{Rad}}&=i\omega\Phi_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Rad}}=-\frac{(-i\omega)^{\nu+\frac{1}{2}}}{(2\nu-2)!!}\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{r^{\frac{d-3}{2}}}\mathcal{J}^{B}(\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m}).\end{split}

(212)

We remind the reader that this holds even when
d
is even, provided the double factorial for even integers is defined recursively with the convention that

0!!\equiv\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}
. We will see later how these asymptotics get modified in dS (
441
).

We next turn to the description in terms of cartesian STF tensors. The STF version of magnetic moment is still defined by Eq.(
171
), but is now generalised to arbitrary frequency,i.e.,

\begin{split}\frac{1}{\ell!}\ [{}^{B}\mathcal{Q}(\omega)]_{k<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}\ x^{i_{1}}\ldots x^{i_{\ell}}\equiv\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-1}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\left(\frac{\partial x_{k}}{\partial\vartheta_{I}}\right)\sum_{\vec{m}\alpha}\mathcal{J}^{B}(\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})r^{\ell-1}\mathbb{V}^{I}_{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ .\end{split}

(213)

Repeating the same logic as in magnetostatics, we can give a direct expression in terms of the vector STF projector:

\begin{split}{}^{B}\mathcal{Q}_{k<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}(\omega)\equiv(\Pi^{V}_{kj})^{<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}_{<j_{1}j_{2}\ldots j_{\ell}>}\int_{\vec{r}_{0}}x_{0}^{j_{1}}\ldots x_{0}^{j_{\ell}}{}_{0}F_{1}\left[1+\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r_{0}^{2}}{4}\right]J^{j}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)\ .\end{split}

(214)

The tensor structure appearing here is exactly identical to that seen in statics (e.g., see (
182
)). The main difference in the time-dependent situation is the smearing due to time delays: using Eq.(
186
), we can write

\begin{split}{}^{B}\mathcal{Q}_{k<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}=(\Pi^{V}_{kj})^{<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}_{<j_{1}j_{2}\ldots j_{\ell}>}\int_{-1}^{1}dz\ \delta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}(z)\int_{\vec{r}_{0}}x_{0}^{j_{1}}\ldots x_{0}^{j_{\ell}}e^{i\omega zr_{0}}J^{j}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)\ .\end{split}

(215)

where

\delta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}(z)\equiv\frac{\Gamma\left(1+\nu\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}+\nu\right)}(1-z^{2})^{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}=\frac{(2\nu)!!}{2^{\nu+\frac{1}{2}}\left(\nu-\frac{1}{2}\right)!}(1-z^{2})^{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}\

(216)

with
\nu\equiv\ell+\frac{d}{2}-1
25
25

25

For even
d
, we work with the convention that
0!!=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}
.
. The interpretation in terms of time delays is more transparent in the time domain where the above equation becomes

\begin{split}{}^{B}\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{k<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}(t)=(\Pi^{V}_{kj})^{<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}_{<j_{1}j_{2}\ldots j_{\ell}>}\int_{\vec{r}_{0}}x_{0}^{j_{1}}\ldots x_{0}^{j_{\ell}}\int_{-1}^{1}dz\ \delta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}(z)\bar{J}^{j}(\vec{r}_{0},t-zr_{0})\ .\end{split}

(217)

The time delay above is further compounded in fields by the standard retardation effect, i.e., the field depends on
{}^{B}\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{k<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}(t-r)
and hence on
\bar{J}^{j}(\vec{r}_{0},t-r-zr_{0})
.
Thus, for a source of size
R
spread around the origin, we get a time delay seen by a detector ranging from
r-R
(in the near end of the source) to
r+R
(in the far end of the source). In an expanding universe, there is a further effect due to redshifts, which have to be correctly taken into account while defining multipole moments of cosmologically big sources.

With these comments, let us return to the task at hand: in terms of the magnetic multipole tensor, the vector potential/EM fields outside the sources can be written as
26
26

26

Here we use the notation
\text{Anti}_{jk}[T_{jk}]\equiv T_{jk}-T_{kj}
for the anti-symmetrisation operator.

\begin{split}\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\text{Out}}(\vec{r},\omega)&=\sum_{\ell}\frac{G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)}{\ell!\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-1}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}{}^{B}\mathcal{Q}_{k<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}\frac{x^{i_{1}}\ldots x^{i_{\ell}}}{r^{\ell+1}}\ ,\\
\mathcal{C}_{kt}^{\text{Out}}(\vec{r},\omega)&=i\omega\sum_{\ell}\frac{G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)}{\ell!\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-1}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}{}^{B}\mathcal{Q}_{k<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}\frac{x^{i_{1}}\ldots x^{i_{\ell}}}{r^{\ell+1}}\ ,\\
\mathcal{C}_{jk}^{\text{Out}}(\vec{r},\omega)&=\text{Anti}_{jk}\sum_{\ell}\frac{G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)}{\ell!\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-1}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}{}^{B}\mathcal{Q}_{j<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}[(2\ell+d-2)x^{k}x^{i_{\ell}}-r^{2}\ell\delta^{ki_{\ell}}]\frac{x^{i_{1}}\ldots x^{i_{\ell-1}}}{r^{\ell+3}}\\
&-\omega^{2}\text{Anti}_{jk}\sum_{\ell}\frac{G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell-1)}{\ell!\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-2}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}\ {}^{B}\mathcal{Q}_{j<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}\frac{x^{k}x^{i_{1}}\ldots x^{i_{\ell}}}{r^{\ell+2}}\ .\end{split}

(218)

The field strengths here can also be derived by direct cartesian differentiation (using Eq.(
198
) when needed).
The multipole vector potential given here can be rewritten using Eq.(
199
) as

\begin{split}\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\text{Out}}(\vec{r},\omega)&=\sum_{\ell}\frac{(-1)^{\ell}}{\ell!}\partial^{i_{1}}\partial^{i_{2}}\ldots\partial^{i_{\ell}}\left\{{}^{B}\mathcal{Q}_{k<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}\frac{G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell=0)}{(d-2)|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|r}\right\}\ .\end{split}

(219)

For odd
d
, we can write

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{G_{{}_{B}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell=0)}{(d-2)|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|r}=\frac{\theta_{\frac{d-3}{2}}(-i\omega r)}{(d-2)!!}\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|r^{d-2}}\ .\end{split}

(220)

The
e^{i\omega r}
factor gives the standard retardation time-delay. Moving to time-domain, we then have a simple statement in
d=3
: the vector potential of a magnetic multipole is obtained by multiplying the retarded STF magnetic tensor with the Coulomb potential, followed by repeated differentiation. For odd
d>3
, we should apply an additional differential operator that depends on
r
and with maximum
\frac{d-3}{2}
time-derivatives

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\theta_{\frac{d-3}{2}}(r\partial_{t})}{(d-4)!!}\ ,\end{split}

(221)

before the repeated differentiation
[
68
]
. Next, the large
r
asymptotics of both the vector potential as well as the field strengths can be obtained via Eq.(
194
). We get

\begin{split}\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\text{Rad}}(\vec{r},\omega)&=\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{(d-2)!!|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|r^{\frac{d-1}{2}}}\sum_{\ell}\frac{(-i\omega)^{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}}{\ell!}{}^{B}\mathcal{Q}_{k<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}n^{i_{1}}\ldots n^{i_{\ell}}\ ,\\
\mathcal{C}_{kt}^{\text{Rad}}(\vec{r},\omega)&=-\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{(d-2)!!|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|r^{\frac{d-1}{2}}}\sum_{\ell}\frac{(-i\omega)^{\nu+\frac{1}{2}}}{\ell!}{}^{B}\mathcal{Q}_{k<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}n^{i_{1}}\ldots n^{i_{\ell}}\ ,\\
\mathcal{C}_{jk}^{\text{Rad}}(\vec{r},\omega)&=\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{(d-2)!!|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|r^{\frac{d-1}{2}}}\times\text{Anti}_{jk}\Bigl\{n^{k}\sum_{\ell}\frac{(-i\omega)^{\nu+\frac{1}{2}}}{\ell!}\ {}^{B}\mathcal{Q}_{j<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}n^{i_{1}}\ldots n^{i_{\ell}}\ \Bigr\}\ ,\end{split}

(222)

where we have used the notation
n^{i}\equiv\frac{x^{i}}{r}
. The infinite sum appearing in the field strengths is the EM waveform or the
light vector
.

B.3
Multipole expansion in statics II : poloidal currents and charges

Magnetic fields due to poloidal currents

We can go further and generalize to any time-independent, divergence-free current distribution. Such a current distribution, which
not
toroidal (i.e., not purely tangential to the sphere directions), is said to be a
poloidal
current distribution. A poloidal current can be expanded as

\begin{split}\bar{J}^{r}&=\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\bar{J}_{P}(r,\ell,\vec{m})\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,\quad\bar{J}^{I}=\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\bar{J}_{Q}(r,\ell,\vec{m})\gamma^{IJ}\mathscr{D}_{J}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,\end{split}

(223)

where
\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})
are orthonormal scalar spherical harmonics (SSHs) on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. They satisfy

\begin{split}\left[\mathscr{D}^{2}+\ell(\ell+d-2)\right]\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}=0\ ,\quad\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell^{\prime}\vec{m}^{\prime}}^{\ast}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}=\delta_{\ell^{\prime}\ell}\delta_{\vec{m}^{\prime}\vec{m}}\ .\end{split}

(224)

We want to determine the vector potential and magnetic field due to such a poloidal current distribution. With some hindsight, we will abandon the Coulomb gauge and use instead a gauge where the vector potential is purely radial. The vector potential and magnetic field are then of the form

\begin{split}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{r}&\equiv-\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\overline{H}_{s}(r,\ell,\vec{m})\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,\quad\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{I}=0\ ,\\
\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{rI}&=\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\overline{H}_{s}(r,\ell,\vec{m})\mathscr{D}_{I}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,\quad\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{IJ}=0\ .\end{split}

(225)

Using the sourced Maxwell equations
27
27

27

They are of the form

\begin{split}-\gamma^{IJ}\mathscr{D}_{I}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{Jr}=r^{2}\bar{J}^{r}\ ,&\quad\frac{1}{r^{d-1}}\partial_{r}[r^{d-3}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{Ir}]+\frac{1}{r^{4}}\gamma^{JK}\mathscr{D}_{K}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{IJ}=\gamma_{IJ}\bar{J}^{J}\ .\end{split}

(226)

,
we obtain the following relation between the currents and the magnetic field:

\begin{split}\bar{J}^{r}&=-\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)}{r^{2}}\overline{H}_{s}(r,\ell,\vec{m})\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,\\
\bar{J}^{I}&=-\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\frac{1}{r^{d-1}}\partial_{r}[r^{d-3}\overline{H}_{s}(r,\ell,\vec{m})]\gamma^{IJ}\mathscr{D}_{J}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,\end{split}

(227)

These two equations are not independent: they are related by current conservation, viz.,

\begin{split}\frac{1}{r^{d-1}}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}(r^{d-1}\bar{J}^{r})+\mathscr{D}_{I}\bar{J}^{I}=0\ .\end{split}

(228)

Thus, it is enough to invert the first equation. Using the orthonormality of scalar spherical harmonics, we can write

\begin{split}\overline{H}_{s}(r,\ell,\vec{m})&=-\frac{r^{2}}{\ell(\ell+d-2)}\int_{\hat{r}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}^{\ast}(\hat{r})\bar{J}^{r}(\vec{r})\\
&=-\frac{1}{\ell(\ell+d-2)}\frac{1}{r^{d-3}}\int_{\vec{r}_{0}}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}^{\ast}(\hat{r}_{0})\delta(r-r_{0})\bar{J}^{r}(\vec{r}_{0})\ .\end{split}

(229)

A few comments are in order: the result we have derived is valid if there is no
\ell=0
component (i.e., there is no spherically symmetric component) in
\bar{J}^{r}
. A bit of thought shows that this has to be true: a spherically symmetric radial current is inconsistent with charge conservation in the static limit. The next comment is about the locality in the radial direction: we see that if the poloidal current is confined within a radius
R
, its magnetic field also never extends beyond
R
. In particular, the far-field multipole expansion we derived before does not get corrected by poloidal currents.

When we move to time-varying currents, we will see that
\overline{H}_{s}
part of the magnetic field can in fact escape the currents and travel out as EM radiation. This suggests that, in the full dynamical situation,
\overline{H}_{s}
satisfies a wave equation with a source. Such an equation should reduce to a Poisson-like equation in the static limit. To see how this works, we combine the two equations of Eq.(
227
) into a Poisson-like equation:

\begin{split}r^{d-3}\partial_{r}\left(r^{3-d}\partial_{r}[r^{d-3}\overline{H}_{s}]\right)&-\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)}{r^{2}}(r^{d-3}\overline{H}_{s})\\
&=\int_{\hat{r}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}r^{d-3}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}^{\ast}(\hat{r})\Bigl\{\bar{J}^{r}(\vec{r})+\frac{1}{\ell(\ell+d-2)}\partial_{r}\left[r^{2}\mathscr{D}_{I}\bar{J}^{I}(\vec{r})\right]\Bigr\}\ .\end{split}

(230)

As we did for toroidal currents, we can solve this equation by thinking of the source as made of spherical shells and then integrating. we end up with

\begin{split}r^{d-3}\overline{H}_{s}&=-\int_{\vec{r}_{0}}\mathbb{G}_{E}(r,r_{0},\ell)\ r_{0}^{1-d}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}^{\ast}(\hat{r}_{0})\Bigl\{\bar{J}^{r}(\vec{r}_{0})+\frac{1}{\ell(\ell+d-2)}\partial_{r_{0}}\left[r_{0}^{2}\mathscr{D}_{I}\bar{J}^{I}(\vec{r}_{0})\right]\Bigr\}\\
\text{with}&\quad\mathbb{G}_{E}(r,r_{0},\ell)\equiv\frac{1}{2\ell+d-2}\left\{\frac{r^{\ell+d-2}}{r_{0}^{\ell}}\Theta(r<r_{0})+\frac{r_{0}^{\ell+d-2}}{r^{\ell}}\Theta(r>r_{0})\right\}\end{split}

(231)

The locality in the radial direction can be seen by rewriting the source part of the integrand using the conservation of current:

\begin{split}&\bar{J}^{r}(\vec{r}_{0})+\frac{1}{\ell(\ell+d-2)}\partial_{r_{0}}\left[r_{0}^{2}\mathscr{D}_{I}\bar{J}^{I}(\vec{r}_{0})\right]\\
&\qquad=\bar{J}^{r}(\vec{r}_{0})-\frac{1}{\ell(\ell+d-2)}\partial_{r_{0}}\left[r_{0}^{3-d}\partial_{r_{0}}(r_{0}^{d-1}\bar{J}^{r}(\vec{r}_{0}))\right]\ .\end{split}

(232)

The radial differential operator here is the same as the one in Eq.(
230
), and after integration by parts, it acts on the Green function to give a delta function. We will see later how this conclusion changes if the poloidal currents become time-dependent.

Electric fields due to static charges

We can repeat our magnetostatic analysis for electrostatics. Let us begin with a time-independent
surface charge density
\bar{\sigma}(\hat{r})
spread out on a thin spherical shell of radius
R
. Explicitly, we take a charge current density of the form

\bar{J}^{t}=\delta(r-R)\ \bar{\sigma}(\hat{r})=\delta(r-R)\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\bar{\sigma}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,

(233)

where we have expanded out the charge density in terms of orthonormal Scalar Spherical Harmonics (SSHs) on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
labelled by
\{\ell,\vec{m}\}
. Using such a decomposition, we can solve for the electric field due to each component and then add it up to get the final answer. Since the symmetry properties of each SSH under
SO(d)
rotation is different, the scalar potential
\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{t}
produced by
\bar{\sigma}_{\ell\vec{m}}
should be proportional to
\bar{\sigma}_{\ell\vec{m}}
. We take an ansatz of the form

\begin{split}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{t}=\sum_{\ell}f_{\ell}(r)\sum_{\vec{m}}\bar{\sigma}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,\end{split}

(234)

and impose the scalar Poisson equation

\begin{split}\frac{1}{r^{d-1}}\partial_{r}[r^{d-1}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{rt}]+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\gamma^{JK}\mathscr{D}_{K}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{It}=\frac{1}{r^{d-1}}\partial_{r}[r^{d-1}\partial_{r}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{t}]+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\mathscr{D}^{2}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{t}=\bar{J}^{t}\ .\end{split}

(235)

Replacing
-\mathscr{D}^{2}
by
\ell(\ell+d-2)
,
we conclude that, away from the spherical shell,
f_{\ell}(r)
should vary as
r^{\ell}
or as
r^{-(\ell+d-2)}
. We should stitch together these two solutions continuously with an appropriate derivative discontinuity given by the charge density. We obtain the final answer

\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{t}=-\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\frac{R\bar{\sigma}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})}{2\ell+d-2}\left[\frac{r^{\ell}}{R^{\ell}}\Theta(r<R)+\frac{R^{\ell+d-2}}{r^{\ell+d-2}}\Theta(r>R)\right]\ .

(236)

In terms of the original data, we have

\begin{split}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{t}&=-\int_{\hat{r}_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\sum_{\ell}\left[\frac{r^{\ell}}{R^{\ell}}\Theta(r<R)+\frac{R^{\ell+d-2}}{r^{\ell+d-2}}\Theta(r>R)\right]\frac{\sum_{\vec{m}}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}^{\ast}(\hat{r}_{0})}{2\ell+d-2}R\bar{\sigma}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r}_{0})\\
&=-\sum_{\ell}\left[\frac{r^{\ell}}{R^{\ell}}\Theta(r<R)+\frac{R^{\ell+d-2}}{r^{\ell+d-2}}\Theta(r>R)\right]\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}\int_{\hat{r}_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\frac{\Pi^{S}_{d,\ell}(\hat{r}|\hat{r}_{0})}{2\ell+d-2}R\bar{\sigma}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r}_{0})\ \\
&=-\int_{\vec{r}_{0}}\sum_{\ell}\left\{\frac{r^{\ell}}{r_{0}^{\ell+d-2}}\Theta(r<r_{0})+\frac{r_{0}^{\ell}}{r^{\ell+d-2}}\Theta(r>r_{0})\right\}\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}\frac{\Pi^{S}_{d,\ell}(\hat{r}|\hat{r}_{0})}{2\ell+d-2}\bar{J}^{t}(\vec{r}_{0})\end{split}

(237)

Here, we have used the SSH addition theorem in the second step. As we did in magnetostatics, we can expand the electrostatic potential as well as the electric fields in terms of spherical harmonics:

\begin{split}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{t}&=\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\overline{E}_{s}(r,\ell,\vec{m})\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,\\
\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{rt}&\equiv\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\overline{E}_{r}(r,\ell,\vec{m})\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,\quad\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{It}\equiv\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\overline{E}_{s}(r,\ell,\vec{m})\mathscr{D}_{I}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,\end{split}

(238)

with
\overline{E}_{r}=\partial_{r}\overline{E}_{s}
. The potential function then has a Green-function expression

\begin{split}\overline{E}_{s}&=-\frac{1}{2\ell+d-2}\int_{\vec{r}_{0}}\left\{\frac{r^{\ell}}{r_{0}^{\ell+d-2}}\Theta(r<r_{0})+\frac{r_{0}^{\ell}}{r^{\ell+d-2}}\Theta(r>r_{0})\right\}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}^{\ast}(\hat{r}_{0})\bar{J}^{t}(\vec{r}_{0})\ .\end{split}

(239)

Multipole expansion outside the electric sources

Unlike the poloidal currents, the static charge distributions do give rise to fields outside them. The electric field outside the charges is then given by

\begin{split}\overline{E}_{s}^{\text{Out}}(r,\ell,\vec{m})=-\frac{\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{J}}$}}^{E}(\ell,\vec{m})}{r^{\ell+d-2}}\ ,\quad\ \overline{E}_{r}^{\text{Out}}(r,\ell,\vec{m})=(\ell+d-2)\frac{\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{J}}$}}^{E}(\ell,\vec{m})}{r^{\ell+d-1}}\ ,\end{split}

(240)

where
\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{J}}$}}^{E}
denotes the spherical electric multipole moments defined via

\begin{split}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{J}}$}}^{E}(\ell,\vec{m})&\equiv\frac{1}{2\ell+d-2}\int_{\vec{r}_{0}}r_{0}^{\ell}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}^{\ast}(\hat{r}_{0})\bar{J}^{t}(\vec{r}_{0})\ .\end{split}

(241)

The corresponding cartesian multipole moment can be defined from the spherical moments via SSH addition theorem, viz.,

\begin{split}\frac{1}{\ell!}\ {}^{E}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{Q}}$}}_{<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}x^{i_{1}}\ldots x^{i_{\ell}}&\equiv\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-1}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\sum_{\vec{m}}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{J}}$}}^{E}(\ell,\vec{m})r^{\ell}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})=\int_{\vec{r}_{0}}\Pi^{S}(\vec{r}|\vec{r}_{0})\bar{J}^{t}(\vec{r}_{0})\ .\end{split}

(242)

or equivalently, we have

\begin{split}{}^{E}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{Q}}$}}_{<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}\equiv(\Pi^{S})^{<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}_{<j_{1}j_{2}\ldots j_{\ell}>}\int_{\vec{r}_{0}}x_{0}^{j_{1}}\ldots x_{0}^{j_{\ell}}\bar{J}^{t}(\vec{r}_{0})\ .\end{split}

(243)

The scalar potential and the cartesian components of the electric field take the form

\begin{split}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{t}^{\text{Out}}&=-\sum_{\ell}\frac{1}{\ell!\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-1}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}{}^{E}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{Q}}$}}_{<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}\frac{x^{i_{1}}\ldots x^{i_{\ell}}}{r^{2\ell+d-2}}=-\sum_{\ell}\frac{(-)^{\ell}}{\ell!}\partial^{i_{1}}\partial^{i_{2}}\ldots\partial^{i_{\ell}}\Bigl\{\frac{{}^{E}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{Q}}$}}_{<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}}{(d-2)|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|r^{d-2}}\Bigr\}\ ,\\
\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{jt}^{\text{Out}}&=\sum_{\ell}\frac{1}{\ell!\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-1}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}{}^{E}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{Q}}$}}_{<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}[(2\ell+d-2)x^{j}x^{i_{\ell}}-r^{2}\ell\delta^{ji_{\ell}}]\frac{x^{i_{1}}\ldots x^{i_{\ell-1}}}{r^{2\ell+d}}\ .\end{split}

(244)

All this is completely analogous to our discussion of magnetostatics in STF language. As we did for magnetic STF moments, explicit expressions for the first few electric STF moments can be written down by contracting against a dummy variable:

\begin{split}{}^{E}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{Q}}$}}&=\int_{\vec{r}}\bar{J}^{t}(\vec{r})\ ,\\
{}^{E}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{Q}}$}}_{<i_{1}>}\kappa^{i_{1}}&=\int_{\vec{r}}(\kappa\cdot r)\ \bar{J}^{t}(\vec{r})\ ,\\
\frac{1}{2!}{}^{E}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{Q}}$}}_{<i_{1}i_{2}>}\kappa^{i_{1}}\kappa^{i_{2}}&=\int_{\vec{r}}\left[\frac{(\kappa\cdot r)^{2}}{2!}-\frac{\kappa^{2}r^{2}}{2d}\right]\bar{J}^{t}(\vec{r})\ ,\\
\frac{1}{3!}{}^{E}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{Q}}$}}_{<i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}>}\kappa^{i_{1}}\kappa^{i_{2}}\kappa^{i_{3}}&=\int_{\vec{r}}\left[\frac{(\kappa\cdot r)^{3}}{3!}-\frac{\kappa^{2}r^{2}}{2(d+2)}(\kappa\cdot r)\right]\bar{J}^{t}(\vec{r})\ ,\\
\frac{1}{4!}{}^{E}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{Q}}$}}_{<i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}i_{4}>}\kappa^{i_{1}}\kappa^{i_{2}}\kappa^{i_{3}}\kappa^{i_{4}}&=\int_{\vec{r}}\Bigg[\frac{(\kappa\cdot r)^{4}}{4!}-\frac{\kappa^{2}r^{2}}{2(d+4)}\frac{(\kappa\cdot r)^{2}}{2!}+\frac{\kappa^{4}r^{4}}{8(d+4)(d+2)}\Bigg]\bar{J}^{t}(\vec{r})\ ,\\
\frac{1}{5!}{}^{E}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{Q}}$}}_{<i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}i_{4}i_{5}>}\kappa^{i_{1}}\kappa^{i_{2}}\kappa^{i_{3}}\kappa^{i_{4}}\kappa^{i_{5}}&=\int_{\vec{r}}\Bigg[\frac{(\kappa\cdot r)^{5}}{5!}-\frac{\kappa^{2}r^{2}}{2(d+6)}\frac{(\kappa\cdot r)^{3}}{3!}+\frac{\kappa^{4}r^{4}}{8(d+6)(d+4)}(\kappa\cdot r)\Bigg]\bar{J}^{t}(\vec{r})\ .\end{split}

(245)

The corresponding STF tensors can be obtained by differentiating with respect to
\kappa_{i}
. Till
\ell=4
, they are given as

\begin{split}{}^{E}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{Q}}$}}&=\int_{\vec{r}}\bar{J}^{t}(\vec{r})\ ,\\
{}^{E}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{Q}}$}}_{<i_{1}>}&=\int_{\vec{r}}x^{i_{1}}\bar{J}^{t}(\vec{r})\ ,\\
{}^{E}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{Q}}$}}_{<i_{1}i_{2}>}&=\int_{\vec{r}}\left[x^{i_{1}}x^{i_{2}}-\frac{r^{2}}{d}\delta^{i_{1}i_{2}}\right]\bar{J}^{t}(\vec{r})\ ,\\
{}^{E}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{Q}}$}}_{<i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}>}&=\int_{\vec{r}}\left[x^{i_{1}}x^{i_{2}}x^{i_{3}}-\frac{r^{2}}{d+2}\left(x^{i_{1}}\delta^{i_{2}i_{3}}+x^{i_{2}}\delta^{i_{1}i_{3}}+x^{i_{3}}\delta^{i_{1}i_{2}}\right)\right]\bar{J}^{t}(\vec{r})\ ,\\
\end{split}

(246)

and

\begin{split}{}^{E}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{Q}}$}}_{<i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}i_{4}>}&=\int_{\vec{r}}\Bigg[x^{i_{1}}x^{i_{2}}x^{i_{3}}x^{i_{4}}\\
&\qquad-\frac{r^{2}}{d+4}\Big(x^{i_{1}}x^{i_{2}}\delta^{i_{3}i_{4}}+x^{i_{1}}x^{i_{3}}\delta^{i_{2}i_{4}}+x^{i_{1}}x^{i_{4}}\delta^{i_{2}i_{3}}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+x^{i_{2}}x^{i_{3}}\delta^{i_{1}i_{4}}+x^{i_{2}}x^{i_{4}}\delta^{i_{1}i_{3}}+x^{i_{3}}x^{i_{4}}\delta^{i_{1}i_{2}}\Big)\\
&\qquad+\frac{r^{4}}{(d+4)(d+2)}\left(\delta^{i_{1}i_{2}}\delta^{i_{3}i_{4}}+\delta^{i_{1}i_{3}}\delta^{i_{2}i_{4}}+\delta^{i_{1}i_{4}}\delta^{i_{2}i_{3}}\right)\Bigg]\bar{J}^{t}(\vec{r})\ .\end{split}

(247)

B.4
Electric multipole radiation

We will now turn to the problem of radiation from time-varying charge distributions and poloidal currents. Over and above the issues discussed above for the toroidal currents, there are some new subtleties which show up in this case.

First, in the dynamical setting, poloidal currents inevitably accompany changes in charge configurations, and the spacetime charge current
\bar{J}^{\mu}(\vec{r},t)
should satisfy the conservation equation

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\bar{J}^{t}+\frac{1}{r^{d-1}}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}(r^{d-1}\bar{J}^{r})+\mathscr{D}_{I}\bar{J}^{I}=0\ .\end{split}

(248)

Maxwell equations are mathematically consistent only if the sources obey this constraint. This means that to solve for the EM fields, we should characterise the class of currents consistent with charge conservation. Second, we need to deal with Gauss law constraint in this sector, i.e., one of the Maxwell equations serves to constrain the initial data of EM fields. Both these facts are intimately tied to gauge invariance in electromagnetism: since the gauge parameter is a scalar function, it is expandable into scalar spherical harmonics (SSHs), and its effect is visible in the scalar sector.

We will address the problem of conservation by imagining that the charge flow is described well by a time-varying electric polarisation field, i.e., we take

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bar{J}^{t}(\vec{r},t)&=-\frac{1}{r^{d-1}}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}[r^{d-1}\bar{P}^{r}(\vec{r},t)]-\mathscr{D}_{I}\bar{P}^{I}(\vec{r},t)\ ,\\
\bar{J}^{r}(\vec{r},t)&=\partial_{t}\bar{P}^{r}(\vec{r},t)\ ,\quad\bar{J}^{I}(\vec{r},t)=\partial_{t}\bar{P}^{I}(\vec{r},t)\ ,\end{split}

(249)

which automatically satisfies the conservation equation. Such a polarisation field can always be defined by time integrating the current densities, i.e.,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bar{P}^{r}(\vec{r},t)\equiv\int dt\ \bar{J}^{r}(\vec{r},t)\ ,\quad\bar{P}^{I}(\vec{r},t)\equiv\int dt\ \bar{J}^{I}(\vec{r},t)\ .\end{split}

(250)

Such polarisation fields also help simplify Gauss law inside charged matter: it becomes the statement of
\vec{E}+\vec{P}
being divergence-free. This parametrisation is not without its subtleties, as we shall discuss in §§
B.5
. But, for now, we will take such time-varying polarisation fields are given and proceed.

We will solve the Maxwell equations by passing to the frequency domain and expanding all fields in terms of scalar spherical harmonics (SSHs). The expansion of the polarisation fields is

\begin{split}\bar{P}^{r}(\vec{r},t)&\equiv\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}e^{-i\omega t}J_{1}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,\\
r^{2}\gamma_{{}_{IJ}}\bar{P}^{J}(\vec{r},t)&\equiv\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}e^{-i\omega t}J_{2}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\mathscr{D}_{I}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ .\end{split}

(251)

Using the same notation/gauge as in statics, we take the scalar/vector potential to be

\begin{split}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{t}(\vec{r},t)&\equiv\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}e^{-i\omega t}E_{s}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,\\
\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{r}(\vec{r},t)&\equiv-\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}e^{-i\omega t}H_{s}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,\\
\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{I}(\vec{r},t)&=0\ .\end{split}

(252)

We aim to solve for
E_{s},H_{s}
in terms of
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
. To this end, we first compute the EM fields corresponding to the above potential:

\begin{split}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{rt}(\vec{r},t)&=\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}e^{-i\omega t}E_{r}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\ \mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,\\
\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{It}(\vec{r},t)&=\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}e^{-i\omega t}E_{s}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\ \mathscr{D}_{I}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,\\
\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{rI}(\vec{r},t)&=\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}e^{-i\omega t}H_{s}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\ \mathscr{D}_{I}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,\\
\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{IJ}(\vec{r},t)&=0\ ,\end{split}

(253)

where
E_{r}=\partial_{r}E_{s}-i\omega H_{s}
. This relation giving
E_{r}
in terms of
E_{s}
and
H_{s}
can also be directly obtained from the Bianchi identity (or the unsourced Maxwell equations).

Next, we write down the sourced Maxwell equations:

\begin{split}\frac{1}{r^{d-1}}\partial_{r}\left(r^{d-1}[E_{r}+J_{1}]\right)-\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)}{r^{2}}[E_{s}+J_{2}]&=0\quad\quad\text{(t-Eqn)}\ ,\\
-\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)}{r^{2}}H_{s}+i\omega[E_{r}+J_{1}]&=0\ \quad\quad\text{(r-Eqn)}\ ,\\
-\frac{1}{r^{d-3}}\partial_{r}\left(r^{d-3}H_{s}\right)+i\omega[E_{s}+J_{2}]&=0\quad\quad\text{(I-Eqn)}\ .\end{split}

(254)

We note how the electric field always shows up in the
\vec{E}+\vec{P}
combination. The above set of coupled ODEs can be solved by introducing the
electric Debye field

\Phi_{E}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})
such that

\begin{split}E_{r}+J_{1}&=\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)}{r^{d-1}}\Phi_{E}\ ,\quad E_{s}+J_{2}=\frac{1}{r^{d-3}}\partial_{r}\Phi_{E}\ ,\quad H_{s}=\frac{i\omega}{r^{d-3}}\Phi_{E}\ .\end{split}

(255)

The relation
E_{r}=\partial_{r}E_{s}-i\omega H_{s}
then becomes the follow inhomogeneous Helmholtz equation for
\Phi_{E}
:

-\frac{1}{r^{3-d}}\partial_{r}\left(r^{3-d}\partial_{r}\Phi_{E}\right)-\omega^{2}\Phi_{E}+\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)}{r^{2}}\Phi_{E}=r^{d-3}\left[J_{1}-\partial_{r}J_{2}\right]\ .

(256)

Hereon, the procedure here is similar to the one adopted for the magnetic Debye field
\Phi_{B}
. The above equation is solved by finding an appropriate Green function
\mathbb{G}_{E}
such that

\Phi_{E}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})=\int_{0}^{\infty}dr_{0}\ \mathbb{G}_{E}(r,r_{0};\omega,\ell)\left[J_{1}(r_{0},\omega,\ell,\vec{m})-\partial_{r_{0}}J_{2}(r_{0},\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\right]

(257)

The Green function
\mathbb{G}_{E}
then obeys

\begin{split}-\partial_{r}[r^{3-d}\partial_{r}\mathbb{G}_{E}]-\omega^{2}r^{3-d}\mathbb{G}_{E}+\ell(\ell+d-2)r^{1-d}\mathbb{G}_{E}=\delta(r-r_{0})\ ,\end{split}

(258)

The magnetic Helmholtz equation in Eq.(
204
) can be mapped to the electric Helmholtz equation through the replacements

d\mapsto 6-d\ ,\quad\ell\mapsto\ell+d-3\ ,\quad\nu\mapsto\nu

(259)

where
\nu\equiv\ell+\frac{d}{2}-1
. Through such a replacement, the results we derived in the magnetic case can be recycled here. Given this, we will be content in stating just the final results in what follows.

Homogeneous spherical waves

The homogenous solutions are as follows: the solution regular everywhere is

r^{\nu+\frac{d}{2}-1}{}_{0}F_{1}\left[1+\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r^{2}}{4}\right]=\Gamma(1+\nu)\left(\frac{\omega}{2}\right)^{-\nu}r^{\frac{d}{2}-1}J_{\nu}(\omega r)\ ,

(260)

and this solution plays the role of the time-delay smearing function in the electric case. The solution that is regular everywhere except the origin is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{1}{r^{\nu-\frac{d}{2}+1}}&\left\{{}_{0}F_{1}\left[1-\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r^{2}}{4}\right]-\frac{\pi\cot\nu\pi}{\Gamma(\nu)\Gamma(1+\nu)}\left(\frac{\omega r}{2}\right)^{2\nu}\ {}_{0}F_{1}\left[1+\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r^{2}}{4}\right]\right\}\\
&=-\left(\frac{\omega}{2}\right)^{\nu}\frac{\pi\ r^{\frac{d}{2}-1}}{\Gamma(\nu)}Y_{\nu}(\omega r)\ .\end{split}

(261)

The outgoing/ingoing solutions are

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{1}{r^{\nu-\frac{d}{2}+1}}&\left\{{}_{0}F_{1}\left[1-\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r^{2}}{4}\right]\pm(1\pm i\cot\nu\pi)\frac{2\pi i}{\Gamma(\nu)^{2}}\frac{1}{2\nu}\left(\frac{\omega r}{2}\right)^{2\nu}\ {}_{0}F_{1}\left[1+\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r^{2}}{4}\right]\right\}\mathbb{V}_{I}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}\\
&=\pm i\left(\frac{\omega}{2}\right)^{\nu}\frac{\pi\ r^{\frac{d}{2}-1}}{\Gamma(\nu)}H^{\pm}_{\nu}(\omega r)\mathbb{V}_{I}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}\end{split}

(262)

As in the magnetic case, when
d
is even and
\nu
is a positive integer, these expressions become indeterminate, and we should take a limit. The radial part of the outgoing waves is

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)&\equiv\frac{1}{r^{\nu-\frac{d}{2}+1}}\\
&\times\left\{{}_{0}F_{1}\left[1-\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r^{2}}{4}\right]+(1+i\cot\nu\pi)\frac{2\pi i}{\Gamma(\nu)^{2}}\frac{1}{2\nu}\left(\frac{\omega r}{2}\right)^{2\nu}\ {}_{0}F_{1}\left[1+\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r^{2}}{4}\right]\right\}\ .\end{split}

(263)

When
\nu
is a half-integer, the function
G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}
can be expressed in terms of reverse Bessel polynomials as

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)=\frac{\theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}(-i\omega r)}{\theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}(0)}\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{r^{\nu-\frac{d}{2}+1}}\ \quad\text{for}\quad\nu\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}\ ,\end{split}

(264)

whose large
r
asymptotic are given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)\to\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-1}\frac{(-i\omega)^{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}}{(d-2)!!}\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{r^{\frac{3-d}{2}}}\ \quad\text{as}\quad r\to\infty\ .\end{split}

(265)

The raising and lowering relations become

\begin{split}-\frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\left[\frac{G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)}{\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-1}r^{\ell+d-2}}\right]&=\frac{G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell+1)}{\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell}r^{\ell+d-1}}\ ,\quad\frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\left[\frac{G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)}{\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-1}\ r^{-\ell}}\right]=\omega^{2}\frac{G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell-1)}{\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-2}\ r^{1-\ell}}\ ,\end{split}

(266)

and the derivative of
G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}
is

\begin{split}\partial_{r}G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)&=-\frac{\ell}{r}G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)\ +\frac{\omega^{2}}{2\ell+d-4}G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell-1)\\
&=-\ell\ G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell+1)+\frac{\ell+d-2}{2\ell+d-2}\frac{\omega^{2}}{2\ell+d-4}G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell-1)\ .\end{split}

(267)

The cartesian version of raising/lowering relations is

\begin{split}-\partial_{i}\left[\frac{G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)}{\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-1}r^{\ell+d-2}}\right]&=x^{i}\frac{G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell+1)}{\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell}r^{\ell+d-1}}\ ,\quad\partial_{i}\left[\frac{G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)}{\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-1}\ r^{-\ell}}\right]=\omega^{2}x^{i}\frac{G_{{}_{@}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell-1)}{\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-2}\ r^{1-\ell}}\ ,\end{split}

(268)

and the electric analog of Eq.(
199
) is given by

\begin{split}(-1)^{\ell}\partial^{<i_{1}}\partial^{i_{2}}\ldots\partial^{i_{\ell}>}\left\{\frac{G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell=0)}{(d-2)r^{d-2}}\right\}=\frac{G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)}{\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-1}r^{\ell+d-2}}x^{<i_{1}}\ldots x^{i_{\ell}>}\ .\end{split}

(269)

Green function for electric radiation

The Green function
\mathbb{G}_{E}
can be constructed by stitching together the homogeneous solutions continuously but with an appropriate discontinuity in its derivative. We have

\begin{split}\mathbb{G}_{E}(r,r_{0};\omega,\ell)&=\frac{1}{2\nu}\frac{r_{<}^{\nu+\frac{d}{2}-1}}{r_{>}^{\nu-\frac{d}{2}+1}}{}_{0}F_{1}\left[1+\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r_{<}^{2}}{4}\right]\\
&\times\left\{{}_{0}F_{1}\left[1-\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r_{>}^{2}}{4}\right]+(1+i\cot\nu\pi)\frac{2\pi i}{\Gamma(\nu)^{2}}\frac{1}{2\nu}\left(\frac{\omega r_{>}}{2}\right)^{2\nu}\ {}_{0}F_{1}\left[1+\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r_{>}^{2}}{4}\right]\right\}\\
&=\frac{1}{2\nu}r_{<}^{\nu+\frac{d}{2}-1}{}_{0}F_{1}\left[1+\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r_{<}^{2}}{4}\right]G_{E}^{\text{Out}}(r_{>},\omega,\ell)\ ,\end{split}

(270)

where we have defined:

\begin{split}r_{>}\equiv\text{Max}(r,r_{0})\ ,\quad r_{<}\equiv\text{Min}(r,r_{0})\ .\end{split}

(271)

We can compute the
the corresponding electric/magnetic field components as

\begin{split}E_{s}+J_{2}&=\frac{1}{r^{d-3}}\partial_{r}\Phi_{E}\\
&=\frac{1}{r^{d-3}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dr_{0}\ \partial_{r}\mathbb{G}_{E}(r,r_{0};\omega,\ell)\left[J_{1}(r_{0},\omega,\ell,\vec{m})-\partial_{r_{0}}J_{2}(r_{0},\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\right]\ ,\\
E_{r}+J_{1}&=\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)}{r^{d-1}}\Phi_{E}\\
&=\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)}{r^{d-1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dr_{0}\ \mathbb{G}_{E}(r,r_{0};\omega,\ell)\left[J_{1}(r_{0},\omega,\ell,\vec{m})-\partial_{r_{0}}J_{2}(r_{0},\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\right]\ ,\\
H_{s}&=\frac{i\omega}{r^{d-3}}\Phi_{E}\\
&=\frac{i\omega}{r^{d-3}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dr_{0}\ \mathbb{G}_{E}(r,r_{0};\omega,\ell)\left[J_{1}(r_{0},\omega,\ell,\vec{m})-\partial_{r_{0}}J_{2}(r_{0},\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\right]\ .\end{split}

(272)

These time-varying electric and magnetic fields sustain each other and propagate outwards as electric multipole radiation.

Fields outside sources

We will now turn to fields outside the sources, determined by electric multipole moments. For
\ell\neq 0
, we will define a dynamic version of the electric multipole moment via

\begin{split}\mathcal{J}^{E}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})&\equiv\frac{\ell}{2\nu}\int_{0}^{\infty}dr_{0}\ r_{0}^{\nu+\frac{d}{2}-1}{}_{0}F_{1}\left[1+\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r_{0}^{2}}{4}\right]\Bigl\{J_{1}(r_{0},\omega,\ell,\vec{m})-\partial_{r_{0}}J_{2}(r_{0},\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\Bigr\}\ .\end{split}

(273)

We will justify this definition and generalise it to the
\ell=0
case later. While this looks very different from the electric multipole moments defined in the static case, we will later see that it reduces to them in the appropriate limit.

The electric Debye field outside the sources is given by

\begin{split}\Phi_{E}^{\text{Out}}&=\frac{1}{\ell}G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)\ \mathcal{J}^{E}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})=\frac{1}{\ell}\frac{\theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}(-i\omega r)}{\theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}(0)}\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{r^{\ell}}\mathcal{J}^{E}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\ .\end{split}

(274)

The corresponding field strength components are

\begin{split}E_{r}^{\text{Out}}&=\frac{\ell+d-2}{r^{d-1}}G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)\ \mathcal{J}^{E}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})=(\ell+d-2)\frac{\theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}(-i\omega r)}{\theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}(0)}\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{r^{\ell+d-1}}\mathcal{J}^{E}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m}),\\
E_{s}^{\text{Out}}&=-\frac{1}{r^{d-2}}G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)\ \mathcal{J}^{E}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})+\frac{1}{r^{d-3}}\frac{\omega^{2}}{\ell(2\ell+d-4)}G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell-1)\ \mathcal{J}^{E}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\\
&=-\frac{\theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}(-i\omega r)}{\theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}(0)}\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{r^{\ell+d-2}}\mathcal{J}^{E}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})+\frac{\omega^{2}}{\ell(2\ell+d-4)}\frac{\theta_{\nu-\frac{3}{2}}(-i\omega r)}{\theta_{\nu-\frac{3}{2}}(0)}\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{r^{\ell+d-4}}\mathcal{J}^{E}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m}),\\
H_{s}^{\text{Out}}&=\frac{i\omega}{r^{d-3}}\frac{1}{\ell}G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)\ \mathcal{J}^{E}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})=\frac{i\omega}{\ell}\frac{\theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}(-i\omega r)}{\theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}(0)}\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{r^{\ell+d-3}}\mathcal{J}^{E}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m}).\end{split}

(275)

Here we have used Eq.(
267
) for evaluating
\partial_{r}G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}
. One check of our multipole moment definition is that it reduces to the correct static expressions in
\omega\to 0
limit, i.e., we get

\begin{split}\overline{E}_{s}^{\text{Out}}(r,\ell,\vec{m})=-\frac{\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{J}}$}}^{E}(\ell,\vec{m})}{r^{\ell+d-2}}\ ,\quad\ \overline{E}_{r}^{\text{Out}}(r,\ell,\vec{m})=(\ell+d-2)\frac{\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{J}}$}}^{E}(\ell,\vec{m})}{r^{\ell+d-1}}\ .\end{split}

(276)

The radiative parts work out to be

\begin{split}\Phi_{E}^{\text{Rad}}&=\frac{1}{\ell}\frac{(-i\omega)^{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}}{(2\nu-2)!!}\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{r^{\frac{3-d}{2}}}\mathcal{J}^{E}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\ ,\\
E_{r}^{\text{Rad}}&=(\ell+d-2)\frac{(-i\omega)^{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}}{(2\nu-2)!!}\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{r^{\frac{d+1}{2}}}\ \mathcal{J}^{E}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m}),\\
E_{s}^{\text{Rad}}&=-\frac{1}{\ell}\frac{(-i\omega)^{\nu+\frac{1}{2}}}{(2\nu-2)!!}\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{r^{\frac{d-3}{2}}}\mathcal{J}^{E}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m}),\\
H_{s}^{\text{Rad}}&=-\frac{1}{\ell}\frac{(-i\omega)^{\nu+\frac{1}{2}}}{(2\nu-2)!!}\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{r^{\frac{d-3}{2}}}\mathcal{J}^{E}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m}),\end{split}

(277)

where we have used Eq.(
265
). Note the faster fall-off of the radial electric field: this is consistent with the expectation that, at large
r
, EM fields can be thought of as transverse plane waves travelling outwards radially.

We now turn to the formulation in terms of cartesian STF tensors. We convert the orthonormal electric moments to STF electric moments by Eq.(
242
) generalised to arbiitrary frequency, i.e.,

\begin{split}\frac{1}{\ell!}\ [{}^{E}\mathcal{Q}(\omega)]_{<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}\ x^{i_{1}}\ldots x^{i_{\ell}}&\equiv\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-1}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\sum_{\vec{m}}\mathcal{J}^{E}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\ r^{\ell}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ .\end{split}

(278)

The cartesian form of the scalar/vector potential can be obtained by substituting
\{E_{s}^{\text{Out}},H_{s}^{\text{Out}}\}
into Eq.(
252
), and converting everything to cartesian coordinates. We end up with

\begin{split}\mathcal{V}_{t}^{\text{Out}}(\vec{r},\omega)&=-\sum_{\ell}\frac{G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)}{\ell!\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-1}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}{}^{E}\mathcal{Q}_{<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}\frac{x^{i_{1}}\ldots x^{i_{\ell}}}{r^{\ell+d-2}}\\
&\qquad+\omega^{2}\sum_{\ell>0}\frac{1}{\ell}\frac{G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell-1)}{\ell!\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-2}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}\ {}^{E}\mathcal{Q}_{<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}\frac{x^{i_{1}}\ldots x^{i_{\ell}}}{r^{\ell+d-3}}\ ,\\
\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\text{Out}}(\vec{r},\omega)&=-i\omega\sum_{\ell>0}\frac{1}{\ell}\frac{G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)}{\ell!\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-1}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}{}^{E}\mathcal{Q}_{<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}\frac{x^{k}x^{i_{1}}\ldots x^{i_{\ell}}}{r^{\ell+d-2}}\ .\end{split}

(279)

The field strengths are given by

28
28

28

Here we use the notation
\text{Anti}_{jk}[T_{jk}]\equiv T_{jk}-T_{kj}
for the anti-symmetrisation operator.

\begin{split}\mathcal{C}_{kt}^{\text{Out}}(\vec{r},\omega)&=\sum_{\ell}\frac{G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)}{\ell!\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-1}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}{}^{E}\mathcal{Q}_{<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}[(2\ell+d-2)x^{k}x^{i_{\ell}}-r^{2}\ell\delta^{ki_{\ell}}]\frac{x^{i_{1}}\ldots x^{i_{\ell-1}}}{r^{\ell+d}}\\
&\qquad-\omega^{2}\sum_{\ell}\frac{G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell-1)}{\ell!\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-2}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}{}^{E}\mathcal{Q}_{<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}[x^{k}x^{i_{\ell}}-r^{2}\delta^{ki_{\ell}}]\frac{x^{i_{1}}\ldots x^{i_{\ell-1}}}{r^{\ell+d-1}}\ ,\\
\mathcal{C}_{jk}^{\text{Out}}(\vec{r},\omega)&=\text{Anti}_{jk}\sum_{\ell}(-i\omega)\frac{G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)}{\ell!\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-1}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}{}^{E}\mathcal{Q}_{<ji_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell-1}>}\frac{x^{k}x^{i_{1}}\ldots x^{i_{\ell-1}}}{r^{\ell+d-2}}\ .\end{split}

(280)

These expressions can be derived by converting the spherical components to cartesian components or by directly differentiating the potentials (using Eq.(
268
) when necessary).
Using Eq.(
265
), the large
r
asymptotics of the potentials work out to be

\begin{split}\mathcal{V}_{t}^{\text{Rad}}(\vec{r},\omega)&=-\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{(d-2)!!|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|r^{\frac{d-3}{2}}}\sum_{\ell>0}\frac{1}{\ell}\frac{(-i\omega)^{\nu+\frac{1}{2}}}{\ell!}\ {}^{E}\mathcal{Q}_{<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}n^{i_{1}}\ldots n^{i_{\ell}}\ ,\\
\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\text{Rad}}(\vec{r},\omega)&=\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{(d-2)!!|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|r^{\frac{d-3}{2}}}\sum_{\ell>0}\frac{1}{\ell}\frac{(-i\omega)^{\nu+\frac{1}{2}}}{\ell!}{}^{E}\mathcal{Q}_{<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}n^{k}n^{i_{1}}\ldots n^{i_{\ell}}\ ,\end{split}

(281)

where we have used the notation
n^{i}\equiv\frac{x^{i}}{r}
. The fall-off here is slower than what might have been naively expected, e.g., in
d=3
, the potentials tend to a non-zero angle-dependent constant as
r\to\infty
instead of becoming zero. The corresponding field strengths, however, have the correct asymptotic fall-off, viz.,

\begin{split}\mathcal{C}_{kt}^{\text{Rad}}(\vec{r},\omega)&=-\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{(d-2)!!|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|r^{\frac{d-1}{2}}}\sum_{\ell}\frac{(-i\omega)^{\nu+\frac{1}{2}}}{\ell!}{}^{E}\mathcal{Q}_{<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}[\delta^{ki_{\ell}}-n^{k}n^{i_{\ell}}]n^{i_{1}}\ldots n^{i_{\ell-1}}\ ,\\
\mathcal{C}_{jk}^{\text{Rad}}(\vec{r},\omega)&=\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{(d-2)!!|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|r^{\frac{d-1}{2}}}\times\text{Anti}_{jk}\Bigl\{n^{k}\sum_{\ell}\frac{(-i\omega)^{\nu+\frac{1}{2}}}{\ell!}{}^{E}\mathcal{Q}_{<ji_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell-1}>}n^{i_{1}}\ldots n^{i_{\ell-1}}\Bigr\}\ .\end{split}

(282)

This suggests that there should be a (large) gauge transformation that brings the potentials to the naively expected fall-offs. Such a gauge transformation can in fact be presented explicitly. Consider the large gauge transformation that removes all the
G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell-1)
terms from the scalar potential. The new potentials are then given by

\begin{split}\mathcal{V}_{t}^{\text{Out,New}}(\vec{r},\omega)&=-\sum_{\ell}\frac{G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)}{\ell!\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-1}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}{}^{E}\mathcal{Q}_{<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}\frac{x^{i_{1}}\ldots x^{i_{\ell}}}{r^{\ell+d-2}}\ ,\\
\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\text{Out,New}}(\vec{r},\omega)&=-i\omega\sum_{\ell>0}\frac{1}{\ell}\frac{G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell)}{\ell!\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-1}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}{}^{E}\mathcal{Q}_{<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}\frac{x^{k}x^{i_{1}}\ldots x^{i_{\ell}}}{r^{\ell+d-2}}\\
&\qquad+\partial_{k}\Bigl\{-i\omega\sum_{\ell>0}\frac{1}{\ell}\frac{G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell-1)}{\ell!\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-2}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}\ {}^{E}\mathcal{Q}_{<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}\frac{x^{i_{1}}\ldots x^{i_{\ell}}}{r^{\ell+d-3}}\Bigr\}\\
&=-i\omega\sum_{\ell>0}\frac{G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell-1)}{\ell!\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-2}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}\ {}^{E}\mathcal{Q}_{<ki_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell-1}>}\frac{x^{i_{1}}\ldots x^{i_{\ell-1}}}{r^{\ell+d-3}}\ .\end{split}

(283)

In the last step, we have evaluated the cartesian derivative using Eq.(
268
).
We can work out the large
r
behaviour for these new potentials using Eq.(
265
):

\begin{split}\mathcal{V}_{t}^{\text{Rad,New}}(\vec{r},\omega)&=-\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{(d-2)!!|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|r^{\frac{d-1}{2}}}\sum_{\ell}\frac{(-i\omega)^{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}}{\ell!}\ {}^{E}\mathcal{Q}_{<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}n^{i_{1}}\ldots n^{i_{\ell}}\ ,\\
\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\text{Rad,New}}(\vec{r},\omega)&=\frac{e^{i\omega r}}{(d-2)!!|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|r^{\frac{d-1}{2}}}\sum_{\ell>0}\frac{(-i\omega)^{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}}{\ell!}\ {}^{E}\mathcal{Q}_{<ki_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell-1}>}n^{i_{1}}\ldots n^{i_{\ell-1}}\ .\end{split}

(284)

These agree with what is expected. Further, potentials in this new gauge also have a nice repeated STF derivative representation (see Eq.(
269
)):

\begin{split}\mathcal{V}_{t}^{\text{Out,New}}(\vec{r},\omega)&=-\sum_{\ell}\frac{(-)^{\ell}}{\ell!}\partial^{i_{1}}\partial^{i_{2}}\ldots\partial^{i_{\ell}}\Bigl\{\frac{{}^{E}\mathcal{Q}_{<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell=0)}{(d-2)|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|r^{d-2}}\Bigr\}\ ,\\
\mathcal{V}_{k}^{\text{Out,New}}(\vec{r},\omega)&=-i\omega\sum_{\ell>0}\frac{(-)^{\ell-1}}{\ell!}\partial^{i_{1}}\partial^{i_{2}}\ldots\partial^{i_{\ell-1}}\Bigl\{\frac{{}^{E}\mathcal{Q}_{<ki_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell-1}>}G_{{}_{E}}^{\text{Out}}(r,\omega,\ell=0)}{(d-2)|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|r^{d-2}}\Bigr\}\ .\end{split}

(285)

The gauge transformation which gives the correct fall-off can also be performed on the full solution in the spherical coordinates. The new potentials are given by

\begin{split}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}^{\text{New}}_{t}(\vec{r},t)&\equiv\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}e^{-i\omega t}[E_{s}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})-i\omega\Lambda_{s}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})]\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,\\
\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}^{\text{New}}_{r}(\vec{r},t)&\equiv-\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}e^{-i\omega t}[H_{s}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})-\partial_{r}\Lambda_{s}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})]\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,\\
\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}^{\text{New}}_{I}(\vec{r},t)&\equiv\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}e^{-i\omega t}\ \Lambda_{s}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\mathscr{D}_{I}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,\end{split}

(286)

where the gauge transformation function is

\begin{split}\Lambda_{s}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})&=\frac{1}{i\omega r^{\ell+d-3}}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\Bigl\{r^{\ell}\Phi_{E}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\Bigr\}\ .\end{split}

(287)

We can simplify the new potential using Eq.(
256
) to get

\begin{split}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}^{\text{New}}_{t}(\vec{r},t)&\equiv-\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}e^{-i\omega t}\left[\frac{\ell}{r^{d-2}}\Phi_{E}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})+J_{1}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\right]\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,\\
\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}^{\text{New}}_{r}(\vec{r},t)&\equiv\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}\frac{e^{-i\omega t}}{i\omega}\left[\frac{\ell}{r^{\ell+d-2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\Bigl\{r^{\ell}\Phi_{E}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\Bigr\}-J_{1}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})+\partial_{r}J_{2}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\right]\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,\\
\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}^{\text{New}}_{I}(\vec{r},t)&\equiv\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}\frac{e^{-i\omega t}}{i\omega}\ \frac{1}{r^{\ell+d-3}}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\Bigl\{r^{\ell}\Phi_{E}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\Bigr\}\mathscr{D}_{I}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ .\end{split}

(288)

B.5
\ell=0
and electrostatic limit

In our discussion of electric multipole radiation till now, we have avoided discussing the static limit, i.e., the limit as
\omega\to 0
. We should expect the static limit to recover our results on static poloidal currents/charges (see §§
B.3
). Unfortunately, this is not easy to see right away from the expressions we have seen till now. The key complication is the charge conservation that relates
time-derivative
of charge density to the currents. In practice, this means that various factors of
\omega
might be introduced or removed using charge conservation. Some care is thus required in
how
we take the
\omega\to 0
limit.

Another related complication is the applicability of our expressions to the
\ell=0
electric multipole. Many formulae in our discussion of electric multipole radiation have
\frac{1}{\ell}
factors, and just setting
\ell=0
does not work. The issue here is again charge conservation: the
\ell=0
electric multipole is just the total electric charge, and it cannot have any time variation. In the frequency domain, this means that
\ell=0
mode always comes with a delta function
\delta(\omega)
, which should then be dealt with some care. The aim of this subsection is twofold: first, we will rewrite the expressions for
\{E_{r},E_{s},H_{s}\}
in the last subsection in terms of (Fourier transforms of) charge and current densities. Next goal is to describe the
\ell=0
and
\omega\to 0
limits of such expressions.

In the last subsection, we parameterised the charge/current densities as (See Eq.(
249
) and Eq.(
251
))

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bar{J}^{t}(\vec{r},t)&=-\frac{1}{r^{d-1}}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}[r^{d-1}\bar{P}^{r}(\vec{r},t)]-\mathscr{D}_{I}\bar{P}^{I}(\vec{r},t)\ ,\\
\bar{J}^{r}(\vec{r},t)&=\partial_{t}\bar{P}^{r}(\vec{r},t)\ ,\quad\bar{J}^{I}(\vec{r},t)=\partial_{t}\bar{P}^{I}(\vec{r},t)\ ,\end{split}

(289)

with the electric polarisation fields expanded as

\begin{split}\bar{P}^{r}(\vec{r},t)&\equiv\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}e^{-i\omega t}J_{1}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,\\
r^{2}\gamma_{{}_{IJ}}\bar{P}^{J}(\vec{r},t)&\equiv\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}e^{-i\omega t}J_{2}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\mathscr{D}_{I}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ .\end{split}

(290)

These equations are equivalent to parameterising the
Fourier transforms
of charge and current densities as

\begin{split}J^{t}(\vec{r},\omega)&=\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\Bigl\{-\frac{1}{r^{d-1}}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}[r^{d-1}J_{1}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})]+\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)}{r^{2}}J_{2}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\Bigr\}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,\\
J^{r}(\vec{r},\omega)&=-i\omega\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}J_{1}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,\\
r^{2}\gamma_{{}_{IK}}J^{K}(\vec{r},\omega)&=-i\omega\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}J_{2}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\mathscr{D}_{I}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ .\end{split}

(291)

For what follows, it is crucial to note that the SSH sum in the last equation has no
\ell=0
contribution. Another observation is that the
\ell=0
component of
J_{2}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})
never enters these expressions. Without loss of generality, we can thus set
J_{2}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})|_{\ell=0}=0
and assume that
J^{I}(\vec{r},\omega)
has no spherically symmetric component.

We will now turn to writing the EM fields in terms of charge/current densities instead of
\{J_{1},J_{2}\}
. To this end, we use the above relations to derive the following identity

\begin{split}&i\omega r^{2}J^{r}(\vec{r},\omega)-\partial_{r}[r^{2}J^{t}(\vec{r},\omega)]\\
&=\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}r^{3-d}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\Bigl\{\frac{1}{r^{3-d}}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(r^{3-d}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\right)+\omega^{2}-\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)}{r^{2}}\Bigr\}[r^{d-1}J_{1}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})]\ ,\\
&\quad+\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\ell(\ell+d-2)[J_{1}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})-\partial_{r}J_{2}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})]\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ .\end{split}

(292)

The combination here is chosen such that the differential operator in the first line of RHS
is the one defining the electric Green function
\mathbb{G}_{E}
(See Eq.(
258
)). In the last line of RHS, we recognise the
\{J_{1},J_{2}\}
source for the electric Debye field
\Phi_{E}
in Eq.(
257
) . Using these facts, we can then write

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{r^{d-1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dr_{0}\int_{\hat{r}_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathscr{Y}^{\ast}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r}_{0})\ \mathbb{G}_{E}(r,r_{0};\omega,\ell)\left\{i\omega r_{0}^{2}J^{r}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)-\partial_{r_{0}}[r_{0}^{2}J^{t}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)]\right\}\\
&=-J_{1}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})+\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)}{r^{d-1}}\Phi_{E}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\ .\end{split}

(293)

We recognise here the combination that defines
E_{r}
(c.f. Eq.(
272
)), i.e.,

\begin{split}E_{r}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})&=\frac{1}{r^{d-1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dr_{0}\int_{\hat{r}_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathscr{Y}^{\ast}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r}_{0})\ \mathbb{G}_{E}(r,r_{0};\omega,\ell)\Bigl\{i\omega r_{0}^{2}J^{r}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)-\partial_{r_{0}}[r_{0}^{2}J^{t}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)]\Bigr\}\ .\end{split}

(294)

One corollary is a formula for electric multipole moment directly in terms of charges/currents: we use Eq.(
270
) to evaluate
\mathbb{G}_{E}
outside the sources and compare the result against Eq.(
275
). This yields

\begin{split}\mathcal{J}^{E}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})&\equiv\frac{1}{2\nu(\ell+d-2)}\int_{0}^{\infty}dr_{0}\int_{\hat{r}_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\ r_{0}^{\nu+\frac{d}{2}-1}\mathscr{Y}^{\ast}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r}_{0})\ {}_{0}F_{1}\left[1+\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r_{0}^{2}}{4}\right]\\
&\qquad\times\Bigl\{i\omega r_{0}^{2}J^{r}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)-\partial_{r_{0}}[r_{0}^{2}J^{t}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)]\Bigr\}\ .\end{split}

(295)

This generalises our earlier definition in Eq.(
273
) to general source distributions. As a bonus, we now have an expression where both
\ell=0
and
\omega\to 0
limits can be taken and be seen to give a non-zero electric moment, as expected. In fact, the static limit coincides with the electrostatics definition in §§
B.3
as can be seen from

\begin{split}\mathcal{J}^{E}(\omega=0,\ell,\vec{m})&=-\frac{1}{2\nu(\ell+d-2)}\int_{0}^{\infty}dr_{0}\int_{\hat{r}_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\ r_{0}^{\ell+d-2}\mathscr{Y}^{\ast}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r}_{0})\ \partial_{r_{0}}[r_{0}^{2}J^{t}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)]\\
&=\frac{1}{2\nu}\int_{0}^{\infty}dr_{0}\int_{\hat{r}_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\ r_{0}^{\ell+d-1}\mathscr{Y}^{\ast}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r}_{0})\ J^{t}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega=0)\ .\end{split}

(296)

The last line follows via integration by parts. The above expression for electric multipole moment can also be converted into an STF moment via

\begin{split}\frac{1}{\ell!}&\ {}^{E}\mathcal{Q}(\omega)_{<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}x^{i_{1}}\ldots x^{i_{\ell}}\\
&\equiv\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell-1}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\sum_{\vec{m}}\mathcal{J}^{E}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\ r^{\ell}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\\
&=\frac{1}{\ell+d-2}\int_{\vec{r}_{0}}\ \Pi^{S}(\vec{r}|\vec{r}_{0})\ {}_{0}F_{1}\left[1+\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r_{0}^{2}}{4}\right]\Bigl\{i\omega r_{0}J^{r}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)-\frac{1}{r_{0}}\partial_{r_{0}}[r_{0}^{2}J^{t}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)]\Bigr\}\ .\end{split}

(297)

Stripping off the
x^{i}
’s on both sides, we get the STF electric multipole tensor as

\begin{split}{}^{E}\mathcal{Q}(\omega)_{<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}&=\frac{(\Pi^{S})^{<i_{1}\ldots i_{\ell}>}_{<j_{1}\ldots j_{\ell}>}}{\ell+d-2}\int_{\vec{r}}\ x^{j_{1}}\ldots x^{j_{\ell}}\ {}_{0}F_{1}\left[1+\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r^{2}}{4}\right]\Bigl\{i\omega rJ^{r}(\vec{r},\omega)-\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}[r^{2}J^{t}(\vec{r},\omega)]\Bigr\}\ .\end{split}

(298)

We now turn to how the magnetostatics of the poloidal currents is recovered from our expressions. We will do this by relating the current combination that sources the poloidal magnetic field (Eq.(
231
)) to the source of the electric Debye field, viz.,

\begin{split}\int_{\hat{r}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}^{\ast}(\hat{r})&\Bigl\{J^{r}(\vec{r},\omega)+\frac{1}{\ell(\ell+d-2)}\partial_{r}\left[r^{2}\mathscr{D}_{I}J^{I}(\vec{r},\omega)\right]\Bigr\}\\
&=-i\omega[J_{1}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})-\partial_{r}J_{2}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})]\ .\end{split}

(299)

This allows us to rewrite
H_{s}
in Eq.(
272
) in the form

\begin{split}H_{s}&=-\frac{1}{r^{d-3}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dr_{0}\int_{\hat{r}_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}^{\ast}(\hat{r}_{0})\ \mathbb{G}_{E}(r,r_{0};\omega,\ell)\\
&\qquad\times\Bigl\{J^{r}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)+\frac{1}{\ell(\ell+d-2)}\partial_{r_{0}}\left[r_{0}^{2}\mathscr{D}_{I}J^{I}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)\right]\Bigr\}\ .\end{split}

(300)

This form then has a straightforward static limit where it reduces to Eq.(
231
). If we account for the fact that
J^{I}
has no
\ell=0
component, the above expression also has a finite
\ell=0
limit. The outside fields in Eq.(
275
) also work out provided

\begin{split}0&=i\omega\ \mathcal{J}^{E}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})+\frac{\ell}{2\nu}\int_{0}^{\infty}dr_{0}\int_{\hat{r}_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}^{\ast}(\hat{r}_{0})\ r_{0}^{\nu+\frac{d}{2}-1}{}_{0}F_{1}\left[1+\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r_{0}^{2}}{4}\right]\\
&\qquad\times\Bigl\{J^{r}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)+\frac{1}{\ell(\ell+d-2)}\partial_{r_{0}}\left[r_{0}^{2}\mathscr{D}_{I}J^{I}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)\right]\Bigr\}\ .\end{split}

(301)

This seems to give a new expression for the electric multipole moment that is different from Eq.(
295
). But, using the conservation equation (in the frequency domain)

\begin{split}i\omega J^{t}=\frac{1}{r^{d-1}}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}(r^{d-1}J^{r})+\mathscr{D}_{I}J^{I}\ ,\end{split}

(302)

the difference between the two
\mathcal{J}^{E}
definitions can be shown to be proportional to

\begin{split}&\int_{0}^{\infty}dr_{0}\int_{\hat{r}_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}^{\ast}(\hat{r}_{0})\ r_{0}^{\nu+\frac{d}{2}-1}{}_{0}F_{1}\left[1+\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r_{0}^{2}}{4}\right]\\
&\qquad\times r_{0}^{3-d}\Bigl\{-\frac{1}{r_{0}^{3-d}}\frac{\partial}{\partial r_{0}}\left(r_{0}^{3-d}\frac{\partial}{\partial r_{0}}\right)-\omega^{2}+\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)}{r_{0}^{2}}\Bigr\}[r_{0}^{d-1}J^{r}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)]\ .\end{split}

(303)

This expression is zero since the derivative operator in the second line can be shifted onto its homogeneous solution in the first line via integration by parts. Finally, we can get an expression for
E_{s}
in terms of charge and current densities by using

\begin{split}\partial_{r}E_{s}&=E_{r}+i\omega H_{s}\\
&=\int_{0}^{\infty}dr_{0}\int_{\hat{r}_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathscr{Y}^{\ast}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r}_{0})\ \mathbb{G}_{E}(r,r_{0};\omega,\ell)\\
&\qquad\times\Bigl\{i\omega\frac{r_{0}^{2}-r^{2}}{r^{d-1}}J^{r}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)-\partial_{r_{0}}\left[\frac{r_{0}^{2}}{r^{d-1}}J^{t}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)+i\omega\frac{r_{0}^{2}}{r^{d-3}}\frac{\mathscr{D}_{I}J^{I}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)}{\ell(\ell+d-2)}\right]\Bigr\}\ .\end{split}

(304)

The last line follows from Eqs.(
294
) and (
300
). As
r\to\infty
the field
E_{s}\to H_{s}
(see Eq.(
277
)), and we can integrate the above equation to obtain

\begin{split}E_{s}&=H_{s}(r\to\infty)+\int_{\infty}^{r}dr_{1}\int_{0}^{\infty}dr_{0}\int_{\hat{r}_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathscr{Y}^{\ast}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r}_{0})\ \mathbb{G}_{E}(r_{1},r_{0};\omega,\ell)\\
&\qquad\times\Bigl\{i\omega\frac{r_{0}^{2}-r_{1}^{2}}{r_{1}^{d-1}}J^{r}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)-\partial_{r_{0}}\left[\frac{r_{0}^{2}}{r_{1}^{d-1}}J^{t}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)+i\omega\frac{r_{0}^{2}}{r_{1}^{d-3}}\frac{\mathscr{D}_{I}J^{I}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)}{\ell(\ell+d-2)}\right]\Bigr\}.\end{split}

(305)

In this form, we can easily take
\omega\to 0
as well as
\ell=0
limits (provided we remember that
J^{I}
has no
\ell=0
component). Note that, for
d>3
, we have
H_{s}(r\to\infty)=0
, and we can drop the first term entirely.

Appendix C
Multipole expansion in flat space II

C.1
Radiative power loss (Larmor’s formula)

Before moving to the influence phase, it is instructive to generalise the textbook description of radiative power loss in EM to arbitrary dimensions. The power carried away by the radiation can be computed using the EM energy-momentum tensor:

{}^{\text{(EM)}}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${T}$}}^{\mu\nu}=\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}^{\mu\alpha}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{\alpha}{}^{\nu}-\frac{1}{4}\eta^{\mu\nu}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{\alpha\beta}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}^{\alpha\beta}\ .

(306)

The energy flux through a sphere at radial coordinate
r
enclosing the origin is then the sphere integral of
T_{t}^{r}
(i.e., the radial component of the Poynting vector in the frequency domain). We are interested in the energy flux at large
r
, which can be computed using radiative fields given in Eqs.(
277
) and (
212
). We obtain

\begin{split}\mathcal{P}(\omega)&\equiv\lim_{r\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{r}}r^{d-1}\ {}^{\text{(EM)}}T_{t}{}^{r}(r,\omega,\Omega)\\
&=\lim_{r\to\infty}r^{d-3}\left[\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\ell(\ell+d-2)E_{s}^{\ast}H_{s}+\sum_{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}E_{v}^{*}H_{v}\right]\\
&=\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\frac{\ell+d-2}{\ell}\frac{\omega^{2\nu+1}}{[(2\nu-2)!!]^{2}}|\mathcal{J}^{E}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})|^{2}+\sum_{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}\frac{\omega^{2\nu+1}}{[(2\nu-2)!!]^{2}}|\mathcal{J}^{B}(\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})|^{2}\ ,\end{split}

(307)

where we have used the orthonormality of scalar/vector spherical harmonics to perform the sphere integrals. The sum over
\ell
ranges from
\ell=1
to
\ell=\infty
, since the monopole moment at
\ell=0
(the total electric charge), is always time-independent and does not result in radiation.

When the number of spatial dimensions
d
is odd, the number
\nu\equiv\ell+\frac{d}{2}-1
is a half-integer, and the power loss
\mathcal{P}(\omega)
is an even function of
\omega
. This means that the power loss is always non-negative and is invariant under time reversal, i.e., time-reversing the charges/currents still results in an irreversible loss of energy into radiation.

The situation is qualitatively different when
d
