For our de Sitter analysis, we will also work with the Hubble’s constant,
H
, set to 1. To check the dimensional consistency of equations in the following sections, one should restore the
H
’s, after which they can be checked against the above table.

Appendix D
EM point source in dS

In this section, we will describe the EM fields of a comoving point source in dS
d+1
. The discussion here is straightforward and is provided mainly to establish our conventions about multipole moments in dS. We will also frame our discussion in a way that the similarities to holographic renormalisation
[
2
,
3
,
4
]
are obvious. Our discussion here also closely parallels the discussion of EM fields around planar AdS black holes

[
99
]
but with crucial differences. Some other authors have also studied electromagnetic fields in de Sitter in various settings, such as
[
100
,
40
,
101
,
102
,
103
,
104
,
105
]
.

Consider then a classical point particle placed at the south pole of global de Sitter, at the centre of the southern static patch. We will characterise this point particle by its electric and magnetic multipole moments, which we take to be time-dependent. We will give a precise definition of these moments below for particles in dS, using how EM fields behave as we approach the particle. Our goal would be to characterise the radiative loss suffered by the particle in terms of these moments.

Our definition of near-zone multipole moments is strongly guided by the principle that they should simply extend the corresponding flat spacetime definitions. This fact should be contrasted with
far-zone
multipole moments defined, say using asymptotic behaviour at future time-like infinity. As we shall see, given the different asymptotics of dS, such far-zone moments do not have any simple relation to their flat-space counterparts
[
106
]
.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{\mu\nu}
denote the EM field strength due to the particle in the frequency domain corresponding to outgoing time
u
. In the outgoing coordinates of the static patch, we will expand each component of this field strength into appropriate scalar/vector spherical harmonics as follows:

\begin{split}\mathcal{C}_{ru}(r,\omega,\Omega)&\equiv\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\mathcal{E}_{r}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\Omega)=\mathcal{C}^{ur}(r,\omega,\Omega)\ ,\\
\mathcal{C}_{rI}(r,\omega,\Omega)&\equiv\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\mathcal{B}_{rs}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\mathscr{D}_{I}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\Omega)+\sum_{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}\mathcal{B}_{rv}(r,\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})\mathbb{V}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}_{I}(\Omega)=r^{2}\gamma_{IJ}\mathcal{C}^{Ju}(r,\omega,\Omega)\ ,\\
\mathcal{C}_{Iu}(r,\omega,\Omega)&=\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\mathcal{E}_{s}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\mathscr{D}_{I}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\Omega)+\sum_{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}\mathcal{E}_{v}(r,\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})\mathbb{V}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}_{I}(\Omega)\ ,\\
\mathcal{C}_{IJ}(r,\omega,\Omega)&\equiv\sum_{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}\mathcal{H}_{vv}(r,\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})\left[\mathscr{D}_{I}\mathbb{V}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}_{J}(\Omega)-\mathscr{D}_{J}\mathbb{V}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}_{I}(\Omega)\right]=r^{4}\gamma_{IK}\gamma_{JL}\mathcal{C}^{KL}(r,\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})\ .\end{split}

(396)

Here
\Omega
denotes the angular co-ordinates on the sphere
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, indices
I,J,K
denote vector indices on the sphere,
\gamma_{IJ}
denotes the unit-sphere metric and
\mathscr{D}_{I}
is its associated covariant derivative. We have also indicated the relation to the tensor
\mathcal{C}^{\mu\nu}
with raised indices for future convenience. Another useful combination is

\begin{split}r^{2}\gamma_{IJ}\mathcal{C}^{rJ}(r,\omega,\Omega)&=(1-r^{2})\mathcal{C}_{rI}(r,\omega,\Omega)+\mathcal{C}_{Iu}(r,\omega,\Omega)\\
&\equiv\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\mathcal{H}_{s}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\mathscr{D}_{I}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\Omega)+\sum_{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}\mathcal{H}_{v}(r,\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})\mathbb{V}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}_{I}(\Omega)\ ,\end{split}

(397)

where we have defined

\begin{split}\mathcal{H}_{s}&\equiv(1-r^{2})\mathcal{B}_{rs}+\mathcal{E}_{s}\ ,\quad\mathcal{H}_{v}\equiv(1-r^{2})\mathcal{B}_{rv}+\mathcal{E}_{v}\ .\end{split}

(398)

Our definitions here closely parallel the spherical harmonic expansion in flat space described in Appendix

B
. We use calligraphic letters here to encode the fact that the above quantities are defined in the frequency domain conjugate to
u
, which differ, in the flat limit, from frequency domain expressions conjugate to
t
by an additional factor of
e^{i\omega r}
. We use subscripts
s
and
v
to denote sphere vector indices in the scalar/vector sector, respectively. The time-reversal covariant combinations are
\{\mathcal{E}_{s},\mathcal{E}_{v},\mathcal{E}_{r}\}
and
\{\mathcal{H}_{s},\mathcal{H}_{v},\mathcal{H}_{vv}\}
, with the intrinsic time-reversal parity being even and odd for the two sets, respectively.

Given the spherical harmonic decomposition given above, we can recast the Maxwell equations in terms of the above components. The source-free Maxwell equations (or equivalently the Bianchi identity of electromagnetism
\partial_{[\mu}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{\nu\lambda]}=0
) take the following form:

\begin{split}\mathcal{E}_{v}&=i\omega\ \mathcal{H}_{vv}\ ,\quad\mathcal{B}_{rv}=\partial_{r}\mathcal{H}_{vv}\ ,\quad\mathcal{E}_{r}=\partial_{r}\mathcal{E}_{s}-i\omega\ \mathcal{B}_{rs}\ ,\end{split}

(399)

or an equivalent time-reversal covariant form

\begin{split}\mathcal{E}_{v}&=i\omega\ \mathcal{H}_{vv}\ ,\quad\mathcal{H}_{v}=D_{+}\mathcal{H}_{vv}\ ,\quad(1-r^{2})\mathcal{E}_{r}=D_{+}\mathcal{E}_{s}-i\omega\ \mathcal{H}_{s}\ ,\end{split}

(400)

where
D_{+}\equiv(1-r^{2})\partial_{r}+i\omega
. As usual, these equations can be solved by introduction of the vector potential in some gauge. While we will indeed chose a gauge eventually, it is more illuminating to first analyse this system via gauge invariant observables (i.e., EM fields).

The sourced Maxwell equations outside the particle take the form
\partial_{\mu}\left[\sqrt{-g}\ \overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}{}^{\mu\nu}\right]=0
, which when written out in terms of the components become

\begin{split}-i\omega\ \mathcal{E}_{r}+\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)}{r^{2}}\mathcal{H}_{s}&=0\qquad\text{(r-Eqn)}\ ,\\
\frac{1}{r^{d-1}}\partial_{r}\left[r^{d-1}\mathcal{E}_{r}\right]+\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)}{r^{2}}\mathcal{B}_{rs}&=0\qquad\text{(u-Eqn)}\ ,\\
\frac{1}{r^{d-3}}\partial_{r}\left[r^{d-3}\mathcal{H}_{s}\right]+i\omega\ \mathcal{B}_{rs}&=0\qquad\text{(I-Eqn scalar)}\ ,\\
\frac{1}{r^{d-3}}D_{+}\left[r^{d-3}\mathcal{H}_{v}\right]-i\omega\ \mathcal{E}_{v}-\frac{(\ell+1)(\ell+d-3)}{r^{2}}\mathcal{H}_{vv}&=0\qquad\text{(I-Eqn vector)}\ .\end{split}

(401)

Here, we have indicated the equation coming from each component. As is well-known, these set of equations are not all independent: the
r
-equation above is the Gauss constraint for radial evolution, which is preserved by the next two equations with radial derivatives. Alternately, the
u
-equation above is the Gauss constraint for
u
evolution, which is preserved by the first/third equations with time derivatives. Since our goal is to examine how the near zone data is given in terms of multipole moments of the particle, we will take the radial evolution perspective.

In terms of time reversal covariant quantities, we can recast the above equations in the form

\begin{split}\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)}{r^{2}}\mathcal{H}_{s}&=i\omega\ \mathcal{E}_{r}\ ,\\
\frac{1}{r^{d-1}}D_{+}\left[r^{d-1}\mathcal{E}_{r}\right]&=\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)}{r^{2}}\mathcal{E}_{s}\ ,\\
\frac{1}{r^{d-3}}D_{+}\left[r^{d-3}\mathcal{H}_{s}\right]&=i\omega\ \mathcal{E}_{s}\ ,\\
\frac{1}{r^{d-3}}D_{+}\left[r^{d-3}\mathcal{H}_{v}\right]&=i\omega\ \mathcal{E}_{v}+\frac{(\ell+1)(\ell+d-3)}{r^{2}}\mathcal{H}_{vv}\ .\end{split}

(402)

To conclude, the set of seven equations in Eqs.(
400
)
and (
402
) define the Maxwell system in dS
d+1
.

Examining them as a set of coupled radial ODEs for the six quantities
\{\mathcal{E}_{s},\mathcal{E}_{v},\mathcal{E}_{r},\mathcal{H}_{s},\mathcal{H}_{v},\mathcal{H}_{vv}\}
, we note the following structure: we can first solve the two equations with no radial derivatives to express
\{\mathcal{E}_{v},\mathcal{H}_{s}\}
in terms of rest four quantities. Once this is done, one of the radial equations involving
D_{+}\left[r^{d-3}\mathcal{H}_{s}\right]
is also solved for ‘free’. Among the seven equations, we are then left with four first-order radial evolution equations (one each for
\{\mathcal{E}_{s},\mathcal{E}_{r},\mathcal{H}_{v},\mathcal{H}_{vv}\}
). Thus, given the near zone values of these four fields, one can radially evolve the above set of equations to get the EM fields far away from the particle.

Among all possible near-zone data, it is intuitively clear that roughly half would give rise to outgoing EM waves, whereas the other half would result in incoming EM waves.
37
37

37

We say roughly since this argument ignores time-independent solutions, which are neither incoming nor outgoing. But such zero frequency solutions are a set of measure zero in the space of all solutions of Maxwell equations and can hence be justifiably ignored in this counting.
More generally, if we wanted to also impose boundary conditions far away (or in the case of dS-SK on the near zone of the other branch), the correct thing to do is to constrain only two out of the four quantities. Motivated by the above heuristic argument, we will seek EM fields that satisfy the following boundary conditions
at
r=0
:

\begin{split}\mathcal{J}^{E}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})&\equiv-\lim_{r\to 0}\ r^{\ell+d-2}\mathcal{E}_{s}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\ ,\\
\mathcal{J}^{B}(\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})&\equiv\lim_{r\to 0}\ r^{\ell+d-3}\mathcal{H}_{vv}(r,\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})\ .\end{split}

(403)

Here
\mathcal{J}^{E,B}
are the electric/magnetic multipole moments of the particle, and we have chosen here the appropriate powers of
r
to agree with the flat space definitions in Eqs.(
209
) and (
273
). We then expect the near-zone behaviour of the form

\begin{split}\mathcal{E}_{r}&\sim(\ell+d-2)\frac{\mathcal{J}^{E}}{r^{\ell+d-1}}\ ,\ \mathcal{E}_{s}\sim-\frac{\mathcal{J}^{E}}{r^{\ell+d-2}}\ ,\ \mathcal{E}_{v}\sim\frac{i\omega\mathcal{J}^{B}}{r^{\ell+d-3}}\ ,\\
\mathcal{H}_{s}&\sim\frac{i\omega\mathcal{J}^{E}}{\ell r^{\ell+d-3}}\ ,\ \mathcal{H}_{v}\sim-(\ell+d-3)\frac{\mathcal{J}^{B}}{r^{\ell+d-2}}\ ,\ \mathcal{H}_{vv}\sim\frac{\mathcal{J}^{B}}{r^{\ell+d-3}}\ .\end{split}

(404)

Here, the terms without
i\omega
correspond exactly to the standard static multipole solutions in flat spacetime. The
i\omega
terms represent the leading quasi-static correction in flat spacetime: the component
\mathcal{E}_{v}
is the induced EMF due to changing magnetic moment, whereas
the component
\mathcal{H}_{s}
is the magnetic field due to Maxwell’s displacement current. This fact
gives a physical justification of why we treat the pair
\{\mathcal{E}_{v},\mathcal{H}_{s}\}
as being derived from the other four: at a given order in quasi-static expansion, these components can be obtained from the other four at one less order.

For a given multipole moment
\mathcal{J}^{E,B}
, we should then arrange the sub-leading near zone behaviour of the other two fields
\{\mathcal{E}_{r},\mathcal{H}_{v}\}
to get an outgoing wave solution: the powers that need to be controlled happen to be
1/r^{\ell-1}
terms in
\mathcal{E}_{r}
and
1/r^{\ell}
terms in
\mathcal{H}_{s}
. In fact, as we shall show below, the near zone behaviour of these two other fields, appropriately renormalised, actually encodes the radiation reaction on the particle corresponding to these multipole moments. More precisely, we will show that

\begin{split}\lim_{r\to 0}r^{1-\ell}\left[\mathcal{E}_{r}+\text{(counter-term proportional to $\mathcal{E}_{s}$)}\right]&=\text{Radiation reaction on $\mathcal{J}^{E}$}\ ,\\
\lim_{r\to 0}r^{-\ell}\left[\mathcal{H}_{v}+\text{(counter-term proportional to $\mathcal{H}_{vv}$)}\right]&=\text{Radiation reaction on $\mathcal{J}^{B}$}\ .\end{split}

(405)

This statement relates the sub-dominant behaviour in the near zone to radiation reaction.

To make these statements precise, it is convenient to express the
\ell\neq 0
EM field strengths in terms of electric/magnetic
Hertz-Debye scalars
, i.e., we write

\begin{split}\mathcal{E}_{r}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})&=\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)}{r^{d-1}}\Phi_{E}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\ ,\\
\mathcal{E}_{s}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})=\frac{1}{r^{d-3}}D_{+}\Phi_{E}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\ &,\ \mathcal{E}_{v}(r,\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})=i\omega\Phi_{B}(r,\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})\ ,\\
\mathcal{H}_{s}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})=\frac{i\omega}{r^{d-3}}\Phi_{E}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\ &,\ \mathcal{H}_{v}(r,\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})=D_{+}\Phi_{B}(r,\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})\ ,\\
\mathcal{H}_{vv}(r,\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})&=\Phi_{B}(r,\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})\ ,\\
\mathcal{B}_{rs}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})=-\frac{1}{r^{d-3}}\partial_{r}\Phi_{E}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\ &,\ \mathcal{B}_{rv}(r,\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})=\partial_{r}\Phi_{B}(r,\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})\ .\\
\end{split}

(406)

The reader is encouraged to check that this form automatically satisfies the Maxwell equations in Eqs.(
400
) and (
402
), provided the Hertz-Debye scalar fields satisfy the following radial ODEs:

\begin{split}\frac{1}{r^{3-d}}D_{+}\left[r^{3-d}D_{+}\Phi_{E}\right]&+\omega^{2}\Phi_{E}-(1-r^{2})\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)}{r^{2}}\Phi_{E}=0\ ,\\
\frac{1}{r^{d-3}}D_{+}\left[r^{d-3}D_{+}\Phi_{B}\right]&+\omega^{2}\Phi_{B}-(1-r^{2})\frac{(\ell+1)(\ell+d-3)}{r^{2}}\Phi_{B}=0\ .\end{split}

(407)

In terms of the scalar fields, the Maxwell system reduces to a set of decoupled radial ODEs. An alternate route to get the same results is to write down the gauge potentials
\mathcal{V}_{\mu}
in the
Hertz-Debye gauge
, i.e., we take

\begin{split}\mathcal{V}_{u}(r,\omega,\Omega)&=r^{3-d}\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}D_{+}\Phi_{E}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\ \mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\Omega)\ ,\\
\mathcal{V}_{r}(r,\omega,\Omega)&=r^{3-d}\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\partial_{r}\Phi_{E}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\ \mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\Omega)\ ,\\
\mathcal{V}_{I}(r,\omega,\Omega)&=\sum_{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}\Phi_{B}(r,\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})\ \mathbb{V}_{I}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}(\Omega)\ .\end{split}

(408)

In this gauge, we essentially set all the electric sector gauge fields to be normal to the sphere directions. The magnetic sector is gauge invariant due to the divergencelessness of the
\mathbb{V}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}
. It can then be checked that the field strengths derived from these potentials turn out to be the expressions in Eq.(
406
).

The radial ODEs for the Debye potentials can be solved with appropriate boundary conditions inherited from the corresponding boundary conditions on the field strengths defined in (
404
), which are:

\begin{split}-\lim_{r\to 0}r^{\ell+1}D_{+}\Phi_{E}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})&=\mathcal{J}^{E}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\ ,\\
\lim_{r\to 0}r^{\ell+d-2}\Phi_{B}(r,\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})&=\mathcal{J}^{B}(\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})\ .\end{split}

(409)

We can now solve the fields in terms of bulk to boundary propagators for the two scalars. These propagators are special cases of the propagators we found for the generic class of scalars in part I
[
14
]
. We will review some of the properties of these propagators that we need in our analysis here, but we refer the reader to part I for a much more extensive discussion. One writes the Debye potentials as:

\begin{split}\Phi_{E}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})&=\frac{1}{\ell}G^{\text{Out}}_{E}(r,\omega,\ell)\mathcal{J}^{E}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\ ,\\
\Phi_{B}(r,\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})&=G^{\text{Out}}_{B}(r,\omega,\ell)\mathcal{J}^{B}(\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})\ .\end{split}

(410)

The extra factor of
\ell
is a convenient normalisation for the
\Phi_{E}
Debye scalar because the scalar satisfies a Neumann boundary condition at the origin.

The boundary to bulk propagators can be written explicitly in terms of Gauss hypergeometric functions given as follows:

\begin{split}&G^{\text{Out}}_{E}(r,\omega,\ell)=\frac{r^{\ell+d-2}(1+r)^{-i\omega}}{\Gamma(1-i\omega)\Gamma\left(\ell+\frac{d}{2}-1\right)}\\
&\quad\times\Gamma\left(\frac{\ell+2-i\omega}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{\ell+d-2-i\omega}{2}\right)\ {}_{2}F_{1}\left[\frac{\ell+2-i\omega}{2},\frac{\ell+d-2-i\omega}{2};1-i\omega;1-r^{2}\right]\ ,\\
&G^{\text{Out}}_{B}(r,\omega,\ell)=\frac{r^{\ell+1}(1+r)^{-i\omega}}{\Gamma(1-i\omega)\Gamma\left(\ell+\frac{d}{2}-1\right)}\\
&\quad\times\Gamma\left(\frac{\ell+1-i\omega}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{\ell+d-1-i\omega}{2}\right)\ {}_{2}F_{1}\left[\frac{\ell+1-i\omega}{2},\frac{\ell+d-1-i\omega}{2};1-i\omega;1-r^{2}\right]\ .\end{split}

(411)

The above hypergeometric functions, for odd values of
d
, are polynomials in
r
that generalise the reverse Bessel polynomials one obtains in the study of outgoing radiation in
3+1
dimensional spacetime
38
38

38

In appendix
C
, we have generalised the reverse Bessel polynomials to arbitrary even dimensional spacetimes.
. Later in this appendix, we show how the
G^{\text{Out}}_{E/B}
gives Hubble corrections to the reverse Bessel polynomials in flat spacetimes with odd values of
d
. Much like the case in the corresponding flat spacetimes, for even values of
d
, the functions do not reduce to a polynomial form in
r
.

The hypergeometric functions are defined by a series expansion about the points where the last argument goes to zero. This tells us that the above retarded boundary-to-bulk propagators have a nice expansion at the horizon
r=1
. On the other hand, to obtain their behaviour at
r=0
, the following equivalent form is instructive:

\begin{split}G^{\text{Out}}_{E}&=r^{-\ell}(1+r)^{-i\omega}\\
&\times\Bigl\{{}_{2}F_{1}\left[\frac{-\ell-i\omega}{2},\frac{4-d-\ell-i\omega}{2};2-\frac{d}{2}-\ell;r^{2}\right]\Bigr.\\
&\Bigl.\quad\qquad-(1+i\cot\nu\pi)\widehat{K}^{\text{Out}}_{E}(\omega,\nu)\frac{r^{2\nu}}{2\nu}\ {}_{2}F_{1}\left[\frac{2+\ell-i\omega}{2},\frac{d+\ell-2-i\omega}{2};\frac{d}{2}+\ell;r^{2}\right]\Bigr\}\ ,\\
G^{\text{Out}}_{B}&=r^{3-d-\ell}(1+r)^{-i\omega}\\
&\times\Bigl\{{}_{2}F_{1}\left[\frac{1-\ell-i\omega}{2},\frac{3-d-\ell-i\omega}{2};2-\frac{d}{2}-\ell;r^{2}\right]\Bigr.\\
&\Bigl.\quad\qquad-(1+i\cot\nu\pi)\widehat{K}^{\text{Out}}_{B}(\omega,\nu)\frac{r^{2\nu}}{2\nu}\ {}_{2}F_{1}\left[\frac{1+\ell-i\omega}{2},\frac{d+\ell-1-i\omega}{2};\frac{d}{2}+\ell;r^{2}\right]\Bigr\}\ .\end{split}

(412)

Here,
\nu=\ell+\frac{d}{2}-1
as defined in the previous sections. The functions
\widehat{K}^{\text{Out}}
are given by:

\begin{split}\widehat{K}^{\text{Out}}_{E}(\omega,\nu)&=-e^{i\nu\pi}\frac{2\pi i}{\Gamma(\nu)^{2}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{3-\frac{d}{2}+\nu-i\omega}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{-1+\frac{d}{2}+\nu-i\omega}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{3-\frac{d}{2}-\nu-i\omega}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{-1+\frac{d}{2}-\nu-i\omega}{2}\right)}\ ,\\
\widehat{K}^{\text{Out}}_{B}(\omega,\nu)&=-e^{i\nu\pi}\frac{2\pi i}{\Gamma(\nu)^{2}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{2-\frac{d}{2}+\nu-i\omega}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{\frac{d}{2}+\nu-i\omega}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{2-\frac{d}{2}-\nu-i\omega}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{\frac{d}{2}-\nu-i\omega}{2}\right)}\ .\end{split}

(413)

When
d
is odd,
\nu
takes half-integer values and the above expression is well-defined. For even dimensional spacetimes, the above expressions for the propagators should be treated as a limit as
\nu
takes the desired integer value. This will play a role when we obtain renormalised boundary correlators on the worldline next.

Now that we have the fields satisfying the prescribed boundary conditions, we want to understand the
self-force
on the multipole moments due to the radiation, i.e. we would like to ask how the fields cause the multipole moments to dissipate energy. We claim that the radiation reaction is encoded in the boundary behaviour of
renormalised
components of the electric and magnetic fields
\mathcal{E}_{r}
and
\mathcal{H}_{v}
. These field components exert a radial force on a spherical shell current density. One can think of the point source as the zero radius limit of such a shell. In this limit, the radial force naively diverges, but if one focuses on purely dissipative parts of the force, they are regular. One would like to remove the conservative pieces containing all the divergences by systematically subtracting them from the
\mathcal{E}_{r}
and
\mathcal{H}_{v}
. This is accomplished by subtracting from the fields, terms proportional to
\mathcal{E}_{s}
and
\mathcal{H}_{vv}
as follows:

\begin{split}-\lim_{r\to 0}r^{-\ell}\left[r\mathcal{E}_{r}+\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)}{\mathscr{C}_{3-d}}\mathcal{E}_{s}\right]&=\frac{\ell+d-2}{\ell}(1+i\cot\pi\nu)\widehat{K}^{\text{Out}}_{E}\ \mathcal{J}^{E}\ ,\\
-\lim_{r\to 0}r^{-\ell}\left[\mathcal{H}_{v}+\frac{\mathscr{C}_{d-3}}{r}\mathcal{H}_{vv}\right]&=(1+i\cot\pi\nu)\widehat{K}^{\text{Out}}_{B}\ \mathcal{J}^{B}\end{split}

(414)

where the
\mathscr{C}
’s are :

\begin{split}\frac{\mathscr{C}_{d-3}}{1-r^{2}}&\equiv-r\frac{d}{dr}\ln\left\{r^{3-d-\ell}(1-r^{2})^{-\frac{i\omega}{2}}{}_{2}F_{1}\left[\frac{1-\ell-i\omega}{2},\frac{3-d-\ell-i\omega}{2};2-\frac{d}{2}-\ell;r^{2}\right]\right\}\ ,\\
\frac{\mathscr{C}_{3-d}}{1-r^{2}}&\equiv-r\frac{d}{dr}\ln\left\{r^{-\ell}(1-r^{2})^{-\frac{i\omega}{2}}{}_{2}F_{1}\left[\frac{-\ell-i\omega}{2},\frac{4-d-\ell-i\omega}{2};2-\frac{d}{2}-\ell;r^{2}\right]\right\}\ .\end{split}

(415)

The
\mathscr{C}
’s are special cases of the counterterm
\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{N}}
that we obtained in part I for the generic class of scalars. The essential difference here is in the case of the electric sector, where the Debye scalar
\Phi_{E}
satisfies a Neumann boundary condition. We review this counterterming procedure for the same generic class of scalars discussed in part I, now satisfying Neumann boundary conditions, in appendix
D.3
.

The radiation reaction kernel is encoded in the boundary values of the renormalised fields which we will call
K^{\text{Out}}_{E/B}
. For odd values of
d
(half-integer values of
\nu
), they are given by:

\begin{split}K^{\text{Out}}_{E}|_{\text{Odd d}}&=(1+i\cot\pi\nu)\widehat{K}^{\text{Out}}_{E}|_{\text{Odd d}}=-e^{i\nu\pi}\frac{2\pi i}{\Gamma(\nu)^{2}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{3-\frac{d}{2}+\nu-i\omega}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{-1+\frac{d}{2}+\nu-i\omega}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{3-\frac{d}{2}-\nu-i\omega}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{-1+\frac{d}{2}-\nu-i\omega}{2}\right)}\ ,\\
K^{\text{Out}}_{B}|_{\text{Odd d}}&=(1+i\cot\pi\nu)\widehat{K}^{\text{Out}}_{B}|_{\text{Odd d}}=-e^{i\nu\pi}\frac{2\pi i}{\Gamma(\nu)^{2}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{2-\frac{d}{2}+\nu-i\omega}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{\frac{d}{2}+\nu-i\omega}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{2-\frac{d}{2}-\nu-i\omega}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{\frac{d}{2}-\nu-i\omega}{2}\right)}\ .\end{split}

(416)

The table
6
lists explicit expressions for this function in even-dimensional spacetimes up to quadrupole. The
K^{\text{Out}}_{E/B}
for odd values of
d
are polynomials which signify the markovian nature of the electromagnetic radiation reaction. This is the same ‘boundary two-point function’ whose poles are used to analyse the quasinormal mode spectrum of the static patch
[
107
,
108
,
38
]
.

\frac{\omega^{6}}{225}+\frac{7\omega^{4}}{75}+\frac{28\omega^{2}}{75}+\frac{64}{225}

\frac{\omega^{6}}{225}+\frac{7\omega^{4}}{45}+\frac{259\omega^{2}}{225}+1

\frac{\omega^{8}}{11025}+\frac{19\omega^{6}}{3675}+\frac{8\omega^{4}}{105}+\frac{3088\omega^{2}}{11025}+\frac{256}{1225}

On the other hand, for even values of
d
, the
\cot\pi\nu
diverges and one needs additional counterterms to obtain the correct radiation reaction kernel. These can be obtained by adding the following counterterm action to our electromagnetic action:

\begin{split}S_{ct,\text{Even}}&=\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\frac{1}{\nu-n}\int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\Bigg[r^{d-4+2n}\Delta\left(n,\frac{d}{2}-1,\omega\right)\Phi_{B}^{*}\Phi_{B}|_{r_{c}}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad+\ell(\ell+d-2)r^{2-d+2n}\Delta\left(n,\frac{d}{2}-2,\omega\right)|D_{+}\Phi_{E}|^{2}|_{r_{c}}\Bigg]\ ,\end{split}

(417)

where
n=\ell+\frac{d}{2}-1

and,

\begin{split}\Delta(n,\mu,\omega)&\equiv\frac{(-)^{n}}{\Gamma(n)^{2}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{1+n-\mu-i\omega}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1+n+\mu-i\omega}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1-n+\mu-i\omega}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1-n-\mu-i\omega}{2}\right)}=\frac{1}{\Gamma(n)^{2}}\prod\limits_{k=1}^{n}\left[\frac{\omega^{2}}{4}+\frac{1}{4}(\mu-n+2k-1)^{2}\right]\\
&=\Delta^{\ast}(n,\mu,\omega)\ .\end{split}

(418)

With this counterterm, we obtain the following form of the radiation reaction kernel for even-dimensional spacetimes:

\begin{split}K^{\text{Out}}_{E}|_{\text{Even $d$}}&=\Delta_{\mathcal{N}}\left(\nu,\frac{d}{2}-2,\omega\right)\left[\psi^{(0)}\left(\frac{3-\frac{d}{2}+\nu-i\omega}{2}\right)+\psi^{(0)}\left(\frac{-1+\frac{d}{2}\nu-i\omega}{2}\right)\right.\\
&\left.+\psi^{(0)}\left(\frac{3-\frac{d}{2}-\nu-i\omega}{2}\right)+\psi^{(0)}\left(\frac{-1+\frac{d}{2}-\nu-i\omega}{2}\right)-4\psi^{(0)}(\nu)\right]\ ,\\
K^{\text{Out}}_{B}|_{\text{Even $d$}}&=\Delta_{\mathcal{N}}\left(\nu,\frac{d}{2}-1,\omega\right)\left[\psi^{(0)}\left(\frac{2-\frac{d}{2}+\nu-i\omega}{2}\right)+\psi^{(0)}\left(\frac{\frac{d}{2}\nu-i\omega}{2}\right)\right.\\
&\left.+\psi^{(0)}\left(\frac{2-\frac{d}{2}-\nu-i\omega}{2}\right)+\psi^{(0)}\left(\frac{\frac{d}{2}-\nu-i\omega}{2}\right)-4\psi^{(0)}(\nu)\right]\ .\end{split}

(419)

Unlike the case for odd values of
d
, we see that these functions do not reduce to polynomials in
\omega
, which is expected from the non-markovian nature of radiation reaction in odd spacetime dimensions
[
23
]
.

In the limit where the Hubble constant is small, i.e. we are looking at sources moving much more rapidly compared to the cosmological time scales, the radiation reaction kernel reduces to its flat space analogues, which have the same behaviour for both magnetic and electric sectors
[
24
,
25
]
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}K^{\text{Out}}_{E/B}\approx\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{2\pi i}{\Gamma(\nu)^{2}}\left(\frac{\omega}{2}\right)^{2\nu}&\text{for $d$ odd ,}\\
\frac{1}{\Gamma(\nu)^{2}}\left(\frac{\omega}{2}\right)^{2\nu}\ln\left(\frac{\omega^{4}}{H^{4}}\right)&\text{for $d$ even .}\\
\end{array}\right.\end{split}

(420)

The procedure we just illustrated provides us with a dS version of the famous Son-Starinets prescription
[
1
]
in AdS/CFT: in this prescription, the field’s value (
\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{\mu}
in our case) is fixed at the boundary and an outgoing boundary condition is imposed at the horizon. This corresponds to imposing a Dirichlet boundary condition on the
\Phi_{B}
, whereas the
\Phi_{E}
satisfies a Neumann boundary condition at
r=0
. Then one takes the boundary limit of the conjugate field
\mathcal{C}^{r\mu}
and renormalises it to obtain the radiation reaction kernel
K^{\text{Out}}
.

Given this procedure of obtaining the radiation reaction, we will now justify it as an on-shell action computation on the dS-SK geometry described in detail in part I
[
14
]
. This is the de Sitter version of the real-time GKPW prescription: We specify boundary data as the observer’s multipole moments, and the dS-SK saddle computes the effective action of the observer’s dynamics.

We will begin by reviewing some useful geometric details required in our analysis here. We take the de Sitter static patch and complexify the radial coordinate. Then, we consider a hypersurface defined by a contour in the complex
r
plane. The following
mock tortoise coordinate
is useful to make this notion precise:

\zeta(r)=\frac{1}{i\pi}\int\limits^{0-i\epsilon}_{r}\frac{dr^{\prime}}{1-r^{\prime 2}}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\ln\left(\frac{1-r}{1+r}\right)\ .

(421)

This integral has logarithmic branch points at
r=\pm 1
, and we pick the branch cut to run from
r=-1
to
r=1
. If we start from
r=1+i\epsilon
and go around the branch cut to
r=1-i\epsilon
, the
\zeta
coordinate is normalised to go from
1
to
0
in its real part. We define
\zeta=0
as the left boundary and
\zeta=1
as the right boundary. Given this geometry, we will now turn to the question of obtaining the electromagnetic fields on it.

We need ingoing counterparts of the outgoing propagators to define the correct boundary-to-bulk propagator on the dS-SK geometry. We can obtain the ingoing propagator simply by time-reversing the outgoing one:

G^{\text{In}}_{E/B}(r,\omega,\ell)=e^{-2\pi\omega\zeta}G^{\text{Out}\ast}_{E/B}(r,\omega,\ell)\ .

(422)

The boundary-to-bulk propagators on the dS-SK geometry then turn out to be:

\begin{split}g^{E/B}_{L}(r,\omega,\ell)&\equiv n_{\omega}\Bigl[G^{\text{Out}}_{E/B}(r,\omega,\ell)-e^{2\pi\omega(1-\zeta)}G^{\text{Out}\ast}_{E/B}(r,\omega,\ell)\Bigr]\ ,\\
g^{E/B}_{R}(r,\omega,\ell)&\equiv(1+n_{\omega})\Bigl[G^{\text{Out}}_{E/B}(r,\omega,\ell)-e^{-2\pi\omega\zeta}G^{\text{Out}\ast}_{E/B}(r,\omega,\ell)\Bigr]\ .\end{split}

(423)

where
n_{\omega}=\frac{1}{e^{2\pi\omega}-1}
, is the Bose-Einstein factor. Essentially, the
g_{L}
connects the sources on the left boundary to the fields on the dS-SK geometry, whereas
g_{R}
does the same for the sources on the right boundary. These boundary-to-bulk propagators are hence defined so as to satisfy the following boundary conditions:

\begin{split}\lim_{\zeta\to 0}r^{\ell}g^{E}_{L}=-1\ ,&\quad\lim_{\zeta\to 0}r^{\ell}g^{E}_{R}=0\ ,\\
\lim_{\zeta\to 1}r^{\ell}g^{E}_{L}=0\ ,&\quad\lim_{\zeta\to 1}r^{\ell}g^{E}_{R}=1,\\
\lim_{\zeta\to 0}r^{d-3+\ell}g^{B}_{L}=-1\ ,&\quad\lim_{\zeta\to 0}r^{d-3+\ell}g^{B}_{R}=0\ ,\\
\lim_{\zeta\to 1}r^{d-3+\ell}g^{B}_{L}=0\ ,&\quad\lim_{\zeta\to 1}r^{d-3+\ell}g^{B}_{R}=1.\end{split}

(424)

Hence, the
g_{L/R}
is defined to be regular on the right/left boundary and to have a source singularity on the other boundary
39
39

39

In part I, we give a more detailed analysis of these boundary-to-bulk propagators, along with explicit expressions, which the reader can refer to for further details.
.

Using these boundary-to-bulk propagators, we can write down the solutions for
\Phi_{E}
and
\Phi_{B}
as:

\begin{split}\Phi_{E}(\zeta,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})&=\frac{1}{\ell}\left\{g_{R}^{E}(\zeta,\omega,\ell)\mathcal{J}^{E}_{R}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})-g_{L}^{E}(\zeta,\omega,\ell)\mathcal{J}^{E}_{L}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\right\},\\
\Phi_{B}(\zeta,\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})&=g_{R}^{B}(\zeta,\omega,\ell)\mathcal{J}^{B}_{R}(\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})-g_{L}^{B}(\zeta,\omega,\ell)\mathcal{J}^{B}_{L}(\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})\ ;\end{split}

(425)

where
\ \mathcal{J}^{E/B}_{L}
and
\ \mathcal{J}_{R}^{E/B}
are the electric/magnetic multipole moments on the left and the right boundary, respectively. The
\Phi_{E}
satisfies Neumann boundary conditions, whereas
\Phi_{B}
satisfies Dirichlet boundary conditions at both the
R
and
L
boundaries of the dS-SK geometry. Correspondingly, we can also write the conjugate fields to
\pi_{E/B}
in the following manner:

\begin{split}\pi_{E}(\zeta,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})&=\frac{1}{\ell}\left\{\pi_{L}^{E}(\zeta,\omega,\ell)\mathcal{J}^{E}_{L}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})-\pi_{R}^{E}(\zeta,\omega,\ell)\mathcal{J}^{E}_{R}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\right\},\\
\pi_{B}(\zeta,\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})&=\pi_{L}^{B}(\zeta,\omega,\ell)\mathcal{J}^{B}_{L}(\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})-\pi_{R}^{B}(r,\omega,\ell)\mathcal{J}^{B}_{R}(\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})\end{split}

(426)

where we have defined
\pi_{L/R}^{E/B}=D_{+}g_{L/R}^{E/B}
. These fields are fixed such that they satisfy the following boundary conditions:

\begin{split}\lim_{\zeta\to 0}r^{\ell+d-2}\pi_{E}=\mathcal{J}^{E}_{L}\ ,&\qquad\lim_{\zeta\to 1}r^{\ell+d-2}\pi_{E}=\mathcal{J}^{E}_{R}\ ,\\
\lim_{\zeta\to 0}r^{\ell+d-3}\Phi_{B}=\mathcal{J}^{B}_{L}\ ,&\qquad\lim_{\zeta\to 1}r^{\ell+d-3}\Phi_{B}=\mathcal{J}^{B}_{R}\ .\end{split}

(427)

Given the solutions with appropriate boundary conditions, one can now substitute them into the action to obtain the boundary Schwinger-Keldysh action:

\begin{split}S&=-\frac{1}{4}\int_{dSSK}d^{d+1}x\sqrt{-g}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}^{\mu\nu}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{\mu\nu}+S_{ct}\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\int_{dSSK}d^{d+1}x\left[\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}\left(\sqrt{-g}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}^{\mu\nu}\right)-\partial_{\mu}\left(\sqrt{-g}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}^{\mu\nu}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{\nu}\right)\right]+S_{ct}\\
&\underset{\text{(On-Shell)}}{=}\left[-\frac{1}{2}\int r^{d-1}dt\ d\Omega_{d-1}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}^{r\mu}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{\mu}\right]^{r_{c}-i\epsilon}_{r_{c}+i\epsilon}+S_{ct}\ .\end{split}

(428)

Here, the first term on the second line can be set to zero using the equations of motion, and the second one evaluates to a boundary term. We can compute this boundary term using the dS-SK solutions:

\begin{split}\lim_{\zeta\to 1}r^{d-1}\mathcal{C}^{ru}_{\text{ren}}\mathcal{V}_{u}^{*}&=\lim_{\zeta\to 1}\frac{1}{r^{\ell-1}}\mathcal{C}^{ru}_{\text{ren}}\times\lim_{\zeta\to 1}r^{\ell+d-2}\mathcal{V}^{*}_{u}\\
&=-\frac{(\ell+d-2)}{\ell}\mathcal{J}^{E\ast}_{R}\left\{K^{E}_{LR}\mathcal{J}^{E}_{R}-K^{E}_{LL}\mathcal{J}^{E}_{L}\right\}\ ,\\
\lim_{\zeta\to 0}r^{d-1}\mathcal{C}^{ru}_{\text{ren}}\mathcal{V}_{u}^{*}&=\lim_{\zeta\to 0}\frac{1}{r^{\ell-1}}\mathcal{C}^{ru}_{\text{ren}}\times\lim_{\zeta\to 0}r^{\ell+d-2}\mathcal{V}^{*}_{u}\\
&=-\frac{(\ell+d-2)}{\ell}\mathcal{J}^{E\ast}_{L}\left\{K^{E}_{RR}\mathcal{J}^{E}_{R}-K^{E}_{RL}\mathcal{J}^{E}_{L}\right\}\ ,\\
\lim_{\zeta\to 1}r^{d-1}\mathcal{C}^{rI}_{\text{ren}}\mathcal{V}_{I}^{*}&=\lim_{\zeta\to 1}\frac{1}{r^{\ell-2}}\mathcal{C}^{rI}_{\text{ren}}\times\lim_{\zeta\to 1}r^{\ell+d-3}\mathcal{V}^{*}_{I}\\
&=-\mathcal{J}^{E\ast}_{R}\left\{K^{B}_{LR}\mathcal{J}^{B}_{R}-K^{B}_{LL}\mathcal{J}^{B}_{L}\right\}\ ,\\
\lim_{\zeta\to 0}r^{d-1}\mathcal{C}^{ru}_{\text{ren}}\mathcal{V}_{u}^{*}&=\lim_{\zeta\to 0}\frac{1}{r^{\ell-2}}\mathcal{C}^{ru}_{\text{ren}}\times\lim_{\zeta\to 0}r^{\ell+d-3}\mathcal{V}^{*}_{u}\\
&=-\mathcal{J}^{B\ast}_{L}\left\{K^{B}_{RR}\mathcal{J}^{B}_{R}-K^{B}_{RL}\mathcal{J}^{B}_{L}\right\}\ .\end{split}

(429)

We have defined these combinations of the wordline two-point functions:

\begin{split}K^{E/B}_{LL}\equiv n_{\omega}K^{\text{Out}}_{E/B}-(1+n_{\omega})K^{\text{Out}\ast}_{E/B}\ ,&\quad K^{E/B}_{LR}\equiv(1+n_{\omega})\Bigl(K^{\text{Out}}_{E/B}-K^{\text{Out}\ast}_{E/B}\Bigr)\ ,\\
K^{E/B}_{RL}\equiv n_{\omega}\Bigl(K^{\text{Out}}_{E/B}-K^{\text{Out}\ast}_{E/B}\Bigr)\ ,&\quad K^{E/B}_{RR}\equiv(1+n_{\omega})K^{\text{Out}}_{E/B}-n_{\omega}K^{\text{Out}\ast}_{E/B}\ .\end{split}

(430)

Given the above expressions on the boundaries of dS-SK, we can write down the on-shell action as follows:

\begin{split}S_{\text{CIP}}&=-\sum_{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}\int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}K^{\text{Out}}_{B}(\omega,\ell)\ \mathcal{J}_{D}^{B*}\ \Bigl[\mathcal{J}^{B}_{A}+\left(n_{\omega}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\mathcal{J}_{D}^{B}\Bigr]\\
&\quad-\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\frac{(\ell+d-2)}{\ell}\int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}K^{\text{Out}}_{E}(\omega,\ell)\ \mathcal{J}_{D}^{E*}\ \Bigl[\mathcal{J}_{A}^{E}+\left(n_{\omega}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\mathcal{J}_{D}^{E}\Bigr]\ ,\end{split}

(431)

where we have defined the average and difference combinations of the source multipole moments:

\mathcal{J}^{E/B}_{A}\equiv\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{J}^{E/B}_{R}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{J}^{E/B}_{L}\ ,\qquad\mathcal{J}^{E/B}_{D}\equiv\mathcal{J}^{E/B}_{R}-\mathcal{J}^{E/B}_{L}\ .

(432)

This is otherwise known as the Keldysh basis, which is convenient for extracting the physics from these expressions. The average-difference terms capture the dissipative piece: they encode the physics, as we will see in the next section, of the Abraham-Lorentz-Dirac force in dS. The difference-difference term encodes the Hawking fluctuations. This can be seen through a Hubbard-Stratonovich transformation of the difference moments, which will induce a noise field whose fluctuations are controlled by the Hawking temperature
40
40

40

See part I
[
14
]
for a proper derivation of the fluctuating field in the long time limit.
. The thermality of the correlators is encoded in the fact that the fluctuations are proportional to the dissipation, as can be seen from our action.

Lastly, we will show how the
S_{ct}
can be written gauge-invariantly. The counterterm lagrangian can be written in a gauge-invariant manner in the following way:

\begin{split}S_{ct}&=\left[\frac{1}{2}\int r^{d-1}dt\ d\Omega_{d-1}\frac{r}{\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{N}=3-d}}\left\{(\mathscr{D}^{I}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{uI})\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{u}\right\}\right.\\
&\left.-\frac{1}{2}\int r^{d-1}dt\ d\Omega_{d-1}\left\{\frac{\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{N}=d-3}-\frac{r^{2}\partial_{u}^{2}}{\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{N}=3-d}}}{(\ell+1)(\ell+d-3)}r\mathscr{D}_{J}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}^{IJ}+\frac{r}{\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{N}=3-d}}\partial_{u}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{u}^{\ I}\right\}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{I}\right]^{r_{c}-i\epsilon}_{r_{c}+i\epsilon}\\
&=\left[\frac{1}{2}\int r^{d-1}dt\ d\Omega_{d-1}\frac{r}{\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{N}=3-d}}\left\{(\mathscr{D}^{I}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{uI})\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{u}-(\partial_{u}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{u}^{\ I})\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{I}+\frac{r^{2}}{(\ell+1)(\ell+d-3)}(\partial_{u}^{2}\mathscr{D}_{J}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}^{IJ})\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{I}\right\}\right.\\
&\left.-\frac{1}{2}\int r^{d-1}dt\ d\Omega_{d-1}\frac{r\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{N}=d-3}}{(\ell+1)(\ell+d-3)}(\mathscr{D}_{J}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}^{IJ})\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{I}\right]^{r_{c}-i\epsilon}_{r_{c}+i\epsilon}\\
&=\left[\frac{1}{2}\int r^{d-1}dt\ d\Omega_{d-1}\frac{r}{\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{N}=3-d}}\left\{\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{uI}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{u}^{\ I}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{r^{2}}{(\ell+1)(\ell+d-3)}(\partial_{u}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}^{IJ})(\partial_{u}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{IJ})\right\}\right.\\
&\left.-\frac{1}{4}\int r^{d-1}dt\ d\Omega_{d-1}\frac{r\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{N}=d-3}}{(\ell+1)(\ell+d-3)}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}^{IJ}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{IJ}\right]^{r_{c}-i\epsilon}_{r_{c}+i\epsilon}\ .\end{split}

(433)

As we can see, the counterterm action is local in time and gauge invariant. This concludes our analysis of constructing a regularised effective action for a point source observer.

D.1
Energy flux through the horizon

The Electromagnetic stress tensor, in our notation, is given by:

\overline{T}_{\text{EM}}^{\mu\nu}=\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}^{\mu\alpha}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}^{\nu}_{\ \alpha}-\frac{1}{4}g^{\mu\nu}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{\alpha\beta}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}^{\alpha\beta}\ .

(434)

We want to calculate the electromagnetic energy flux that exits through the horizon. This is encoded in the
T_{u}^{r}
component of the stress tensor, which we integrate over the sphere to obtain the total flux:

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{r}}r^{d-1}(\overline{T}_{\text{EM}})_{u}^{\ r}&=-\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}r^{d-3}\left[\sum_{\alpha}\mathcal{E}_{v}^{*}\mathcal{H}_{v}+\ell(\ell+d-2)\mathcal{H}_{s}^{*}\mathcal{E}_{s}\right]\\
&=\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}i\omega\left[\sum_{\alpha}r^{d-3}\ \Phi_{B}^{*}D_{+}\Phi_{B}+r^{3-d}\ \ell(\ell+d-2)\Phi_{E}^{*}D_{+}\Phi_{E}\right]\ .\end{split}

(435)

Here, we have expressed the stress tensor in terms of the Debye scalars.
In
[
106
]
, the authors compute using covariant phase space formalism, the flux through
\mathscr{I}^{+}
given by:

\int_{\mathscr{I}^{+}}dV_{\mathscr{I}^{+}}\ g^{\mu\nu}\left[\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{u\mu}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{u\nu}-(1-r^{2})\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{u\mu}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{C}}$}}_{r\nu}\right]\ ,

(436)

which matches our expression for the flux through a constant
r
slice.

The boundary to bulk retarded Green’s functions in de Sitter static patch for the Debye scalars are:

\begin{split}G^{\text{Out}}_{E}(r,\omega,\ell)&=H^{2\nu}r^{\ell+d-2}(1+Hr)^{-\frac{i\omega}{H}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{\ell+2-\frac{i\omega}{H}}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{\ell+d-2-\frac{i\omega}{H}}{2}\right)}{\Gamma(1-\frac{i\omega}{H})\Gamma\left(\ell+\frac{d}{2}-1\right)}\\
&\qquad\times\ {}_{2}F_{1}\left[\frac{\ell+2-\frac{i\omega}{H}}{2},\frac{\ell+d-2-\frac{i\omega}{H}}{2};1-\frac{i\omega}{H};1-r^{2}H^{2}\right]\ ,\\
G^{\text{Out}}_{B}(r,\omega,\ell)&=H^{2\nu}r^{\ell+1}(1+Hr)^{-\frac{i\omega}{H}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{\ell+1-\frac{i\omega}{H}}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{\ell+d-1-\frac{i\omega}{H}}{2}\right)}{\Gamma(1-\frac{i\omega}{H})\Gamma\left(\ell+\frac{d}{2}-1\right)}\\
&\qquad\times\ {}_{2}F_{1}\left[\frac{\ell+1-\frac{i\omega}{H}}{2},\frac{\ell+d-1-\frac{i\omega}{H}}{2};1-\frac{i\omega}{H};1-H^{2}r^{2}\right]\ .\end{split}

(437)

This form of writing the propagators allows us to easily read off the horizon behaviour as the hypergeometric function goes to 1. We have:

\begin{split}\lim_{r\to H^{-1}}G^{\text{Out}}_{E}&=2^{-\frac{i\omega}{H}}H^{\ell}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{\ell+2-\frac{i\omega}{H}}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{\ell+d-2-\frac{i\omega}{H}}{2}\right)}{\Gamma(1-\frac{i\omega}{H})\Gamma\left(\ell+\frac{d}{2}-1\right)}\equiv H^{3-d}\mathfrak{f}_{E}(\omega)\ ,\\
\lim_{r\to H^{-1}}G^{\text{Out}}_{B}&=2^{-\frac{i\omega}{H}}H^{\ell+d-3}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{\ell+1-\frac{i\omega}{H}}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{\ell+d-1-\frac{i\omega}{H}}{2}\right)}{\Gamma(1-\frac{i\omega}{H})\Gamma\left(\ell+\frac{d}{2}-1\right)}\equiv\mathfrak{f}_{B}(\omega)\ .\end{split}

(438)

where we have defined the
\mathfrak{f}_{E}
to have the same mass dimension as
\mathfrak{f}_{B}
.
Similarly, we can also obtain the following:

\begin{split}\lim_{r\to H^{-1}}D_{+}G^{\text{Out}}_{E}&=i\omega H^{3-d}\ \mathfrak{f}_{E}\ ,\\
\lim_{r\to H^{-1}}D_{+}G^{\text{Out}}_{B}&=i\omega\ \mathfrak{f}_{B}\ .\end{split}

(439)

To find the flux of outgoing radiation, we simply substitute the outgoing solutions evaluated at the horizon. Substituting this relation back into (
435
) evaluated at the horizon, we have:

\begin{split}\lim_{r\to H^{-1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{r}}r^{d-1}(\overline{T}_{\text{EM}})_{u}^{\ r}&=-\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}i\omega\left[\sum_{\alpha}K^{\text{Out}}_{B}|\mathcal{J}^{B}|^{2}+\frac{\ell+d-2}{\ell}K^{\text{Out}}_{E}|\mathcal{J}^{E}|^{2}\right]\ .\end{split}

(440)

From the above expressions, we can obtain the behaviour of the electromagnetic fields at the horizon:

\begin{split}\lim_{r\to H^{-1}}\mathcal{E}_{r}=(\ell+d-2)H^{2}\ \mathfrak{f}_{E}\ \mathcal{J}^{E}\ ,\qquad\lim_{r\to H^{-1}}\mathcal{E}_{s}&=i\omega\ \mathfrak{f}_{E}\frac{\mathcal{J}^{E}}{\ell}\ ,\qquad\lim_{r\to H^{-1}}\mathcal{H}_{s}=i\omega\ \mathfrak{f}_{E}\frac{\mathcal{J}^{E}}{\ell}\ ,\\
\lim_{r\to H^{-1}}\mathcal{H}_{vv}=\mathfrak{f}_{B}\ \mathcal{J}^{B}\ ,\qquad\lim_{r\to H^{-1}}\mathcal{E}_{v}&=i\omega\ \mathfrak{f}_{B}\ \mathcal{J}^{B}\ ,\qquad\lim_{r\to H^{-1}}\mathcal{H}_{v}=i\omega\ \mathfrak{f}_{B}\ \mathcal{J}^{B}\ .\end{split}

(441)

We can see that these expressions reproduce the flat space expressions as
H\to 0
with
r
set to
\frac{1}{H}
. To see this, we give the flat space limits of the
\mathfrak{f}_{E/B}
(for odd values of
d
):

\begin{split}\lim_{H\to 0}H^{\frac{3-d}{2}}\mathfrak{f}_{E}=\lim_{H\to 0}H^{\frac{3-d}{2}}\mathfrak{f}_{B}=\frac{(-i\omega)^{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}}{(2\nu-2)!!}\ .\end{split}

(442)

For generic values of
d
we have:

\begin{split}\lim_{H\to 0}H^{\frac{3-d}{2}}\mathfrak{f}_{E}=\lim_{H\to 0}H^{\frac{3-d}{2}}\mathfrak{f}_{B}=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{\Gamma\left(\nu\right)}\left(-\frac{i\omega}{2}\right)^{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}\ .\end{split}

(443)

D.2
dS-Bessel Polynomials

In this section, we will generalise the flat space reverse Bessel polynomials
(
109
,
18.34
)
, obtained in the study of outgoing radiation in
3+1
dimensions, to a generic class of scalar fields in all even-dimensional de Sitter spacetimes. In part I, we introduced a system of ‘designer scalars’ that are governed by the action:

S=-\frac{1}{2}\int d^{d+1}x\sqrt{-g}\ r^{\mathcal{N}+1-d}\left\{(\partial\Phi_{\mathcal{N}})^{2}+\frac{\Phi_{\mathcal{N}}^{2}}{4r^{2}}\left[(d+\mathcal{N}-3)(d-\mathcal{N}-1)-r^{2}\left(4\mu^{2}-(\mathcal{N}+1)^{2}\right)\right]\right\}\ .

(444)

where the
\mathcal{N}
and
\mu
parametrise the various scalar fields. The centrifugal and mass terms are chosen in the action such that for appropriate values of
\mathcal{N}
and
\mu
, one can obtain various scalar fields that are obtained in the study of massive Klein-Gordon fields, electromagnetism, and linearised gravity.

In even dimensional de Sitter spacetimes, for massless fields, i.e., when
4\mu^{2}=(\mathcal{N}+1)^{2}
, one finds that the outgoing boundary to bulk propagator
G^{\text{Out}}_{\mathcal{N}}
can be written in polynomials in
\omega r
and
rH
. These polynomials are the de Sitter analogues of the reverse Bessel polynomials generalised to
d+1
dimensions and reproduce them in the zero curvature limit. To make this explicit, we will write the designer scalar EOM as follows:

\frac{1}{r^{1-2\nu}}D_{+}\left(r^{1-2\nu}D_{+}\psi_{\mathcal{N}}\right)+\omega^{2}\psi_{\mathcal{N}}+H^{2}(1-r^{2}H^{2})\left[\mu^{2}-(\nu-1)^{2}\right]\psi_{\mathcal{N}}=0\ .

(445)

where we have scaled the designer scalar with a power of
r
for convenience:

\Phi_{\mathcal{N}}(r,\omega,\ell)=r^{\frac{1-\mathcal{N}}{2}-\nu}\psi_{\mathcal{N}}(r,\omega,\ell)\ .

(446)

We remind the reader that
\nu=\ell+\frac{d}{2}-1
.
One can write the solution for this equation in the following form:

\psi_{\mathcal{N}}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(r^{2}H^{2})^{n}\frac{\left(\frac{1-\mu-\nu}{2}\right)_{n}\left(\frac{1+\mu-\nu}{2}\right)_{n}}{\Gamma(n+1)\left(1-\nu\right)_{n}}\Theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}-n}(z)\ .

(447)

Here,

(a)_{n}=\prod\limits_{k=0}^{k=n-1}(a-k)\ ,

(448)

is the falling factorial and
\Theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}
satisfies the
\mu
independent equation of motion:

\frac{1}{r^{1-2\nu}}D_{+}\left(r^{1-2\nu}D_{+}\Theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}\right)+\omega^{2}\Theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}=0\ .

(449)

Although this differential equation can be solved in terms of Hypegeometric functions, that form is not particularly illuminating to extract out the polynomial nature of the propagator. Instead, we will express the solutions in a Hubble expansion, which makes the polynomial nature explicit.

Table 7:
\Theta_{\nu-\frac{1}{2}}
for various values of
\nu
(
H=1
)

+r^{8}z\left(1155+945z\right)+945r^{10}z

Taking
z\equiv-i\omega r
, we can write the Hubble expanded solutions as:

\begin{split}&\Theta_{\ell}(z,Hr)=\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\frac{z^{\ell-k}}{2^{k}k!}\frac{(\ell+k)!}{(\ell-k)!}\\
&\quad+\frac{H^{2}r^{2}}{2!}\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-1}(\ell-1-k)\frac{z^{\ell-1-k}}{2^{k}k!}\frac{(\ell-1+k)!}{(\ell-1-k)!}\Bigl\{\ell+\frac{1}{3}k\Bigr\}\\
&\quad+\frac{H^{4}r^{4}}{4!}\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-2}(\ell-2-k)\frac{z^{\ell-2-k}}{2^{k}k!}\frac{(\ell-2+k)!}{(\ell-2-k)!}\Bigl\{\ell(\ell-1)(3\ell+3-k)-\frac{1}{15}k(k-1)(25\ell+5k-3)\Bigr\}\\
&\quad+\frac{H^{6}r^{6}}{6!}\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-3}(\ell-3-k)\frac{z^{\ell-3-k}}{2^{k}k!}\frac{(\ell-3+k)!}{(\ell-3-k)!}\\
&\qquad\times\Bigl\{15\ell(\ell^{2}-1)(\ell^{2}-4)+k\ell(\ell-1)[(42+25\ell-15\ell^{2})-(12+10\ell)k]\Bigr.\\
&\qquad\Bigl.\qquad+\frac{1}{63}k(k-1)(k-2)[-2-441\ell+350\ell^{2}+7(-9+35\ell)k+35k^{2}]\Bigr\}\\
&\quad+O(H^{8})\ .\end{split}

(450)

We give explicit expressions for the retarded boundary to bulk propagators in tables
8
,

,

and
11
.
