D.3
Regularisation for Neumann scalars

This section gives a procedure for obtaining the boundary 2-point function
K_{\text{Out}}
for the designer scalar with the scalar satisfying Neumann boundary conditions at the
r=0
boundary. The action (
444
) describes the dynamics of the designer scalar field with two parameters
\mathcal{N}
and
\mu
. For the specific case of the electric Debye potential, for which we use the results of this section, these parameters take the values
\mathcal{N}=d-3
and
\mu=\frac{d}{2}-2
.

The conjugate field for radial evolution for
\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}
is given by
\pi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}=-r^{\mathcal{N}}D_{+}\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}
, where we have defined
D_{+}\equiv(1-r^{2})\partial_{r}+i\omega
and
D_{-}\equiv(1-r^{2})\partial_{r}-i\omega
. For a Neumann boundary condition, we will fix the value of
\pi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}
to some source multipole moment. The boundary 2-pt function is then specified by the behaviour of the
\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}
at
r\to 0
. Naively, this limit yields a divergence similar to the divergence of the Coulomb field of a point charge at
r=0
. We would like to regulate this divergence by the usual QFT technique of adding appropriate counterterms to our action. To this end, let us first look at the divergent behaviour of the
\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}
as we take the limit
r\to 0
.

We require
\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}
to satisfy outgoing boundary conditions at the horizon, which is equivalent to demanding analyticity at
r=1
in the outgoing Eddington-Finkelstein coordinates. The Neumann boundary condition at
r=0
is imposed as:

\lim_{r\to 0}r^{\nu+\frac{\mathcal{N}+1}{2}}\left\{-r^{\mathcal{N}}D_{+}\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}\right\}=\mathcal{J}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\omega)\ .

(451)

Given these boundary condition,
\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}
can be written as:

\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}=\frac{G^{\text{Out}}_{\mathcal{N}}}{\nu+\frac{\mathcal{N}-1}{2}}\mathcal{J}_{\ell\vec{m}}\ ,

(452)

where
G^{\text{Out}}_{\mathcal{N}}
is given by:

\begin{split}G^{\text{Out}}_{\mathcal{N}}&=r^{-\nu-\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{N}-1)}(1+r)^{-i\omega}\\
&\times\Bigl\{{}_{2}F_{1}\left[\frac{1-\nu+\mu-i\omega}{2},\frac{1-\nu-\mu-i\omega}{2};1-\nu;r^{2}\right]\Bigr.\\
&\Bigl.\quad\qquad-(1+i\cot\nu\pi)\widehat{K}_{\text{Out}}\frac{r^{2\nu}}{2\nu}\ {}_{2}F_{1}\left[\frac{1+\nu-\mu-i\omega}{2},\frac{1+\nu+\mu-i\omega}{2};1+\nu;r^{2}\right]\Bigr\}\ .\end{split}

(453)

As one can see from the above formula,
\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}
has a term with leading behaviour of
r^{-\nu-\frac{\mathcal{N}-1}{2}}
as
r\to 0
that diverges and needs to be countertermed away.

The renormalised field (
\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}\text{ren}}
) is given by:

\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}\text{ren}}=\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}+\frac{r}{\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{N}}}D_{+}\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}\ ,

(454)

where
\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{N}}
is the same function that appears in the counterterming of the conjugate field in the corresponding Dirichlet problem (see part I). In particular,

\begin{split}\frac{\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{N}}}{1-r^{2}}&\equiv-r\frac{d}{dr}\ln\left\{r^{-\nu-\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{N}-1)}(1-r^{2})^{-\frac{i\omega}{2}}{}_{2}F_{1}\left[\frac{1-\nu+\mu-i\omega}{2},\frac{1-\nu-\mu-i\omega}{2};1-\nu;r^{2}\right]\right\}\ .\end{split}

(455)

In part I, we showed that
\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{N}}
is an even function in
\omega
and has a well-behaved small
r
expansion. Let us see how
\frac{1}{\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{N}}}
behaves at small
r
:

\frac{1}{\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{N}}}=\frac{2}{2\nu+\mathcal{N}-1}\left\{1+r^{2}-\frac{(\nu-1)^{2}-\mu^{2}-\omega^{2}}{(\nu-1)(2\nu+\mathcal{N}-1)}r^{2}+\dots\right\}\ .

(456)

Even in this case, the counterterm is local in time, which can be verified by further expanding the above function. Given this definition of the renormalised field, its boundary behaviour becomes:

\lim_{r\to 0}r^{-\nu+\frac{\mathcal{N}-1}{2}}\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}\text{ren}}=-\frac{1+i\cot\nu\pi}{\left(\nu+\frac{\mathcal{N}-1}{2}\right)^{2}}\widehat{K}_{\text{Out}}

(457)

Renormalising the
\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}
in this manner is equivalent to adding the following counterterm to the action:

\begin{split}S_{ct}&=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}r^{\mathcal{N}+1}\frac{1}{\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{N}}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})}(D_{+}\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}})^{*}D_{+}\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}|_{\text{Bnd}}\ .\end{split}

(458)

For the case of even
d
, one needs an additional counterterm to make the action finite. This counterterm is the same as the one required in the Dirichlet case:

\begin{split}S_{ct,\text{Even}}&=\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\frac{1}{\nu-n}\int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}r^{\mathcal{N}-1+2n}\Delta_{\mathcal{N}}(n,\mu,\omega)\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}^{*}\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}|_{\text{Bnd}}\ .\end{split}

(459)

Appendix E
Extended EM sources in dS and radiation reaction

In this section, we want to describe in detail the results about extended EM sources in de Sitter that were alluded to in the main text. Our goal here is twofold: first, we want to describe radiative multipole expansion in de Sitter with correct normalisations for multipole moments, etc., which in the
H\to 0
limit reproduces the flat space analysis. The second goal is to compute the analogue of ALD force in de Sitter (they also have a

H\to 0
limit). One main difference to our flat spacetime analysis is the following: the analysis in this section uses retarded time
u
instead of the Schwarzschild time
t
.

Magnetic Multipole Radiation

We will begin by describing the magnetic multipole radiation due to toroidal currents in dS
d+1
. These currents are identically conserved; hence, conservation equations play no role in this sector, making the analysis conceptually simpler. One begins with decomposing the currents in terms of vector spherical harmonics on the sphere.

\begin{split}\bar{J}^{u}(r,u,\hat{r})=\bar{J}^{r}(r,u,\hat{r})=0\ ,\quad\bar{J}^{I}(r,u,\hat{r})&=\sum_{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}J_{V}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}(r,\omega)\mathbb{V}^{I}_{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ .\end{split}

(460)

As in the flat space as well as the point source analysis, we will use a convenient parameterization of the gauge field in terms of the magnetic Debye scalar:

\begin{split}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{u}(r,u,\hat{r})=\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{r}(r,u,\hat{r})=0\ ,\quad\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{I}(r,u,\hat{r})=\sum_{\alpha,\ell,\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}e^{-i\omega u}\Phi_{B}(r,\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})\ \mathbb{V}_{I}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ .\end{split}

(461)

We will remind the reader that due to the orthogonality of the VSH with
\mathscr{D}_{I}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}
, the electromagnetic fields due to toroidal currents completely decouple from those due to charge densities and poloidal currents. Hence, we can proceed with independently analysing the effects of toroidal current distributions.

Given the above parametrisation of the sources and the gauge fields, the electromagnetic field equations reduce to the following inhomogeneous equation for the magnetic Debye potential:

\begin{split}\frac{1}{r^{d-3}}D_{+}\left[r^{d-3}D_{+}\Phi_{B}\right]+\omega^{2}\Phi_{B}-&\frac{(\ell+1)(\ell+d-3)(1-r^{2})}{r^{2}}\Phi_{B}+r^{2}(1-r^{2})J_{V}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}=0\ .\end{split}

(462)

We will construct a Green function for solving the above inhomogeneous differential equation, such that it satisfies the following equation:

\begin{split}\frac{1}{r^{d-3}}D_{+}\left[r^{d-3}D_{+}\mathbb{G}_{B}(r,r_{0};\ell)\right]+\omega^{2}\mathbb{G}_{B}(r,r_{0};\ell)-&\frac{(\ell+1)(\ell+d-3)(1-r^{2})}{r^{2}}\mathbb{G}_{B}(r,r_{0};\ell)\\
&\qquad+(1-r^{2})\frac{\delta(r-r_{0})}{r^{d-3}}=0\ .\end{split}

(463)

One interprets this green function as the field generated by a single shell of unit toroidal current placed at
r=r_{0}
. The appropriate boundary conditions for this problem are that the field should be outgoing at the horizon, i.e. at
r=1
and that it should be finite at
r=0
. The field is also required to satisfy the correct jump condition at the sphere obtained by integrating the above equation about
r=r_{0}
:

r^{d-3}D_{+}\mathbb{G}_{B}\Big|^{r_{0}-}_{r_{0}+}=1\ .

(464)

We have already analysed the homogeneous solution to this equation that satisfies the outgoing boundary condition:
G^{\text{Out}}_{B}
, which dictates the field outside the sphere. We also need a homogeneous solution normalisable at the origin to obtain the appropriate Green function for the inhomogeneous solution. This normalisable solution is given by:

\begin{split}\left(\frac{1-r}{1+r}\right)^{\frac{i\omega}{2}}\Xi^{B}_{n}(r,\omega,\ell)&\equiv\frac{1}{2\ell+d-2}r^{\ell+1}(1+r)^{-i\omega}{}_{2}F_{1}\left[\frac{\ell+1-i\omega}{2},\frac{\ell+d-1-i\omega}{2};\ell+\frac{d}{2};r^{2}\right]\ .\end{split}

(465)

The function
\Xi^{B}_{n}(r,\omega,\ell)
is the corresponding normalisable solution in the Schwarzschild time
t
[
38
]
.
\Xi^{B}_{n}(r,\omega,\ell)
reproduces the flat space normalisable solution (the Bessel function) (
185
) in the
H\to 0
limit(see appendix
E
). Given these solutions to the homogeneous equation satisfying appropriate boundary conditions, we can construct the Green function for the above inhomogeneous equation such that the solution takes the form:

\Phi_{B}(r,\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})=\int dr_{0}r_{0}^{d-1}\mathbb{G}_{B}(r,r_{0};\omega,\ell)J_{V}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}(r_{0},\omega)

(466)

Imposing these boundary conditions and the appropriate jump condition at
r=r_{0}
, we can write the form of the Green function as follows:

\mathbb{G}_{B}(r,r_{0};\omega,\ell)=\frac{1}{W_{B}(r_{0},\omega,\ell)}\left(\frac{1-r_{<}}{1+r_{<}}\right)^{\frac{i\omega}{2}}\Xi^{B}_{n}(r_{<},\omega,\ell)G^{\text{Out}}_{B}(r_{>},\omega,\ell)\ ,

(467)

where the Wronskian
W_{B}(r_{0},\omega,\ell)
is given by:

W_{B}(r_{0},\omega,\ell)=\left(\frac{1-r_{0}}{1+r_{0}}\right)^{i\omega}

(468)

and

\begin{split}r_{>}\equiv\text{Max}(r,r_{0})\ ,\quad r_{<}\equiv\text{Min}(r,r_{0})\ .\end{split}

(469)

We have now solved for the Debye potential
\Phi_{B}
given a poloidal current distribution. Given the potential, we can now use (
406
) to obtain the solutions for the fields:

\begin{split}\mathcal{E}_{v}&=i\omega\ \Phi_{B}=\int_{\vec{r}_{0}}i\omega\ \mathbb{G}_{B}(r,r_{0};\omega,\ell)\mathbb{V}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}_{I}(\hat{r}_{0})J^{I}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)\ ,\\
\mathcal{H}_{v}&=D_{+}\Phi_{B}=\int_{\vec{r}_{0}}D_{+}\mathbb{G}_{B}(r,r_{0};\omega,\ell)\mathbb{V}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}_{I}(\hat{r}_{0})J^{I}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)\ ,\\
\mathcal{H}_{vv}&=\Phi_{B}=\int_{\vec{r}_{0}}\mathbb{G}_{B}(r,r_{0};\omega,\ell)\mathbb{V}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}_{I}(\hat{r}_{0})J^{I}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)\ .\end{split}

(470)

These equations are analogous to the equations (
208
) in flat space. As pointed out in the flat space analysis, the time-dependent toroidal currents give rise not only to a magnetic field but also to an induced electric field. Given the expressions of the fields, we can now describe the fields outside the sources. We identify the magnetic multipole moment to be:

\begin{split}\mathcal{J}^{B}(\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})&=\int_{\vec{r}_{0}}\left(\frac{1-r_{0}}{1+r_{0}}\right)^{-\frac{i\omega}{2}}\Xi^{B}_{n}(r_{0},\omega,\ell)\mathbb{V}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}_{I}(\hat{r}_{0})J^{I}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)\\
&=\frac{1}{2\ell+d-2}\int_{\vec{r}_{0}}r_{0}^{\ell+1}(1-r_{0})^{-i\omega}\\
&\quad\times{}_{2}F_{1}\left[\frac{\ell+1-i\omega}{2},\frac{\ell+d-1-i\omega}{2};\ell+\frac{d}{2};r_{0}^{2}\right]\mathbb{V}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}_{I}(\hat{r}_{0})J^{I}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)\ .\end{split}

(471)

We can rewrite the multipole moment in Schwarzschild time to obtain a formula that can be compared directly with the corresponding flat space expression(
209
). For this, we need to convert our expressions in the Fourier transform of the outgoing time to expressions in terms of Schwarzschild time
t
. This can be achieved by first recognising that:

\begin{split}J^{I}(\vec{r},\omega)=\int du\ e^{i\omega u}\bar{J}^{I}(\vec{r},t)=\int dt\ e^{i\omega t}\left(\frac{1-r}{1+r}\right)^{\frac{i\omega}{2}}\bar{J}^{I}(\vec{r},t)\ .\end{split}

(472)

This allows us to rewrite our expression for
\mathcal{J}_{B}
as:

\mathcal{J}^{B}(\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})=\int dt\ e^{i\omega t}\int_{\vec{r}_{0}}\Xi^{B}_{n}(r_{0},i\partial_{t},\ell)\mathbb{V}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}_{I}(\hat{r}_{0})J^{I}(\vec{r}_{0},t)

(473)

Electric Multipole Radiation

Charge density and poloidal currents source the ‘electric’ multipole radiation. Unlike toroidal currents, which are conserved identically and hence carry no constraint coming from conservation equations, poloidal currents are interlinked with the temporal change of charge density. One can rewrite the current density in a form where the conservation equation is manifest:

\begin{split}\bar{J}^{u}(r,u,\hat{r})&=\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}\left[\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)}{r^{2}}J_{2}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})-\frac{1}{r^{d-1}}\partial_{r}\left\{r^{d-1}J_{1}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\right\}\right]\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,\\
\bar{J}^{r}(r,u,\hat{r})&=-\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}i\omega J_{1}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,\\
\bar{J}^{I}(r,u,\hat{r})&=-\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}\frac{i\omega}{r^{2}}J_{2}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\mathscr{D}^{I}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ .\end{split}

(474)

The electric parity multipole radiation has a gauge redundancy in its description tied to the above conservation equation. Due to such a gauge redundancy, one cannot impose the same gauge conditions as we did for free electromagnetic fields in the presence of extended sources. In particular, the gauge field parametrisation, in terms of the Debye scalar, requires modification with additional source-local terms to satisfy the sourced Maxwell equations. The gauge field parametrisation then becomes:

\begin{split}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{u}(r,u,\hat{r})&=\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}e^{-i\omega u}\left[r^{3-d}D_{+}\Phi_{E}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})-(1-r^{2})J_{2}\right]\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,\\
\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{r}(r,u,\hat{r})&=\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}e^{-i\omega u}\left[r^{3-d}\partial_{r}\Phi_{E}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})-J_{2}\right]\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,\\
\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{I}(r,u,\hat{r})&=\sum_{\alpha,\ell,\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}e^{-i\omega u}\Phi_{B}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\ \mathbb{V}_{I}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ .\end{split}

(475)

One can check the consistency of this gauge choice by plugging it into the Maxwell equations, which are satisfied contingent on the fact that the Debye scalar is a solution to second-order inhomogeneous differential equations:

\begin{split}\frac{1}{r^{3-d}}D_{+}\left[r^{3-d}D_{+}\Phi_{E}\right]+\omega^{2}\Phi_{E}-&\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)(1-r^{2})}{r^{2}}\Phi_{E}\\
-r^{d-3}&\left[D_{+}\left[(1-r^{2})J_{2}\right]-(1-r^{2})J_{1}\right]=0\ .\end{split}

(476)

The particular combination of the source parameters
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
that appears on the RHS of the EOM of
\Phi_{E}
can be considered the ‘radiative’ source combination. This combination is solely responsible for the radiative energy loss.

Given the above parametrisation of the gauge field, the electromagnetic field strengths in the presence of sources can be computed to give:

\begin{split}\mathcal{E}_{r}&=\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)}{r^{d-1}}\Phi_{E}-J_{1}\ ,\\
\mathcal{E}_{s}&=\frac{1}{r^{d-3}}D_{+}\Phi_{E}-(1-r^{2})J_{2}\ ,\\
\mathcal{H}_{s}&=\frac{i\omega}{r^{d-3}}\Phi_{E}\ .\end{split}

(477)

One can think of the above formulae alternatively as being obtained from the gauge field parametrisation in terms of the electromagnetic fields:

\begin{split}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{u}(r,u,\hat{r})&=\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}e^{-i\omega u}\mathcal{E}_{s}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,\\
\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{r}(r,u,\hat{r})&=\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}\frac{e^{-i\omega u}}{1-r^{2}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{s}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})-\mathcal{H}_{s}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\right]\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r})\ ,\\
\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}[1.1pt]{${\mathcal{V}}$}}_{I}(r,u,\hat{r})&=0.\end{split}

(478)

Given the inhomogeneous equations of motion for
\Phi_{E}
, one can solve for the electromagnetic fields in de Sitter by finding the corresponding Green’s functions. The natural boundary condition to impose in de Sitter is the outgoing boundary condition at the horizon. The outgoing Green’s functions can be written as:

\begin{split}\mathbb{G}_{E}(r,r_{0};\omega,\ell)&=\frac{1}{W(r_{0},\omega,\ell)}\left(\frac{1-r_{<}}{1+r_{<}}\right)^{\frac{i\omega}{2}}\Xi^{E}_{n}(r_{<},\omega,\ell)G^{\text{Out}}_{E}(r_{>},\omega,\ell)\ .\end{split}

(479)

where the
G^{\text{Out}}_{E}
is the boundary-to-bulk outgoing propagator defined in the previous section and
\Xi^{E}_{n}
is the
normalisable
mode:

\begin{split}\Xi^{E}_{n}(r,\omega,\ell)&\equiv\frac{1}{2\ell+d-2}r^{\ell+d-2}(1-r^{2})^{-\frac{i\omega}{2}}\\
&\qquad\times{}_{2}F_{1}\left[\frac{\ell+2-i\omega}{2},\frac{\ell+d-2-i\omega}{2};\ell+\frac{d}{2};r^{2}\right]\ .\end{split}

(480)

For odd values of
d
, the above expressions are well-defined, but for even values of
d
, one should evaluate the expressions as a limiting case.
With these Green’s functions, we can write the Debye scalars as:

\begin{split}\Phi_{E}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})&=\int dr_{0}\mathbb{G}_{E}(r,r_{0};\omega,\ell)\left[J_{1}(r_{0},\omega,\ell,\vec{m})-\frac{1}{1-r^{2}}D_{+}^{0}\left\{(1-r^{2})J_{2}(r_{0},\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\right\}\right]\ .\end{split}

(481)

The above solutions in terms of the source parameters
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
can be rewritten in terms of the source currents to give:

\begin{split}\Phi_{E}=&-\frac{1}{i\omega}\int dr_{0}\int d\Omega_{d-1}\Bigg[\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}^{*}J^{r}\mathbb{G}_{E}(r,r_{0};\omega,\ell)-\frac{1}{\ell(\ell+d-2)}r_{0}^{2}J^{I}\mathscr{D}_{I}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}^{*}D_{-}^{0}\mathbb{G}_{E}(r,r_{0};\omega,\ell)\Bigg]\\
\mathcal{H}_{s}=&-\frac{1}{r^{d-3}}\int dr_{0}\int d\Omega_{d-1}\Bigg[\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}^{*}J^{r}\mathbb{G}_{E}(r,r_{0};\omega,\ell)-\frac{1}{\ell(\ell+d-2)}r_{0}^{2}J^{I}\mathscr{D}_{I}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}^{*}D_{-}^{0}\mathbb{G}_{E}(r,r_{0};\omega,\ell)\Bigg]\\
\mathcal{E}_{r}=&\frac{1}{r^{d-1}}\int dr_{0}\int d\Omega_{d-1}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}^{*}r_{0}^{2}\Bigg[J^{t}D_{-}^{0}\mathbb{G}_{E}(r,r_{0};\omega,\ell)+i\omega J^{r}\mathbb{G}_{E}(r,r_{0};\omega,\ell)\Bigg]\ .\end{split}

(482)

The expression for
\mathcal{E}_{s}
can be obtained from the above by integrating the first Bianchi as we did in flat space. The first Bianchi in EF coordinates in dS is:

D_{+}\mathcal{E}_{s}=(1-r^{2})\mathcal{E}_{r}+i\omega\mathcal{H}_{s}\ .

(483)

To integrate this equation, we will rewrite it as:

\begin{split}\partial_{r}\left(e^{i\omega r_{\ast}}\mathcal{E}_{s}\right)&=e^{i\omega r_{\ast}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{r}+\frac{i\omega}{(1-r^{2})}\mathcal{H}_{s}\right]\ \\
&=e^{i\omega r_{\ast}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dr_{0}\int_{\hat{r}_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathscr{Y}^{\ast}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r}_{0})\ \mathbb{G}_{E}(r,r_{0};\omega,\ell)\Bigl\{i\omega\frac{r_{0}^{2}-\frac{r^{2}}{1-r^{2}}}{r^{d-1}}J^{r}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)\\
&\qquad-\frac{1}{1-r_{0}^{2}}D_{+}^{0}\left[\frac{(1-r_{0}^{2})r_{0}^{2}}{r^{d-1}}J^{t}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)+i\omega\frac{(1-r_{0}^{2})r_{0}^{2}}{r^{d-3}}\frac{\mathscr{D}_{I}J^{I}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)}{\ell(\ell+d-2)}\right]\Bigr\}\ ,\end{split}

(484)

where
r_{\ast}
is the tortoise coordinate. We can now integrate this equation to write an expression for
\mathcal{E}_{s}
as follows:

\begin{split}\mathcal{E}_{s}&=e^{-i\omega r_{\ast}}\int dr_{1}e^{i\omega r_{1\ast}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{r}(r_{1})+\frac{i\omega}{(1-r_{1}^{2})}\mathcal{H}_{s}(r_{1})\right]\ \\
&=e^{-i\omega r_{\ast}}\int dr_{1}e^{i\omega r_{1\ast}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dr_{0}\int_{\hat{r}_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathscr{Y}^{\ast}_{\ell\vec{m}}(\hat{r}_{0})\ \mathbb{G}_{E}(r_{1},r_{0};\omega,\ell)\Bigl\{i\omega\frac{r_{0}^{2}-\frac{r_{1}^{2}}{1-r_{1}^{2}}}{r_{1}^{d-1}}J^{r}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)\\
&\qquad-\frac{1}{1-r_{0}^{2}}D_{+}^{0}\left[\frac{(1-r_{0}^{2})r_{0}^{2}}{r_{1}^{d-1}}J^{t}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)+i\omega\frac{(1-r_{0}^{2})r_{0}^{2}}{r_{1}^{d-3}}\frac{\mathscr{D}_{I}J^{I}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)}{\ell(\ell+d-2)}\right]\Bigr\}\ .\end{split}

(485)

Action reduction

We must evaluate the on-shell action on the dS-SK geometry to obtain the effective action describing the extended observer. Although the explicit derivation specific to the dS-SK geometry is treated in the next section, we want to simplify the sourced Maxwell action in this section after imposing the equations of motion to bring it to a simpler form. We begin with the Maxwell action:

S_{EM}=-\int d^{d+1}x\ \left[\frac{1}{4}\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu\nu}\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{\mu\nu}-\bar{\mathcal{V}}_{\mu}J^{\mu}\right]\ .

(486)

We perform, as before, an expansion in the spherical harmonics and a Fourier transform on the outgoing time to obtain, in terms of the field strengths:

\begin{split}S_{EM}=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int_{\omega}\int dr\frac{r^{d-1}}{1-r^{2}}\Bigg[&(1-r^{2})|\mathcal{E}_{r}|^{2}-\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)}{r^{2}}\left\{|\mathcal{H}_{s}|^{2}-|\mathcal{E}_{s}|^{2}\right\}+2i\omega\mathcal{H}_{s}J_{1}^{*}\\
&\qquad+\mathcal{E}_{s}\left\{\frac{1}{r^{d-1}}D_{-}(r^{d-1}J_{1}^{*})-\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)}{r^{2}}(1-r^{2})J_{2}^{*}\right\}\Bigg]\ .\end{split}

(487)

Using integration by parts and the
\Phi_{E}
EOM:

\begin{split}S_{\text{on-shell}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\Bigg[\partial_{r}\left\{-r^{2}(D_{+}\Phi_{E})^{*}J_{1}+r^{2}(1-r^{2})\ell(\ell+d-2)\Phi_{E}^{*}J_{2}+r^{d+1}J_{1}J_{2}^{*}\right\}\\
+\ell(\ell+d-2)\Phi_{E}^{*}\left\{\frac{1}{1-r^{2}}D_{+}\left[(1-r^{2})J_{2}\right]+J_{1}\right\}\\
-r^{d-1}|J_{1}|^{2}-r^{d+1}\ell(\ell+d-2)(1-r^{2})|J_{2}|^{2}\Bigg]\ .\end{split}

(488)

To obtain the parameters
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
given a current density, we invert the equations in (
474
) to obtain:

\begin{split}J_{1}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})&=\int d\Omega_{d-1}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}^{*}(\hat{r})\frac{J^{r}(r,\omega,\hat{r})}{-i\omega}\ ,\\
J_{2}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})&=-\int d\Omega_{d-1}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}^{*}(\hat{r})\frac{r^{2}}{-i\omega\ell(\ell+d-2)}\mathscr{D}_{I}J^{I}(r,\omega,\hat{r})\\
&=\int d\Omega_{d-1}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}^{*}(\hat{r})\frac{1}{\ell(\ell+d-2)}\frac{1}{1-r^{2}}\left[\frac{1}{-i\omega r^{d-3}}D_{+}\left(r^{d-1}J^{r}\right)-r^{2}J_{u}\right]\ .\end{split}

(489)

Here we have rewritten the
\mathscr{D}_{I}J^{I}
term using the conservation equation:

\mathscr{D}_{I}J^{I}=-\frac{1}{1-r^{2}}\left[i\omega J_{u}+\frac{1}{r^{d-1}}D_{+}\left(r^{d-1}J^{r}\right)\right]\ .

(490)

E.1
Radiative multipole moments on dS-SK

We will use the result of the previous section and identify the average/difference electromagnetic radiative multipole moments on the dS-SK geometry. The equation of motion for
\Phi_{E}
shows that the
J_{2}
acts like a ‘Neumann’ source, i.e., the
\Phi_{E}
is sourced by a derivative operator acting on
J_{2}
. On the other hand,
J_{1}
acts like a ‘Dirichlet’ source. For the ‘Neumann’ case, we identify
\rho
with
\frac{J_{2}}{r^{3-d}}
. Consider the contribution to the ‘
R
’ electric multipole moment from the
J_{2}
source takes the form:

\begin{split}&-\ell\int_{R}dr\ \left(\frac{1-r}{1+r}\right)^{-\frac{i\omega}{2}}(1-r^{2})\partial_{r}\Xi^{E}_{n}(r,\omega,\ell)\ J_{2}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\\
=&\frac{1}{\ell+d-2}\int_{R}dr\ \left(\frac{1-r}{1+r}\right)^{-\frac{i\omega}{2}}\partial_{r}\Xi^{E}_{n}(r,\omega,\ell)\int d\Omega_{d-1}\mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}^{*}(\hat{r})\left[r^{2}J_{u}+\frac{1}{i\omega r^{d-3}}D_{+}\left(r^{d-1}J^{r}\right)\right]\ .\end{split}

(491)

We can use integration by parts on the
J^{r}
term along with the following identity for
\Xi_{n}
:

\begin{split}D_{-}&\left[\left(\frac{1-r}{1+r}\right)^{-\frac{i\omega}{2}}r^{3-d}(1-r^{2})\partial_{r}\Xi^{E}_{n}\right]\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad=-\left\{\omega^{2}-\frac{\ell(\ell+d-2)}{r^{2}}(1-r^{2})\right\}\left(\frac{1-r}{1+r}\right)^{-\frac{i\omega}{2}}r^{3-d}\Xi^{E}_{n}\ .\end{split}

(492)

The full radiative multipole moment then becomes:

\begin{split}\mathcal{J}^{E}_{R}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})&=\frac{1}{2\nu(\ell+d-2)}\int_{R}d^{d}x\ \mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}^{*}(\hat{r})\frac{1}{r^{d-3}}\left(\frac{1-r}{1+r}\right)^{-\frac{i\omega}{2}}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\Bigg[\partial_{r}\Xi^{E}_{n}(r,\omega,\ell)J_{u}(r,\omega,\hat{r})+i\omega\ \Xi^{E}_{n}(r,\omega,\ell)J^{r}(r,\omega,\hat{r})\Bigg]\\
\mathcal{J}^{E}_{L}(r,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})&=\frac{1}{2\nu(\ell+d-2)}\int_{R}d^{d}x\ \mathscr{Y}_{\ell\vec{m}}^{*}(\hat{r})\frac{1}{r^{d-3}}\left(\frac{1-r}{1+r}\right)^{-\frac{i\omega}{2}}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\Bigg[\partial_{r}\Xi^{E}_{n}(r,\omega,\ell)J_{u}(r,\omega,\hat{r})+i\omega\ \Xi^{E}_{n}(r,\omega,\ell)J^{r}(r,\omega,\hat{r})\Bigg]\ .\end{split}

(493)

The computation of the magnetic multipole moments on the dS-SK geometry, on the other hand, is straightforward as there is no ambiguity due to conservation equations. We find:

\begin{split}\mathcal{J}^{B}_{R}(\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})&=\int_{R}d^{d}r\left(\frac{1-r_{0}}{1+r_{0}}\right)^{-\frac{i\omega}{2}}\Xi^{B}_{n}(r_{0},\omega,\ell)\mathbb{V}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}_{I}(\hat{r}_{0})J^{I}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)\ ,\\
\mathcal{J}^{B}_{L}(\omega,\alpha,\ell,\vec{m})&=\int_{L}d^{d}r\left(\frac{1-r_{0}}{1+r_{0}}\right)^{-\frac{i\omega}{2}}\Xi^{B}_{n}(r_{0},\omega,\ell)\mathbb{V}^{\alpha\ell\vec{m}}_{I}(\hat{r}_{0})J^{I}(\vec{r}_{0},\omega)\ .\end{split}

(494)

Corresponding to these multipole moments, we can write down the STF moments, which are better suited for a post-newtonian expansion:

\begin{split}{}^{E}\mathcal{Q}_{A,STF}^{i_{1}\ldots i_{\ell}}(\omega)&\equiv\frac{1}{(\ell+d-2)}(\Pi^{S})^{<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}_{<j_{1}j_{2}\ldots j_{\ell}>}\int d^{d}r\ \hat{r}^{j_{1}}\hat{r}^{j_{2}}\ldots\hat{r}^{j_{\ell}}\ \\
&\times\frac{2\nu}{r^{d-3}}\Bigg[\partial_{r}\Xi^{E}_{n}(r,\omega,\ell)J_{u}(r,\omega,\hat{r})+i\omega\ \Xi^{E}_{n}(r,\omega,\ell)J^{r}(r,\omega,\hat{r})\Bigg]\ ,\\
{}^{E}\mathcal{Q}_{D,STF}^{i_{1}\ldots i_{\ell}}(\omega)&\equiv\frac{1}{(\ell+d-2)}(\Pi^{S})^{<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}_{<j_{1}j_{2}\ldots j_{\ell}>}\int d^{d}r\ \hat{r}^{j_{1}}\hat{r}^{j_{2}}\ldots\hat{r}^{j_{\ell}}\ \\
&\times\frac{2\nu}{r^{d-3}}\left(\frac{1-r}{1+r}\right)^{-\frac{i\omega}{2}}\Bigg[\partial_{r}\Xi^{E}_{n}(r,\omega,\ell)J_{u}(r,\omega,\hat{r})\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+i\omega\Xi^{E}_{n}(r,\omega,\ell)J^{r}(r,\omega,\hat{r})\Bigg]\ .\end{split}

(495)

Similarly, the magnetic multipole moments on dS-SK can be written as:

\begin{split}{}^{B}\mathcal{Q}_{A}^{i<i_{1}\ldots i_{\ell}>}\equiv(\Pi^{V})^{i<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}_{j<j_{1}j_{2}\ldots j_{\ell}>}\int d^{d}x\ x^{j_{1}}x^{j_{2}}\ldots x^{j_{\ell}}\ \frac{2\nu}{r^{\ell+1}}\left(\frac{1-r}{1+r}\right)^{-\frac{i\omega}{2}}\Xi^{B}_{n}(r,\omega,\ell)J^{j}_{A}\ ,\\
{}^{B}\mathcal{Q}_{D}^{i<i_{1}\ldots i_{\ell}>}\equiv(\Pi^{V})^{i<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}_{j<j_{1}j_{2}\ldots j_{\ell}>}\int d^{d}x\ x^{j_{1}}x^{j_{2}}\ldots x^{j_{\ell}}\ \frac{2\nu}{r^{\ell+1}}\left(\frac{1-r}{1+r}\right)^{-\frac{i\omega}{2}}\Xi^{B}_{n}(r,\omega,\ell)J^{j}_{D}\end{split}

(496)

The dissipative part of the on-shell action in terms of these STF multipoles can then be written as:

\begin{split}S_{RR}^{\text{Odd $d$}}&=-\sum_{\ell}\int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\frac{1}{4\nu^{2}\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}\frac{1}{\ell!}\Bigg[K^{\text{Out}}_{E}\frac{\ell+d-2}{\ell}{}^{E}\mathcal{Q}_{D,STF}^{\ast<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}{}^{E}\mathcal{Q}^{A,STF}_{<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+K^{\text{Out}}_{B}\ {}^{B}\mathcal{Q}_{D,STF}^{\ast i<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}{}^{B}\mathcal{Q}^{A,STF}_{i<i_{1}i_{2}\ldots i_{\ell}>}\Bigg]\ .\end{split}

(497)

Near Flat Expansion

In the next section, we will calculate the radiation reaction of a point particle moving along an arbitrary trajectory. This result is obtained in a Hubble expansion about flat spacetime up to order
H^{4}
terms. To facilitate this calculation, we quote the Hubble expansions of some useful quantities in this section
41
41

41

The expressions quoted here are special cases of those derived in appendix D.1 of part I
[
14
]
. There, one can find a detailed derivation of these formulae.
. Since the radiation reaction force is well defined only in
dS_{d+1}
for odd
d
, we will restrict our analysis to that particular case.

The radiation reaction kernels
K^{\text{Out}}_{E/B}
have the following expansions in the small
H
limit:

\begin{split}K^{\text{Out}}_{B}|_{\text{Odd $d$}}&=\frac{2\pi i}{\Gamma(\nu)^{2}}\left(\frac{\omega}{2}\right)^{2\nu}\Bigg[1+\left\{\nu^{2}+\frac{3}{4}d(d-4)+2\right\}\frac{\nu}{3!!}\frac{H^{2}}{\omega^{2}}+c_{B}\frac{\nu(\nu-1)}{5!!}\frac{H^{4}}{\omega^{4}}+O\left(\frac{H^{6}}{\omega^{6}}\right)\Bigg]\ ,\end{split}

(498)

as well as

\begin{split}&K^{\text{Out}}_{E}|_{\text{Odd $d$}}=\frac{2\pi i}{\Gamma(\nu)^{2}}\left(\frac{\omega}{2}\right)^{2\nu}\Bigg[1+\left\{\nu^{2}+\frac{3}{4}d(d-8)+11\right\}\frac{\nu}{3!!}\frac{H^{2}}{\omega^{2}}+c_{E}\frac{\nu(\nu-1)}{5!!}\frac{H^{4}}{\omega^{4}}+O\left(\frac{H^{6}}{\omega^{6}}\right)\Bigg]\ .\end{split}

(499)

Here, we have defined the coefficients

\begin{split}c_{B}&\equiv\frac{5\nu^{4}-4\nu^{3}+\left\{\frac{15d(d-4)+32}{2}\right\}\nu^{2}-\left\{15d(d-4)+44\right\}\nu+\frac{45}{16}d^{2}(d-4)^{2}-24}{2\times 3}\ ,\\
c_{E}&\equiv\frac{5\nu^{4}-4\nu^{3}+\left\{\frac{15d(d-8)+212}{2}\right\}\nu^{2}-\left\{15d(d-8)+224\right\}\nu+\frac{45}{16}d(d-8)\left\{d(d-8)+24\right\}+381}{2\times 3}\ .\end{split}

(500)

These expressions can be obtained using the Stirling approximation. In the flat limit, the combination appearing in the influence phase evaluates to

\frac{K^{\text{Out}}|_{\text{Odd $d$}}}{4\nu^{2}\mathcal{N}_{d,\ell}}=\frac{\omega^{2\ell+d-2}}{(d-2)!!(2\ell+d-2)!!}\ ,

(501)

so that the above expressions can be used to give an explicit expression for the influence phase in a small
H
expansion.

The smearing functions for the multipole moments
\Xi^{E/B}_{n}
can also be expanded about small
H
in the following manner:

\begin{split}\Xi^{E/B}_{n}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathfrak{p}^{E/B}_{k}(\nu,H^{2},\omega^{2})\mathfrak{B}^{E/B}_{k}\ ,\end{split}

(502)

where,

\begin{split}\mathfrak{B}^{E}_{k}&\equiv\frac{r^{\nu+\frac{d}{2}-1+2k}}{2\nu(\nu+1)\ldots(\nu+k)}\ {}_{0}F_{1}\left[1+k+\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r^{2}}{4}\right]=\frac{\Gamma(\nu)\ r^{\frac{d-2}{2}+k}}{2(\omega/2)^{k+\nu}}J_{k+\nu}(\omega r)\ ,\\
\mathfrak{B}^{B}_{k}&\equiv\frac{r^{\nu-\frac{d}{2}+2+2k}}{2\nu(\nu+1)\ldots(\nu+k)}\ {}_{0}F_{1}\left[1+k+\nu,-\frac{\omega^{2}r^{2}}{4}\right]=\frac{\Gamma(\nu)\ r^{2-\frac{d}{2}+k}}{2(\omega/2)^{k+\nu}}J_{k+\nu}(\omega r)\ ,\end{split}

(503)

and

\begin{split}\mathfrak{p}^{E/B}_{k}&\equiv\frac{H^{2k}}{k!}\sum_{m=0}^{k}(-)^{m}\binom{k}{m}\sum_{n=0}^{m}(-)^{n}\binom{m}{n}\sigma^{2k-2m}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{E/B}+m)\Gamma(1+\nu+m)}{\Gamma(\alpha_{E/B}+m-n)\Gamma(1+\nu+m-n)}\ \\
&\quad\qquad\qquad\times\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{E/B}+i\sigma+m-n)\Gamma(\alpha_{E/B}-i\sigma+m-n)}{\Gamma(\alpha_{E/B}+i\sigma)\Gamma(\alpha_{E/B}-i\sigma)}\ .\end{split}

(504)

The electric vs magnetic parity smearing function only differs in the
\alpha
parameter in the above formula:

\begin{split}\alpha_{E}\equiv\frac{1}{2}(3-\frac{d}{2}+\nu)\ ,\quad\alpha_{B}\equiv\frac{1}{2}(2-\frac{d}{2}+\nu)\ ,\quad\sigma=\frac{\omega}{2H}\ .\end{split}

(505)

This expansion was derived for the generic case of the designer scalar in part I, which we have used for the specific cases of
\{\mathcal{N}=3-d,\mu=\frac{d}{2}-2\}
for the electric and
\{\mathcal{N}=d-3,\mu=\frac{d}{2}-1\}
for the magnetic smearing function.

E.2
Non-relativistic expansion

We will now derive the Abraham-Lorentz-Dirac force in arbitrary dimensions and find curvature corrections in dS spacetime. We will also find terms, up to cubic order in amplitude, contributing to the full radiation reaction(RR) force. We will follow the technique used in
[
14
]
with some crucial differences for a charged particle interacting with electromagnetic fields.

Let us start with a point source travelling along a worldline
x(\tau)
. We will evaluate the RR force in a non-relativistic approximation. We will also take the particle to move close to the south pole, i.e.
rH\ll 1
. The wavelength of the radiation is much larger than the ‘amplitude’ of the trajectory about the south pole(
\omega r\ll 1
) but much smaller than the Hubble constant (
\omega\gg H
). In
[
14
]
, we referred to these approximations as the post-newtonian(PN) approximations adapted to dS.

The 4-current density associated with a charged particle in dS is given by,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bar{J}^{\mu}(x^{\prime})=\int\frac{dx^{\mu}}{d\tau}\delta^{d+1}(x(\tau)-x^{\prime})d\tau=\frac{dx^{\mu}}{dt}\delta(\vec{x}-\vec{x}^{\prime})\ \end{split}

(506)

In the doubled dS-SK geometry, this source will also be doubled, i.e. given by two worldlines
x_{L}(\tau)
and
x_{R}(\tau)
. Correspondingly, they will source the electromagnetic fields by current densities
\bar{J}_{L}
and
\bar{J}_{R}
. The particle degrees of freedom, on which the effective action of radiation reaction is defined, are the positions of the two particles on either side of the geometry, as well as their time derivatives i.e.
\{x_{L},x_{R},\dot{x}_{L},\dot{x}_{R},\ddot{x}_{L},\ddot{x}_{R},\dots\}
. In the RR Lagrangian, we will retain only up to quartic terms in the
x
’s. The RR force is determined by the terms linear in the difference of their positions as well as time derivatives, i.e.
\{x_{D},\dot{x}_{D},\ddot{x}_{D},\dots\}
. The terms cubic in
x_{D}
give rise to non-thermal fluctuations, which will be discussed later. The fact that there are only linear and cubic
x_{D}
terms in the Lagrangian follows from the fact that the action is odd under
R-L
exchange.

Such an amplitude expansion of the lagrangian allows us to evaluate the forces and fluctuations in a straightforward way by Taylor expanding the lagrangian about the point where
x_{D}
and its time derivatives are zero. As an illustration, consider a given function
\mathfrak{f}
of position. Its corresponding average and difference are obtained as:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left[\mathfrak{f}\left(x_{A}+\frac{x_{D}}{2}\right)+\mathfrak{f}\left(x_{A}-\frac{x_{D}}{2}\right)\right]=\mathfrak{f}(x_{A})+\frac{x_{D}^{2}}{4}\frac{\partial^{2}\mathfrak{f}}{\partial x_{A}^{2}}+O(x_{D}^{4})\ ,

(507)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{f}\left(x_{A}+\frac{x_{D}}{2}\right)-\mathfrak{f}\left(x_{A}-\frac{x_{D}}{2}\right)=x_{D}\frac{\partial\,\mathfrak{f}}{\partial x_{A}}+\frac{x_{D}^{3}}{24}\frac{\partial^{3}\,\mathfrak{f}}{\partial x_{A}^{3}}+O(x_{D}^{4})\ .

(508)

In general, we will need to expand functions which are not just functions of positions but also depend on the time derivatives of the positions, in which case one uses a multi-variable Madhava-Taylor expansion.

The electric sector on-shell action gives the following RR lagrangian:

\begin{split}&|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|(d-2)!!\times(-1)^{\frac{d+1}{2}}L_{E}\\
=&(d-1)[x_{i}]_{D}\mathbb{D}_{1}[x^{i}]_{A}-\frac{d}{4}\left[x_{i}x_{j}-\frac{x^{2}}{d}\delta_{ij}\right]_{D}\mathbb{D}_{2}\left[x^{i}x^{j}-\frac{x^{2}}{d}\delta_{ij}\right]_{A}\\
&+\left\{\frac{1}{2}(x_{i})_{D}\mathbb{D}_{1}^{X}[x^{i}x^{2}]_{A}+\frac{1}{2}(x^{2}x_{i})_{D}\mathbb{D}_{1}^{X}[x^{i}]_{A}\right\}-\left\{(x_{i})_{D}\mathbb{D}_{1}^{V}\partial_{t}[(\vec{x}\cdot\vec{v})x^{i}]_{A}+((\vec{x}\cdot\vec{v})x^{i})_{D}\mathbb{D}_{1}^{V}\partial_{t}[x^{i}]_{A}\ \right\}\ .\end{split}

(509)

Only the dipole and quadrupole terms contribute to this order. The differential operators used in the above expressions
\{\mathbb{D}_{1},\mathbb{D}_{2},\mathbb{D}_{1}^{X},\mathbb{D}_{1}^{V}\}
are given explicitly in table
12
. The number on the differential operator indicates which multipole contributes to that particular term whereas the subscripts signify the structure on which this operator acts. Similar to the scalar case, the magnetic sector action gives the following RR Lagrangian:

\begin{split}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|(d-2)!!\times(-1)^{\frac{d+1}{2}}L_{B}=\frac{1}{4}(x_{i}v_{j}-x_{j}v_{i})_{D}\mathbb{D}_{1}^{\mathbb{V}}[x^{i}v^{j}-x^{j}v^{i}]_{A}\ .\end{split}

(510)

In this case, only the dipole contributes to the quartic lagrangian, and we only have one differential operator. The full lagrangian is just a sum of these two contributions.

Given the lagrangian, we can use integration by parts to rewrite it in a way that one can read off the RR force:

L=\frac{(-1)^{\frac{d-1}{2}}}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|(d-2)!!}\left[f_{i}(x_{A})x^{i}_{D}+\frac{1}{4}N_{i}(x_{D})x^{i}_{A}\right]\ .

(511)

Here,
f^{i}
are the Euler-Lagrange derivatives of the terms linear in
x_{D}
with respect to
x^{i}_{D}
. Similarly,
N^{i}
are the Euler-Lagrange derivatives of the terms linear in
x_{A}
with respect to
x^{i}_{A}
. The
f^{i}
’s can be written as:

\begin{split}f^{i}=&-(d-1)\mathbb{D}_{1}[x^{i}]+\frac{d}{2}x_{j}\mathbb{D}_{2}[x^{i}x^{j}]-\frac{x^{i}}{2}\mathbb{D}_{2}[x^{2}]\\
&-\left\{\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{D}_{1}^{X}[x^{i}x^{2}]+\frac{1}{2}x^{2}\mathbb{D}_{1}^{X}[x^{i}]+x^{i}x^{j}\mathbb{D}_{1}^{X}[x_{j}]\right\}\\
&+\left\{\mathbb{D}_{1}^{V}\partial_{t}\left(x^{i}(x_{j}v^{j})\right)+(x_{j}v^{j})\mathbb{D}_{1}^{V}[v^{i}]+(x^{j}v^{i})\mathbb{D}_{1}^{V}[v_{j}]-\partial_{t}\left(x^{i}x^{j}\mathbb{D}_{1}^{V}[v_{j}]\ \right)\right\}\\
&+v_{j}\mathbb{D}^{\mathbb{V}}_{1}[x^{j}v^{i}-x^{i}v^{j}]+\frac{1}{2}x_{j}\mathbb{D}^{\mathbb{V}}_{1}[x^{j}a^{i}-x^{i}a^{j}]\ .\end{split}

(512)

Symbol

f^{\mu}_{d}

\mathbb{D}_{1}

\frac{\partial_{t}^{d}}{d!!}-\frac{H^{2}}{3!}(d^{2}-6d+11)\frac{\partial_{t}^{d-2}}{(d-2)!!}+\frac{H^{4}}{5!}\frac{(d-1)(d-3)}{3}(5d^{2}-48d+127)\frac{\partial_{t}^{d-4}}{(d-4)!!}

\mathbb{D}_{2}

\frac{\partial_{t}^{d+2}}{(d+2)!!}-\frac{H^{2}}{3!}(d^{2}-5d+12)\frac{\partial_{t}^{d}}{d!!}+\frac{H^{4}}{5!}\frac{(d-1)(d-2)}{3}(5d^{2}-43d+132)\frac{\partial_{t}^{d-2}}{(d-2)!!}

\mathbb{D}_{1}^{X}

(d+1)\frac{\partial_{t}^{d+2}}{(d+2)!!}-\frac{H^{2}}{3!}(d-3)(d^{2}-4d+1)\frac{\partial_{t}^{d}}{d!!}+\frac{H^{4}}{5!}\frac{(d-1)}{3}(5d^{4}-78d^{3}+420d^{2}-946d+711)\frac{\partial_{t}^{d-2}}{(d-2)!!}

\mathbb{D}_{1}^{V}

\frac{\partial_{t}^{d}}{d!!}-\frac{H^{2}}{3!}(d^{2}-6d+11)\frac{\partial_{t}^{d-2}}{(d-2)!!}+\frac{H^{4}}{5!}\frac{(d-1)(d-3)}{3}(5d^{2}-48d+127)\frac{\partial_{t}^{d-4}}{(d-4)!!}

\mathbb{D}^{\mathbb{V}}_{1}

\frac{\partial_{t}^{d}}{d!!}-\frac{H^{2}}{3!}(d-1)(d-2)\frac{\partial_{t}^{d-2}}{(d-2)!!}+\frac{H^{4}}{5!}\frac{(d-1)(d-3)(d-4)(5d+2)}{3}\frac{\partial_{t}^{d-4}}{(d-4)!!}

Table 12:
The differential operators that appear in de Sitter electromagnetic radiation reaction (for
d
odd).

E.3
dS covariantisation

The
f^{i}
’s can be obtained from the following de Sitter covariant vectors:

\begin{split}f^{\mu}_{3}&\equiv\frac{P^{\mu\nu}}{3!!}\left\{-2a_{\nu}^{(1)}\right\}\ ,\\
f^{\mu}_{5}&\equiv\frac{P^{\mu\nu}}{5!!}\left\{-4a_{\nu}^{(3)}+10\ (a\cdot a)\ a_{\nu}^{(1)}+30\ (a\cdot a^{(1)})\ a_{\nu}\right\}-H^{2}\frac{P^{\mu\nu}}{5!!}\left\{16a_{\nu}^{(1)}\right\}\ ,\\
f^{\mu}_{7}&\equiv\frac{P^{\mu\nu}}{7!!}\left\{-6a_{\nu}^{(5)}+42\ (a\cdot a)\ a_{\nu}^{(3)}+210\ (a\cdot a^{(1)})\ a_{\nu}^{(2)}+224\ (a\cdot a^{(2)})\ a_{\nu}^{(1)}+\frac{574}{3}\ (a^{(1)}\cdot a^{(1)})\ a_{\nu}^{(1)}\right.\\
&\left.\qquad\qquad+126\ (a\cdot a^{(3)})\ a_{\nu}+280\ (a^{(1)}\cdot a^{(2)})\ a_{\nu}+O(a^{5})\right\}\\
&\quad+H^{2}\frac{P^{\mu\nu}}{7!!}\left\{120a_{\nu}^{(3)}-342\ (a\cdot a)\ a_{\nu}^{(1)}-978\ (a\cdot a^{(1)})\ a_{\nu}\right\}-H^{4}\frac{P^{\mu\nu}}{7!!}\left\{384a^{(1)}_{\nu}\right\}\ ,\\
\end{split}

(513)

\begin{split}f^{\mu}_{9}&\equiv\frac{P^{\mu\nu}}{9!!}\left\{-8a_{\nu}^{(7)}+132\ (a\cdot a)\ a_{\nu}^{(5)}+924\ (a\cdot a^{(1)})\ a_{\nu}^{(4)}+1512\ (a\cdot a^{(2)})\ a_{\nu}^{(3)}\right.\\
&\left.\qquad+1470\ (a\cdot a^{(3)})\ a_{\nu}^{(2)}+888\ (a\cdot a^{(4)})\ a_{\nu}^{(1)}+324\ (a\cdot a^{(5)})\ a_{\nu}\right.\\
&\left.\qquad+1344\ (a^{(1)}\cdot a^{(1)})\ a_{\nu}^{(3)}+3570\ (a^{(1)}\cdot a^{(2)})\ a_{\nu}^{(2)}+2640\ (a^{(1)}\cdot a^{(3)})\ a_{\nu}^{(1)}\right.\\
&\left.\qquad+1092\ (a^{(1)}\cdot a^{(4)})\ a_{\nu}+1830\ (a^{(2)}\cdot a^{(2)})\ a_{\nu}^{(1)}+1890\ (a^{(2)}\cdot a^{(3)})\ a_{\nu}+O(a^{5})\right\}\\
&-H^{2}\frac{P^{\mu\nu}}{9!!}\left\{448a_{\nu}^{(5)}+3488\ (a\cdot a)\ a_{\nu}^{(3)}+17240\ (a\cdot a^{(1)})\ a_{\nu}^{(2)}+18132\ (a\cdot a^{(2)})\ a_{\nu}^{(1)}\right.\\
&\left.\qquad+9944\ (a\cdot a^{(3)})\ a_{\nu}+15424\ (a^{(1)}\cdot a^{(1)})\ a_{\nu}^{(1)}+21980\ (a^{(1)}\cdot a^{(2)})\ a_{\nu}+O(a^{5})\right\}\\
&+H^{4}\frac{P^{\mu\nu}}{9!!}\left\{-784a_{\nu}^{(3)}+19286\ (a\cdot a)\ a_{\nu}^{(1)}+53770\ (a\cdot a^{(1)})\ a_{\nu}\right\}+O(H^{6})\ ,\\
\end{split}

(514)

\begin{split}f^{\mu}_{11}&\equiv\frac{P^{\mu\nu}}{11!!}\left\{-10a_{\nu}^{(9)}+330\ (a\cdot a)\ a_{\nu}^{(7)}+2970\ (a\cdot a^{(1)})\ a_{\nu}^{(6)}+6600\ (a\cdot a^{(2)})\ a_{\nu}^{(5)}\right.\\
&\left.\qquad\qquad\quad+9240\ (a\cdot a^{(3)})\ a_{\nu}^{(4)}+8712\ (a\cdot a^{(4)})\ a_{\nu}^{(3)}\right.\\
&\left.\qquad\qquad\quad+5610\ (a\cdot a^{(5)})\ a_{\nu}^{(2)}+2420\ (a\cdot a^{(6)})\ a_{\nu}^{(1)}+660\ (a\cdot a^{(7)})\ a_{\nu}\right.\\
&\left.\qquad\qquad+5940\ (a^{(1)}\cdot a^{(1)})\ a_{\nu}^{(5)}+23100\ (a^{(1)}\cdot a^{(2)})\ a_{\nu}^{(4)}+27324\ (a^{(1)}\cdot a^{(3)})\ a_{\nu}^{(3)}\right.\\
&\left.\qquad\qquad\quad+20790\ (a^{(1)}\cdot a^{(4)})\ a_{\nu}^{(2)}+10120\ (a^{(1)}\cdot a^{(5)})\ a_{\nu}^{(1)}+2970\ (a^{(1)}\cdot a^{(6)})\ a_{\nu}\right.\\
&\left.\qquad\qquad+19140\ (a^{(2)}\cdot a^{(2)})\ a_{\nu}^{(3)}+36960\ (a^{(2)}\cdot a^{(3)})\ a_{\nu}^{(2)}+21560\ (a^{(2)}\cdot a^{(4)})\ a_{\nu}^{(1)}\right.\\
&\left.\qquad\qquad+7260\ (a^{(2)}\cdot a^{(5)})\ a_{\nu}+13706\ (a^{(3)}\cdot a^{(3)})\ a_{\nu}^{(1)}+11088\ (a^{(3)}\cdot a^{(4)})\ a_{\nu}\right\}\\
&+H^{2}\frac{P^{\mu\nu}}{11!}\left\{-1200a_{\nu}^{(7)}+21460\ (a\cdot a)\ a_{\nu}^{(5)}+149660\ (a\cdot a^{(1)})\ a_{\nu}^{(4)}+244048\ (a\cdot a^{(2)})\ a_{\nu}^{(3)}\right.\\
&\left.\qquad\qquad\quad+236030\ (a\cdot a^{(3)})\ a_{\nu}^{(2)}+141308\ (a\cdot a^{(4)})\ a_{\nu}^{(1)}+50660\ (a\cdot a^{(5)})\ a_{\nu}\right.\\
&\left.\qquad\qquad+216186\ (a^{(1)}\cdot a^{(1)})\ a_{\nu}^{(3)}+570000\ (a^{(1)}\cdot a^{(2)})\ a_{\nu}^{(2)}+417254\ (a^{(1)}\cdot a^{(3)})\ a_{\nu}^{(1)}\right.\\
&\left.\qquad\qquad+169692\ (a^{(1)}\cdot a^{(4)})\ a_{\nu}+288650\ (a^{(2)}\cdot a^{(2)})\ a_{\nu}^{(1)}+292932\ (a^{(2)}\cdot a^{(3)})\ a_{\nu}+O(a^{5})\right\}\ \\
&-H^{4}\frac{P^{\mu\nu}}{11!!}\left\{43680a_{\nu}^{(5)}+363860\ (a\cdot a)\ a_{\nu}^{(3)}+1786540\ (a\cdot a^{(1)})\ a_{\nu}^{(2)}+1864072\ (a\cdot a^{(2)})\ a_{\nu}^{(1)}\right.\\
&\left.\qquad\qquad+1006750\ (a\cdot a^{(3)})\ a_{\nu}+1581920\ (a^{(1)}\cdot a^{(1)})\ a_{\nu}^{(1)}+2218698\ (a^{(1)}\cdot a^{(2)})\ a_{\nu}+O(a^{5})\right\}\\
&+O(H^{6})\ .\end{split}

(515)

Here
v^{\mu}=\frac{dx^{\mu}}{d\tau}
is the proper velocity of the particle computed using dS metric,
a^{\mu}\equiv\frac{D^{2}x^{\mu}}{D\tau^{2}}
is its proper acceleration and
P^{\mu\nu}\equiv g^{\mu\nu}+v^{\mu}v^{\nu}
is the transverse projector to the worldline. We use
a_{\mu}^{(k)}\equiv\frac{D^{k}a^{\mu}}{D\tau^{k}}
to denote the proper-time derivatives of the acceleration. All the spacetime dot products are computed using the dS metric.

The problem of flat space electromagnetic radiation reaction in 3+1 dimensions has been discussed in textbooks of classical electrodynamics (see for reference
[
45
,
47
]
). The corresponding RR force in higher dimensions has been treated in many works
[
30
,
26
,
28
,
29
,
24
,
25
]
. In
[
25
]
, the authors compute the electromagnetic radiation reaction action in arbitrary dimensions. The flat space limit of our action matches the one they obtained. We match their post-newtonian expansion of the RR force with ours for the cases
d=3
and
d=5
given in their paper.
We also match the flat limit of our covariant expressions to those given in previous works. Our expressions match the flat space results from
[
25
]
and
[
26
]
for
d=3
and
d=5
. Galakhov
[
27
]
gives covariant expressions up to
d=7
, which matches ours up to signs of certain terms. We disagree with the curved space results of
[
26
]
at the
H^{2}
order and higher, even though we match the flat space result. The source of this disagreement is unclear due to the very different nature of our derivations.

Appendix F
Neumann designer scalar on the dS-SK Geometry

In paper I, we analysed extended sources in de Sitter coupled to designer scalars through Dirichlet boundary conditions. In this section, we will solve for the designer scalar fields in the presence of extended sources but obeying Neumann boundary conditions. This will be relevant to our study of the electric Debye scalar.

For a generic bulk source
\rho_{\mathcal{N}}
, the Neumann boundary condition arises from a coupling of
\rho_{\mathcal{N}}
to the conjugate field
\pi_{\mathcal{N}}=-r^{\mathcal{N}}D_{+}\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}
, where we have defined
D_{+}\equiv(1-r^{2})\partial_{r}+i\omega
and
D_{-}\equiv(1-r^{2})\partial_{r}-i\omega
.

\begin{split}S&=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\oint\frac{r^{\mathcal{N}}dr}{1-r^{2}}\Bigl[(D_{+}\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}})^{*}D_{+}\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}-\omega^{2}\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}^{*}\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}\Bigr.\\
&\Bigl.\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\frac{1-r^{2}}{4r^{2}}\Bigl\{(\mathcal{N}-1)^{2}-4\nu^{2}+[4\mu^{2}-(\mathcal{N}+1)^{2}]r^{2}\Bigr\}\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}^{*}\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}\Bigr]\\
&\qquad+\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\oint dr\ \pi_{\mathcal{N}}^{*}\varrho_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}+S_{ct}[\varrho_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}]\ .\end{split}

(516)

Here
\oint
denotes the integral over the complex radial contour of dS-SK geometry.
The inhomogeneous equation of motion satisfied by such a Neumann scalar is given by:

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{r^{\mathcal{N}}}D_{+}[r^{\mathcal{N}}D_{+}\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}]+\omega^{2}\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}+\frac{1-r^{2}}{4r^{2}}\Bigl\{(\mathcal{N}-1)^{2}-4\nu^{2}+[4\mu^{2}-(\mathcal{N}+1)^{2}]r^{2}\Bigr\}\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{r^{\mathcal{N}}}D_{+}\left[r^{\mathcal{N}}(1-r^{2})\varrho_{\mathcal{N}}(\zeta,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\right]=0\ .\end{split}

(517)

Notice that the equation is the same as the inhomogeneous equation of motion for the Dirichlet scalar with the inhomogeneous term replaced with
\frac{1}{r^{\mathcal{N}}}D_{+}\left[r^{\mathcal{N}}(1-r^{2})\varrho_{\mathcal{N}}(\zeta,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\right]
. In such a case, we can use the same bulk-to-bulk Green function derived in
[
14
]
42
42

42

See appendix C.3 of the reference for a detailed derivation. The corresponding bulk-to-bulk two-point functions in the case of black holes can be found in
[
55
]
.
, convolved with the appropriate Neumann source, to write the solution. In particular:

\begin{split}\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}(\zeta,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})&=\oint dr_{0}\ \frac{\mathbb{G}(\zeta|\zeta_{0},\omega,\ell)}{1-r_{0}^{2}}D_{+}^{0}\left[r_{0}^{\mathcal{N}}(1-r_{0}^{2})\varrho_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}(\zeta_{0},\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\right]\ .\end{split}

(518)

The bulk-to-bulk Green’s function
\mathbb{G}
is given by:

\begin{split}\mathbb{G}(\zeta|\zeta_{0},\omega,\ell)&=\frac{1}{W_{LR}(\zeta_{0},\omega,\ell)}g_{R}(\zeta_{\succ},\omega,\ell)g_{L}(\zeta_{\prec},\omega,\ell)\\
&\equiv\frac{1}{W_{LR}(\zeta_{0},\omega,\ell)}\begin{cases}g_{R}(\zeta,\omega,\ell)g_{L}(\zeta_{0},\omega,\ell)&\quad\text{if}\;\zeta\succ\zeta_{0}\\
g_{L}(\zeta,\omega,\ell)g_{R}(\zeta_{0},\omega,\ell)&\quad\text{if}\;\zeta\prec\zeta_{0}\end{cases}\ .\end{split}

(519)

where
\succ
and
\prec
respectively mean ‘
succeeds
’ and ‘
precedes
’ on the dS-SK contour. In (
518
), one can use integration by parts to rewrite it in the more conventional definition of the bulk-to-bulk Green function:

\begin{split}\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}(\zeta,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})&=-\oint dr_{0}\ r_{0}^{\mathcal{N}}D_{-}^{0}\mathbb{G}(\zeta|\zeta_{0},\omega,\ell)\varrho_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}(\zeta_{0},\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\ .\end{split}

(520)

If we now repackage this new Green function into a ‘Neumann’ Green function defined by:

\begin{split}\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}(\zeta,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})&=\oint dr_{0}\ \widetilde{\mathbb{G}}(\zeta|\zeta_{0},\omega,\ell)\varrho_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}(\zeta_{0},\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\ ,\end{split}

(521)

we find the expression for the Neumann Green function in terms of bulk to boundary propagators as:

\begin{split}\widetilde{\mathbb{G}}(\zeta|\zeta_{0},\omega,\ell)&=-r_{0}^{\mathcal{N}}D_{-}^{0}\left[\frac{1}{W_{LR}(\zeta_{0},\omega,\ell)}g_{R}(\zeta_{\succ},\omega,\ell)g_{L}(\zeta_{\prec},\omega,\ell)\right]\\
&=\frac{1}{W_{LR}(\zeta_{0},\omega,\ell)}\begin{cases}g_{R}(\zeta,\omega,\ell)\pi_{L}(\zeta_{0},\omega,\ell)&\quad\text{if}\;\zeta\succ\zeta_{0}\\
g_{L}(\zeta,\omega,\ell)\pi_{R}(\zeta_{0},\omega,\ell)&\quad\text{if}\;\zeta\prec\zeta_{0}\end{cases}\ .\end{split}

(522)

In going from the first line of the equation to the second, we have used the fact that for any function
\mathfrak{f}(r)
:

D_{-}\left[\frac{\mathfrak{f}(r)}{W_{LR}}\right]=\frac{D_{+}\mathfrak{f}(r)}{W_{LR}}.

(523)

This Neumann Green function then solves the following differential equation:

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{r^{\mathcal{N}}}D_{+}[r^{\mathcal{N}}D_{+}\widetilde{\mathbb{G}}]+\omega^{2}\widetilde{\mathbb{G}}\\
&\qquad+\frac{1-r^{2}}{4r^{2}}\Bigl\{(\mathcal{N}-1)^{2}-4\nu^{2}+[4\mu^{2}-(\mathcal{N}+1)^{2}]r^{2}\Bigr\}\widetilde{\mathbb{G}}+\frac{1}{r^{\mathcal{N}}}D_{+}\left[r^{\mathcal{N}}(1-r^{2})\delta_{c}(r-r_{0})\right]=0\ .\end{split}

(524)

Spherical shell influence phase with Neumann boundary conditions

In this section, we will derive the influence phase of an extended source for scalar fields that obey Neumann boundary conditions. We will discretize the extended source into a set of spherical shells, centered at the origin, on both left and right static patches. The discontinuity in the field is given by the surface density
\sigma_{i}^{R}
for the sphere placed at
\zeta=1+\zeta_{i}
on the right patch and by the surface density
\sigma_{i}^{L}
for the sphere placed at
\zeta=\zeta_{i}
on the left patch. One can think of such a source in terms of the bulk source
\rho
defined in the previous section as being given by:

\begin{split}r^{\mathcal{N}}\varrho_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}(\zeta,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})=\sum_{i}\sigma^{R}_{i}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\ \delta_{c}(\zeta|1+\zeta_{i})-\sum_{i}\sigma^{L}_{i}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\ \delta_{c}(\zeta|\zeta_{i})\ .\end{split}

(525)

The crucial difference from the Dirichlet case is that when this source enters the inhomogeneous differential equation for
\varphi
, it is acted upon by a
D_{+}
operator.
The solution is given by:

\begin{split}&\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}=\\
&\sum_{i}\frac{1}{W_{LR}(\zeta_{i},\omega,\ell)}\begin{cases}e^{2\pi\omega}g_{L}(\zeta,\omega,\ell)\ \Bigl[\pi_{L}(1+\zeta_{i},\omega,\ell)\ \sigma^{L}_{i}-\pi_{R}(1+\zeta_{i},\omega,\ell)\ \sigma^{R}_{i}\Bigr]&\quad\text{if}\;\zeta\prec 1+\zeta_{i}\ ,\\
&\\
\quad\pi_{R}(\zeta_{i},\omega,\ell)\ \Bigl[g_{L}(\zeta,\omega,\ell)\ \sigma^{L}_{i}-g_{R}(\zeta,\omega,\ell)\ \sigma^{R}_{i}\Bigr]&\quad\text{if}\;1+\zeta_{i}\prec\zeta\prec\zeta_{i}\ ,\\
&\\
\quad g_{R}(\zeta,\omega,\ell)\ \Bigl[\pi_{L}(\zeta_{i},\omega,\ell)\ \sigma^{L}_{i}-\pi_{R}(\zeta_{i},\omega,\ell)\ \sigma^{R}_{i}\Bigr]&\quad\text{if}\;\zeta\succ\zeta_{i}\ .\end{cases}\end{split}

(526)

We can substitute this solution into the action to obtain the effective action in terms of the surface charge densities of the shells. This yields the following:

\begin{split}S|_{\textbf{On-shell}}&=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\oint r^{\mathcal{N}}dr\ \varrho_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}^{*}D_{+}\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}|_{\textbf{On-shell}}\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j,\ell,\vec{m}}\int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\frac{\pi_{R}(\zeta_{i},\omega,\ell,\vec{m})}{W_{LR}(\zeta_{i},\omega,\ell,\vec{m})}\Bigl\{\sigma^{R\ast}_{j}\ \Bigl[\pi_{R}(1+\zeta_{j},\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\sigma^{R}_{i}-\pi_{L}(1+\zeta_{j},\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\sigma^{L}_{i}\Bigr]\Bigr.\\
&\qquad\Bigl.\qquad-\sigma^{L\ast}_{j}\ \Bigl[\pi_{R}(\zeta_{j},\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\sigma^{R}_{i}-\pi_{L}(\zeta_{j},\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\sigma^{L}_{i}\Bigr]\Bigr\}\ .\end{split}

(527)

We can now use explicit expressions for the bulk to boundary propagators and rewrite the action in a convenient form. We make use of the following equations:

\begin{split}W_{LR}(\zeta_{i},\omega,\ell)&=-(1+n_{\omega})\left(\frac{1-r_{i}}{1+r_{i}}\right)^{i\omega}[K_{\text{Out}}-K_{\text{In}}]\ ,\\
\frac{\pi_{R}(\zeta_{i},\omega,\ell)}{W_{LR}(\zeta_{i},\omega,\ell)}&=\left(\frac{1-r_{i}}{1+r_{i}}\right)^{-\frac{i\omega}{2}}(1-r_{i}^{2})\partial_{r_{i}}\Xi_{n}(r_{i},\omega,\ell)\ ,\\
\pi_{L}(\zeta_{i},\omega,\ell)&=-\left(\frac{1-r_{i}}{1+r_{i}}\right)^{\frac{i\omega}{2}}(1-r_{i}^{2})\partial_{r_{i}}\Biggl\{\Xi_{nn}(r_{i},\omega,\ell)+\left[n_{\omega}K_{\text{Out}}-(1+n_{\omega})K_{\text{In}}\right]\Xi_{n}(r_{i},\omega,\ell)\Biggr\}\ ,\\
\pi_{L}(1+\zeta_{i},\omega,\ell)&=-n_{\omega}\left(\frac{1-r_{i}}{1+r_{i}}\right)^{\frac{i\omega}{2}}\left[K_{\text{Out}}-K_{\text{In}}\right](1-r_{i}^{2})\partial_{r_{i}}\Xi_{n}(r_{i},\omega,\ell)\ ,\\
\pi_{R}(\zeta_{i},\omega,\ell)&=-(1+n_{\omega})\left(\frac{1-r_{i}}{1+r_{i}}\right)^{\frac{i\omega}{2}}\left[K_{\text{Out}}-K_{\text{In}}\right](1-r_{i}^{2})\partial_{r_{i}}\Xi_{n}(r_{i},\omega,\ell)\ ,\\
\pi_{R}(1+\zeta_{i},\omega,\ell)&=\left(\frac{1-r_{i}}{1+r_{i}}\right)^{\frac{i\omega}{2}}(1-r_{i}^{2})\partial_{r_{i}}\Biggl\{\Xi_{nn}(r_{i},\omega,\ell)-\left[(1+n_{\omega})K_{\text{Out}}-n_{\omega}K_{\text{In}}\right]\Xi_{n}(r_{i},\omega,\ell)\Biggr\}\ .\end{split}

(528)

Substituting these expressions in the above action yields the following:

\begin{split}S|_{\textbf{On-shell}}&=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\oint r^{\mathcal{N}}dr\ \varrho_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}^{*}D_{+}\varphi_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}|_{\textbf{On-shell}}\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j,\ell,\vec{m}}\int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\left(\frac{1-r_{i}}{1+r_{i}}\right)^{-\frac{i\omega}{2}}\left(\frac{1-r_{j}}{1+r_{j}}\right)^{\frac{i\omega}{2}}(1-r_{i}^{2})\partial_{r_{i}}\Xi_{n}(r_{i},\omega,\ell)\\
&\Bigl\{\sigma^{R\ast}_{j}\ \Bigl[(1-r_{j}^{2})\partial_{r_{j}}\Biggl\{\Xi_{nn}(r_{j},\omega,\ell)-\left[(1+n_{\omega})K_{\text{Out}}-n_{\omega}K_{\text{In}}\right]\Xi_{n}(r_{j},\omega,\ell)\Biggr\}\sigma^{R}_{i}\\
&+n_{\omega}\left[K_{\text{Out}}-K_{\text{In}}\right](1-r_{j}^{2})\partial_{r_{j}}\Xi_{n}(r_{j},\omega,\ell)\sigma^{L}_{i}\Bigr]\Bigr.\\
&\Bigl.-\sigma^{L\ast}_{j}\ \Bigl[-(1+n_{\omega})\left[K_{\text{Out}}-K_{\text{In}}\right](1-r_{j}^{2})\partial_{r_{j}}\Xi_{n}(r_{j},\omega,\ell)\sigma^{R}_{i}\\
&+(1-r_{j}^{2})\partial_{r_{j}}\Biggl\{\Xi_{nn}(r_{j},\omega,\ell)+\left[n_{\omega}K_{\text{Out}}-(1+n_{\omega})K_{\text{In}}\right]\Xi_{n}(r_{j},\omega,\ell)\Biggr\}\sigma^{L}_{i}\Bigr]\Bigr\}\ .\end{split}

(529)

Simplifying the above expression and rewriting the terms in specific combinations of the sources, we obtain

\begin{split}&S|_{\textbf{On-shell}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{ij\ell}\int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\left(\frac{1-r_{i}}{1+r_{i}}\right)^{-\frac{i\omega}{2}}\left(\frac{1-r_{j}}{1+r_{j}}\right)^{\frac{i\omega}{2}}\\
&\quad\times\Bigl\{(1-r_{i}^{2})\partial_{r_{i}}\Xi_{n}(r_{i},\omega,\ell)(1-r_{j}^{2})\partial_{r_{j}}\Xi_{nn}(r_{j},\omega,\ell)\ [\sigma^{R\ast}_{j}\sigma^{R}_{i}-\sigma^{L\ast}_{j}\sigma^{L}_{i}]\Bigr.\\
&\quad\qquad-(1-r_{i}^{2})\partial_{r_{i}}\Xi_{n}(r_{i},\omega,\ell)(1-r_{j}^{2})\partial_{r_{j}}\Xi_{n}(r_{j},\omega,\ell)\ K_{\text{Out}}(\sigma^{R}_{j}-\sigma^{L}_{j})^{\ast}[(1+n_{\omega})\sigma^{R}_{i}-n_{\omega}\sigma^{L}_{i}]\\
&\quad\qquad\Bigl.-(1-r_{i}^{2})\partial_{r_{i}}\Xi_{n}(r_{i},\omega,\ell)(1-r_{j}^{2})\partial_{r_{j}}\Xi_{n}(r_{j},\omega,\ell)\ K_{\text{In}}(\sigma^{R}_{i}-\sigma^{L}_{i})[(1+n_{-\omega})\sigma^{R\ast}_{j}-n_{-\omega}\sigma^{L\ast}_{j}]\Bigr\}\ .\end{split}

(530)

The last two lines of the above expression are related by relabelling
\omega\to-\omega
. These two terms can be interpreted physically by defining the radiative multipole moments:

\begin{split}\mathcal{J}_{R}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})&\equiv\sum_{i}\left(\frac{1-r_{i}}{1+r_{i}}\right)^{-\frac{i\omega}{2}}(1-r_{i}^{2})\partial_{r_{i}}\Xi_{n}(r_{i},\omega,\ell)\ \sigma_{i}^{R}\\
&\equiv\int_{R}dr\ r^{\mathcal{N}}(1-r^{2})\partial_{r}\Xi_{n}(r,\omega,\ell)\ \left(\frac{1-r}{1+r}\right)^{-\frac{i\omega}{2}}\varrho_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}(\zeta,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\ ,\\
\mathcal{J}_{L}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})&\equiv\sum_{i}\left(\frac{1-r_{i}}{1+r_{i}}\right)^{-\frac{i\omega}{2}}(1-r_{i}^{2})\partial_{r_{i}}\Xi_{n}(r_{i},\omega,\ell)\ \sigma_{i}^{L}\\
&\equiv-\int_{L}dr\ r^{\mathcal{N}}(1-r^{2})\partial_{r}\Xi_{n}(r,\omega,\ell)\ \left(\frac{1-r}{1+r}\right)^{-\frac{i\omega}{2}}\varrho_{{}_{\mathcal{N}}}(\zeta,\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\ .\end{split}

(531)

This now allows us to recast the last two lines of the action into the cosmological influence phase we obtained for the point source:

\begin{split}S_{\text{CIP}}^{\text{Pt}}&\equiv-\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}K_{\text{Out}}(\mathcal{J}_{R}-\mathcal{J}_{L})^{\ast}[(1+n_{\omega})\mathcal{J}_{R}-n_{\omega}\mathcal{J}_{L}]\\
&=-\sum_{\ell\vec{m}}\int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}K_{\text{Out}}\ \mathcal{J}_{D}^{*}\ \Bigl[\mathcal{J}_{A}+\left(n_{\omega}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\mathcal{J}_{D}\Bigr]\ .\end{split}

(532)

where we have defined the average and difference multipole moments as

\begin{split}\mathcal{J}_{A}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})&\equiv\frac{1}{2}[\mathcal{J}_{R}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})+\mathcal{J}_{L}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})]\ ,\\
\mathcal{J}_{D}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})&\equiv\mathcal{J}_{R}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})-\mathcal{J}_{L}(\omega,\ell,\vec{m})\ .\end{split}

(533)
